## 1. Measurement & foundations

### Why we need standard units
*BB 85 · physics · measurement*

**Idea.** You can't meaningfully say how heavy an elephant is without agreeing on what "heavy" means. A number alone means nothing — it needs a unit attached, and that unit must mean the same thing to everyone who uses it.

**Concrete.** An elephant is 200 times the mass of a goat. That statement works because both are compared to the same invisible standard — a unit mass. Without that standard, "200" is just a number with no physical meaning. All measurement works this way: a physical quantity is expressed as a numerical value times a unit. "2 hours" means two times the standard unit called an hour.

**Definition.** A **unit** is a fixed, internationally agreed quantity of a physical dimension used as a reference for measurement. The numerical value of any measurement is the ratio of the measured quantity to this reference.

**In action.** The international body that decides units is the CGPM — the General Conference on Weights and Measures. It meets periodically and any change to standard units is communicated worldwide. This ensures that a metre in Delhi is the same length as a metre in Tokyo. Before such bodies existed, every region had its own units — cubits, feet, stones — which made trade and science across borders nearly impossible.

---

### Fundamental and derived quantities
*BB 86 · physics · measurement*

**Idea.** Not all quantities need their own independently defined unit. If you define length, area comes free. If you define length and time, speed comes free. Most quantities can be built from a small set of foundation pieces.

**Concrete.** Make a square with each side equal to the unit length. That square's area is the unit of area — it wasn't defined separately. If a particle covers one unit of length in one unit of time, we call that a unit of speed. Speed was never independently defined either; it emerged from length and time.

**Definition.** **Fundamental quantities** (also called base quantities) are a set of mutually independent physical quantities from which all others can be derived by multiplication and division. There are exactly seven of them. Their units are **fundamental units**. All other quantities are **derived quantities** and carry **derived units**.

**In action.** The choice of which quantities are fundamental is partly conventional. Length, mass, and time are the three most familiar. But one could alternatively take speed and time as fundamental and derive length from them. Different systems of units exist precisely because different choices of base quantities can be made. The SI system settled on seven: length, mass, time, electric current, temperature, amount of substance, and luminous intensity.

---

### The seven pillars of SI
*BB 87 · physics · measurement*

**Idea.** Every measurement in science rests on just seven foundation blocks. The entire edifice of physics — from the mass of an electron to the distance of a quasar — is expressed in combinations of these seven units.

**Concrete.** The seven SI base units are: the metre (m) for length, the kilogram (kg) for mass, the second (s) for time, the ampere (A) for electric current, the kelvin (K) for temperature, the mole (mol) for amount of substance, and the candela (cd) for luminous intensity. Two supplementary units exist for angles: the radian (rad) for plane angle and the steradian (sr) for solid angle.

**Definition.** Since May 2019, all seven SI base units are defined in terms of fixed numerical values assigned to universal physical constants — not by physical artefacts. The metre is defined by fixing the speed of light at exactly 299,792,458 m/s. The kilogram is defined by fixing Planck's constant at exactly 6.62607015 × 10⁻³⁴ J·s. The second is defined by fixing the caesium hyperfine transition frequency. These definitions are invariable and universally available.

**In action.** Because physical quantities vary across enormous ranges — from the size of a proton (10⁻¹⁵ m) to the distance of a quasar (10²⁶ m) — SI uses standard prefixes: kilo (10³), mega (10⁶), giga (10⁹) on the large side, and milli (10⁻³), micro (10⁻⁶), nano (10⁻⁹) on the small side. A nanometre is a billionth of a metre; a gigawatt is a billion watts. The prefix carries the scale, the base unit carries the kind of quantity.

---

### Dimensions: the fingerprint of a physical quantity
*BB 88 · physics · measurement*

**Idea.** Every physical quantity carries a hidden code written in the alphabet of base quantities. Speed is always length divided by time. Force is always mass times length divided by time squared. This code — the quantity's dimensions — does not depend on units and never changes.

**Concrete.** Force equals mass times acceleration. Acceleration is velocity divided by time. Velocity is length divided by time. Chaining these together: force = mass × (length/time) / time = mass × length × time⁻². So force has dimensions of 1 in mass, 1 in length, and −2 in time — written compactly as MLT⁻². This is called the **dimensional formula** of force.

**Definition.** The **dimension** of a physical quantity in a given base quantity is the exponent to which that base quantity is raised in the expression of the quantity. The **dimensional formula** is the product of the base quantities raised to their respective exponents: [quantity] = Mᵃ Lᵇ Tᶜ Iᵈ Kᵉ molᶠ cdᵍ. A quantity with all exponents zero is **dimensionless**.

**In action.** Dimensions are not units. Speed can be expressed in m/s, km/h, or miles per hour — its numerical value changes but its dimensions (LT⁻¹) remain identical. A dimensionless quantity like an angle or a pure number has no dimensions regardless of the unit system. Knowing a quantity's dimensions lets you check whether equations make physical sense without doing any arithmetic at all.

---

### The homogeneity principle
*BB 89 · physics · measurement*

**Idea.** You cannot add a speed to a force. You cannot subtract an electric current from a temperature. Every term in a physically meaningful equation must be the same kind of thing — the same dimensions. This simple rule catches errors before any calculation is done.

**Concrete.** Take the kinematics equation: x = ut + ½at². The left side x is a length, dimension L. The first term ut is velocity × time = (L/T) × T = L. The second term ½at²: the ½ is dimensionless, acceleration is L/T², multiplied by T² gives L. All three terms have the same dimension, L. The equation passes the test — it may be correct.

**Definition.** The **principle of homogeneity of dimensions** states that in any valid physical equation, every term must have identical dimensions. An equation that fails this test is necessarily wrong. An equation that passes it may be correct, but dimensional homogeneity alone is not a guarantee — numerical factors must still be verified.

**In action.** This principle is a fast filter. If a student writes F = mv (force equals mass times velocity), a quick dimensional check reveals: [F] = MLT⁻², but [mv] = M × LT⁻¹ = MLT⁻¹. The dimensions do not match, so the equation is impossible. The correct form, F = ma, gives MLT⁻² on both sides. Dimensional analysis cannot tell you the ½ in ½at², but it can tell you that at² belongs there and at does not.

---

### Order of magnitude
*BB 90 · physics · measurement*

**Idea.** Sometimes you don't need an exact number. You need to know whether something is roughly a thousand, a million, or a billion. Knowing the right ballpark — the order of magnitude — is often enough to catch nonsense and make sense of the world.

**Concrete.** The mass of an electron is about 9.1 × 10⁻³¹ kg. The mass of our galaxy is about 2.2 × 10⁴¹ kg. Saying "an electron is very light and a galaxy is very heavy" is vague. Saying the galaxy is about 10⁷² times heavier — a 1 followed by 72 zeros — pins it down. The exponent, 72, is the order-of-magnitude difference between them.

**Definition.** The **order of magnitude** of a quantity is the power of ten closest to its numerical value when expressed in a consistent unit. If a value is N = a × 10ᵇ where 0.5 ≤ a < 5, the order of magnitude is 10ᵇ. If a ≥ 5, it rounds to 10ᵇ⁺¹. Orders of magnitude compress an enormous range — the 10⁶¹ span from subatomic to cosmic scales — into about 60 manageable steps.

**In action.** Physicists use order-of-magnitude estimates constantly. When Enrico Fermi asked his students "how many piano tuners are there in Chicago?", he wasn't looking for an exact answer — he was training them to break an unfamiliar problem into estimable pieces, multiply rough numbers, and land in the right ballpark. A Fermi estimate that's off by a factor of 100 may still rule out a thousand impossible answers.

---

### The structure of the world
*BB 91 · physics · foundations*

**Idea.** Reality is arranged in layers, each about a million times larger or smaller than the next. The same laws of physics that hold atoms together also govern the motion of galaxies — but they play out very differently at each rung of this ladder.

**Concrete.** Start at the human scale, roughly 1 metre. Go down: a grain of sand is about 10⁻³ m (a thousand times smaller). A cell is 10⁻⁵ m. An atom is 10⁻¹⁰ m. A proton is 10⁻¹⁵ m. Quarks, as far as we know, are below 10⁻¹⁸ m. Go up: a city is 10⁴ m. Earth is 10⁷ m. The solar system is 10¹³ m. The Milky Way is 10²¹ m. The observable universe is about 10²⁶ m.

**Definition.** The physical world is hierarchically structured: subatomic particles form atoms, atoms form molecules, molecules form condensed matter, matter forms planets and stars, stars form galaxies, galaxies form clusters and superclusters. At each level, new phenomena emerge that do not exist at the level below. Temperature is meaningless for a single atom but dominates the behaviour of a gas.

**In action.** Physics operates at every rung simultaneously. Quantum mechanics governs the subatomic, classical mechanics governs the everyday, general relativity governs the cosmic. A complete description of nature requires all of them, and one of the great unsolved problems in physics is that they do not yet fit together into a single framework. The hunt for a unified theory is, in part, the hunt for the rungs that connect them.

---

### Errors in measurement: no instrument tells the exact truth
*BB 107 · physics · measurement*

**Idea.** No measurement is perfect. Every instrument has a limit, every reading carries some uncertainty. The question is not whether there is error — there always is. The question is how much, and of what kind.

**Concrete.** Weigh the same object five times on a sensitive balance: 12.34 g, 12.36 g, 12.33 g, 12.35 g, 12.37 g. The readings cluster around 12.35 g but never settle on a single number. The spread is random — maybe from air currents, slight vibrations, or the balance's internal noise. If the balance itself is consistently reading 0.05 g too high because it was poorly zeroed, that's a different kind of error — systematic, not random — and it won't show up in the spread.

**Definition.** **Random errors** scatter readings around the true value — they are equally likely to overestimate or underestimate. They can be reduced by averaging many trials. **Systematic errors** shift all readings in one direction — a faulty calibration, a zero error, a consistent bias. Averaging does not reduce them. **Accuracy** is closeness to the true value. **Precision** is how tightly repeated readings cluster, regardless of whether they are accurate. A measurement can be precise but inaccurate (tight cluster, wrong target).

**In action.** The mean of repeated readings is the best estimate of the true value. The **standard deviation** σ = √[Σ(xᵢ − x̄)² / (n − 1)] measures the spread — a small σ means tight readings. Reported results should include the uncertainty: "12.35 ± 0.02 g." The ± notation tells the reader how much to trust the last digit. In labs, error bars on graphs do the same visual work — they show the range within which the true value likely lies.

---

### Relative velocity: motion depends on who is watching
*BB 108 · physics · kinematics*

**Idea.** Two cars on a highway, both doing 80 km/h. To someone on the roadside, both are moving fast. But to the driver of one car, the other car is standing still — they share the same speed and direction. Velocity is always relative to the observer. Change the observer and the velocity changes too.

**Concrete.** A train moves east at 10 m/s relative to the ground. A passenger walks toward the front at 2 m/s relative to the train. To someone on the platform, the passenger moves at 10 + 2 = 12 m/s east. Now the passenger turns and walks toward the rear at 2 m/s. To the platform observer, the passenger moves at 10 − 2 = 8 m/s east — still going forward, just slower. The formula: v(person relative to ground) = v(person relative to train) + v(train relative to ground).

**Definition.** If object A has velocity v⃗_A relative to the ground, and object B has velocity v⃗_B relative to the ground, then the velocity of A relative to B is v⃗_{A/B} = v⃗_A − v⃗_B. Swapping: v⃗_{B/A} = −v⃗_{A/B}. In one dimension, this reduces to subtracting speeds with appropriate signs. In two dimensions, it is vector subtraction: draw both velocity vectors from a common origin, and the vector from the tip of B to the tip of A is v⃗_{A/B}.

**In action.** Raindrops fall vertically at 5 m/s. You're in a car moving at 20 m/s. To you, the rain appears to come at you diagonally — the vector sum of the rain's downward 5 and your forward 20 (in the rain's frame, your car is moving). The apparent direction is tan⁻¹(20/5) ≈ 76° from the vertical. Relative velocity explains why umbrellas tilt forward in a moving car, why a boat drifts downstream when crossing a river, and why head-on collisions are so much worse than rear-end ones — the relative speed adds up.

---

### What physics studies
*BB 01 · physics · foundations*

**Idea.** Physics is the study of matter, motion, force, and energy.

**Concrete.** Physics is the study of matter, motion, force, and energy, and of how these interact over time. It explains why a bridge can bear its own weight without collapsing, why a cup of coffee cools at a predictable rate, and why objects fall toward the ground rather than away from it.

It also explains larger-scale phenomena, including why planets and asteroids follow the orbits and collision courses they do. Some questions remain open, including whether time could run in reverse, whether teleportation is physically possible, and what happens to matter that falls into a black hole. This deck begins with what has already been established through observation and experiment.

---

### Matter and mass
*BB 02 · physics · matter*

**Idea.** Matter is anything that occupies space and has mass.

**Concrete.** Matter is anything that occupies space and has mass: solids, liquids, gases, and everything between, from a single bacterium to a star. The quantity present does not change what counts as matter, only that it takes up space and has mass.

**Definition.** Mass measures an object's resistance to a change in its motion. Changing how an object moves, speeding it up, slowing it down, or redirecting it, requires a force; the more mass an object has, the more force that change requires.

Weight is one way to measure mass, using the pull of gravity on an object, but weight and mass are not the same quantity. A given object has the same mass underwater, in orbit, or anywhere else, because its resistance to a change in motion does not depend on its surroundings. Its weight depends on the strength of the local gravitational pull.

---

### Inertia and Newton's first law
*BB 03 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** An object retains its current state of motion unless acted on by an outside force.

**Concrete.** An object retains its current state of motion unless acted on by an outside force. An object at rest remains at rest; an object already moving continues in a straight line at constant speed, unless something interferes. This is Newton's first law of motion, often called the law of **inertia**.

**Definition.** A hockey puck sliding across rough pavement stops quickly, which can make stopping look like the natural outcome. It is not: the ground exerts a continuous backward force on the puck through friction. On smooth ice, where that backward force is much weaker, the same puck travels far longer before it stops. On a frictionless surface, it would never stop at all. The puck's continued motion is not what needs explaining. Its eventual stop does.

---

### Speed, velocity, and acceleration
*BB 06 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A bus between stops shows speed, velocity, and acceleration changing in turn.

**Concrete.** A bus at a stop is motionless. As the driver pulls away, accelerates down the road, and brakes at a traffic light, three distinct quantities change during that short trip: speed, velocity, and acceleration.

**Definition.** **Speed** is how fast an object is moving, with no reference to direction. At the stop, the bus's speed is zero. As it pulls away, its speed increases. At the light, it falls back to zero. Speed is a single number, such as twenty miles per hour; it states how fast, not which way.

**In action.** **Velocity** is speed combined with a direction. "Twenty miles per hour" is a speed; "twenty miles per hour northbound" is a velocity. Two buses travelling at the same speed but in opposite directions have identical speeds and opposite velocities.

**Deeper.** **Acceleration** is the rate at which velocity changes. As the bus pulls away, its speed increases, so it is accelerating. As it brakes at the light, its speed decreases, so it is decelerating, which means its acceleration is negative.

At rest at the stop, nothing is changing, so acceleration is zero. Cruising at a constant speed in a straight line, acceleration is also zero. Acceleration exists only when the motion itself is changing, whether in speed, in direction, or in both.

---

### How physics expresses a law mathematically
*BB 71 · physics · foundations*

**Idea.** A physical law is a precise relationship between measurable quantities, expressed as an equation rather than a description. Each quantity in that equation has a defined unit, and the equation holds regardless of which specific numbers are substituted into it.

Newton's second law, introduced in BB 05, is one example: F = ma relates force (measured in newtons), mass (in kilograms), and acceleration (in metres per second squared). The equation does not just describe the relationship in words, it specifies exactly how the three quantities trade off against each other, for any values at all. The BBs that follow formalize the qualitative ideas from BB 01-07 into equations of this kind.

---

### Mass, weight, and units
*BB 72 · physics · matter*

**Idea.** Mass is a scalar quantity, measured in **kilograms (kg)**. It has a magnitude only, no direction, and the same number applies wherever the object is located.

Weight is the force of gravity acting on that mass, and unlike mass, it is measured in **newtons (N)**, the unit of force. The two are related by:

W = mgWeight equals mass times the local gravitational acceleration, g.Near Earth's surface, g is approximately 9.8 m/s², so a 1 kg mass weighs about 9.8 N. On the Moon, where g is about 1.6 m/s², the same 1 kg mass weighs only about 1.6 N. The mass, 1 kg, does not change; only the weight does.

---

### Newton's first law as a condition
*BB 73 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Newton's first law can be stated as a single condition: an object moves at constant velocity, including remaining at rest, exactly when the net force acting on it is zero.

&Sigma;F = 0 &rArr; v = constantIf the sum of all forces on an object is zero, its velocity does not change.This condition, &Sigma;F = 0, is what "left alone" meant in BB 03: not the absence of any forces, but the absence of a net, unbalanced one. A book resting on a table has gravity pulling it down and the table pushing it up; these two forces sum to zero, which is why the book stays at constant velocity, meaning at rest.

---

### The friction equation
*BB 74 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** The amount of friction between two surfaces can be calculated, not just described qualitatively. It depends on two things: how strongly the two surfaces are pressed together, and a number describing how rough that particular pair of surfaces is.

F = &mu;NFriction force equals the coefficient of friction (&mu;) times the normal force (N).N is the **normal force**, the force pressing the two surfaces together (for an object resting on a flat surface, this equals its weight). &mu;, the **coefficient of friction**, is a number specific to the pair of materials in contact: rubber on dry concrete has a high &mu;; steel on ice has a low one. A larger &mu; or a larger N both produce more friction.

---

### Rearranging F = ma
*BB 75 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Newton's second law, F = ma, can be rearranged to solve for any one of its three quantities once the other two are known.

a = F / m
m = F / aThe same relationship, solved for acceleration or for mass.Each quantity carries its own SI unit: force in newtons (N), mass in kilograms (kg), acceleration in metres per second squared (m/s&sup2;). One newton is defined as exactly the force that gives a 1 kg mass an acceleration of 1 m/s&sup2;, which is what ties the three units together: 1 N = 1 kg&middot;m/s&sup2;.

---

### Velocity and acceleration as rates of change
*BB 76 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Velocity and acceleration, introduced qualitatively in BB 06, are each defined as a rate of change.

v = &Delta;x / &Delta;t
a = &Delta;v / &Delta;tVelocity is the change in position over the change in time; acceleration is the change in velocity over the change in time.&Delta;x (change in position) and &Delta;v (change in velocity) are measured over an interval of time, &Delta;t. Velocity is measured in metres per second (m/s); acceleration, being a change in velocity per second, is measured in metres per second squared (m/s&sup2;).

---

### Newton's third law as an equation
*BB 77 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Newton's third law can be written as a single equation relating the two forces in any interacting pair.

F&#8321;&#8322; = &minus;F&#8322;&#8321;The force of object 1 on object 2 is equal in magnitude and opposite in direction to the force of object 2 on object 1.The minus sign represents the opposite direction; the magnitudes on both sides are equal. This holds regardless of the masses of the two objects involved: a person pushing on a wall and a person pushing on another person experience the same kind of equal-and-opposite pair, even though the wall does not move and the other person might.

---

### Numerical: force, mass, and units
*BB 78 · physics · foundations*

**Idea.** Newton's second law states F = ma. Consider a 4 kg mass given an acceleration of 3 m/s&sup2;. The force required is:

F = m &times; a = 4 kg &times; 3 m/s&sup2; = 12 kg&middot;m/s&sup2; = 12 NMultiplying mass by acceleration gives a result in kg&middot;m/s&sup2;, which is defined as the newton.This confirms the unit relationship from BB 71: one newton is exactly one kilogram-metre-per-second-squared, not a separately defined unit.

---

### Numerical: weight on Earth and the Moon
*BB 79 · physics · matter*

**Idea.** A 12 kg object sits on a table. Its weight is:

W = mg = 12 kg &times; 9.8 m/s&sup2; = 117.6 NWeight equals mass times gravitational acceleration.On the Moon, where g &asymp; 1.6 m/s&sup2;, the same object's weight would be 12 &times; 1.6 = 19.2 N. Its mass, 12 kg, is unchanged in both cases; only the gravitational acceleration differs.

---

### Numerical: the normal force on a resting box
*BB 80 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A 5 kg box rests on a table. Gravity pulls down on it with a force of W = mg = 5 &times; 9.8 = 49 N. For the box to remain at rest, per BB 73, the net force on it must be zero.

&Sigma;F = 0 &rArr; N = 49 NThe table must push up on the box with a normal force exactly equal to its weight.If the table instead pushed up with only 40 N, the forces would not balance, and the box would accelerate downward. The 49 N normal force is not a coincidence: it is the exact value required by &Sigma;F = 0.

---

### Numerical: maximum friction on a crate
*BB 81 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A 10 kg crate rests on a concrete floor, where the coefficient of friction between crate and floor is &mu; = 0.5. The normal force is N = mg = 10 &times; 9.8 = 98 N.

F = &mu;N = 0.5 &times; 98 = 49 NThe maximum friction force resisting the crate's sliding.A horizontal push smaller than 49 N will not be enough to start the crate sliding; a push exceeding 49 N will overcome friction and set it in motion.

---

### Numerical: force and acceleration of a car
*BB 82 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A car of mass 1200 kg needs to accelerate at 2 m/s&sup2;. Using F = ma, the force its engine must produce is:

F = m &times; a = 1200 kg &times; 2 m/s&sup2; = 2400 NForce required for the given mass and acceleration.If the same car's engine instead produces a constant 1800 N, the resulting acceleration is a = F/m = 1800 / 1200 = 1.5 m/s&sup2;, lower than before, because the same mass now receives less force.

---

### Numerical: a cyclist's velocity and acceleration
*BB 83 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A cyclist travels from a position 20 m to a position 170 m along a straight road, taking 30 seconds to do so. Their average velocity is:

v = &Delta;x / &Delta;t = (170 &minus; 20) / 30 = 150 / 30 = 5 m/sAverage velocity over the 30-second interval.If the cyclist's velocity increases from 5 m/s to 9 m/s over the next 4 seconds, their acceleration over that interval is a = &Delta;v / &Delta;t = (9 &minus; 5) / 4 = 1 m/s&sup2;.

---

### Numerical: two skaters pushing off
*BB 84 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A 70 kg skater pushes off a 50 kg skater on frictionless ice. The push exerts 140 N on the lighter skater. By Newton's third law, the lighter skater exerts exactly 140 N back on the heavier one, in the opposite direction.

a&#8321; = F / m&#8321; = 140 / 70 = 2 m/s&sup2;
a&#8322; = F / m&#8322; = 140 / 50 = 2.8 m/s&sup2;Equal and opposite forces, applied to different masses, produce different accelerations.The forces are identical in magnitude, exactly as Newton's third law requires, but the resulting accelerations differ because the two skaters have different masses. Equal force does not mean equal acceleration.

---

## 2. Forces & motion

### What is a force?
*BB 698 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A force is a push or a pull. When you push a door open, pull a suitcase, or kick a football, you're applying a force. Forces can make things speed up, slow down, change direction, or change shape. The unit of force is the newton. 1 newton is roughly the weight of a small apple. Your body weighs about 700 newtons. This is where physics starts: every movement, every change, begins with a force.

**Concrete.** Pick up a 100 g apple. The force your hand exerts to hold it steady against gravity is about 1 newton. Push a 1 kg bag of sugar across a table — you feel resistance. That's friction, another force. A 70 kg person weighs 70 × 9.8 = 686 N. A car crash at 50 km/h generates forces of tens of thousands of newtons. Forces are everywhere — gravity pulls you down, the chair pushes you up, the wind pushes the trees.

**Definition.** Force F is a push or pull measured in newtons (N). 1 N = 1 kg·m/s². Force is a vector — it has both magnitude (how strong) and direction (which way). Types: contact forces (push, pull, friction, normal, tension) and field forces (gravity, electric, magnetic). Newton's laws describe how forces affect motion. The key phrase: "A force changes motion or shape."

---

### Gravity: the force you can't see
*BB 699 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Drop a pen. It falls. Every single object on Earth is pulled downward by gravity. The Earth is enormous — 6,000,000,000,000,000,000,000,000 kg — and its gravity pulls on everything near it. The force of gravity on an object is called its weight. On the Moon, you'd weigh one-sixth as much because the Moon is smaller. Your mass (how much stuff you're made of) is the same everywhere. Your weight changes depending on where you are.

**Concrete.** A 1 kg bag of sugar has mass 1 kg everywhere — on Earth, on the Moon, in space. On Earth, it weighs 1 × 9.8 = 9.8 N. On the Moon, it weighs 1 × 1.6 = 1.6 N. In deep space, far from any planet, it weighs almost 0 N — but it still has mass 1 kg. A 70 kg person weighs 686 N on Earth, 113 N on the Moon, and 1736 N on Jupiter. Mass is the same; weight depends on where you are. Gravity pulls everything downward at 9.8 m/s² near Earth's surface. Every second of falling, your speed increases by 9.8 m/s. After 3 seconds: 29.4 m/s (106 km/h).

**Definition.** Gravity is why we stay on the ground, why the Moon orbits Earth, and why Earth orbits the Sun. Without gravity, the atmosphere would float away into space. Bridges and buildings must withstand gravity's pull on every beam and brick. Even your own body is shaped by gravity — astronauts in space grow 3–5 cm taller because their spines decompress without gravity's constant squeeze. Weight = mass × gravity. That's all. But that simple multiplication explains everything from falling apples to orbiting planets.

---

### Friction: why things stop
*BB 700 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Slide a book across a table. It slows down and stops. Why? Friction. The bottom of the book and the top of the table are rough — microscopically rough, with tiny peaks and valleys that catch on each other. Friction always opposes motion. Without it, the book would slide forever. Friction turns kinetic energy into heat — rub your hands together fast and they get warm. That's friction in action.

**Concrete.** A 2 kg book on a wooden table. The weight is 19.6 N. The friction force that opposes sliding is roughly 0.3 × 19.6 = 5.9 N (for wood on wood). Push with 4 N — the book doesn't move (static friction holds it). Push with 7 N — the book slides (kinetic friction is slightly less than static). Once sliding, the friction is about 4.9 N, so the net force is 7 − 4.9 = 2.1 N, and the book accelerates slowly. A car tyre on dry asphalt: friction coefficient ≈ 0.8. On ice: ≈ 0.1. That's why you can't stop on ice — there's almost no friction.

**Definition.** Friction is everywhere. Car brakes use friction to stop — brake pads clamp onto a metal disc, converting kinetic energy to heat. If brakes overheat, the friction drops (brake fade) — that's why trucks use engine braking on long descents. Without friction, you couldn't walk (your foot would slip backward), hold a pencil, or drive a car. Even screws and nails stay in place because of friction. Friction is usually the enemy of efficiency (it wastes energy as heat), but it's also what makes controlled motion possible. A world without friction is a world where nothing stays where you put it.

---

### Normal force: why you don't fall through the floor
*BB 701 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Stand on the floor. Gravity pulls you down. Why don't you fall through? The floor pushes you up. This upward push is the normal force — "normal" because it's perpendicular to the surface. It's the reason tables hold plates, chairs hold people, and bridges hold cars. The normal force adjusts itself — it's exactly as strong as it needs to be to stop things from passing through each other.

**Concrete.** A 70 kg person stands on a flat floor. Weight = 70 × 9.8 = 686 N downward. The floor pushes up with exactly 686 N — the normal force N. If the person carries a 10 kg backpack (total mass 80 kg, weight 784 N), the normal force increases to 784 N. The floor doesn't care — it just pushes harder. If the floor tilts (like a ramp), the normal force decreases. On a 30° ramp: N = mg cos 30° = 686 × 0.866 = 594 N. The remaining component of weight (mg sin 30° = 343 N) pulls the person down the slope — that's what friction must oppose.

**Definition.** The normal force N is the force a surface exerts perpendicular to itself to support an object. It's a reaction force — it exists only when something pushes against the surface. N = mg for a flat horizontal surface with no other vertical forces. On an incline: N = mg cos θ. The normal force is NOT always equal to weight — in an accelerating lift: N = m(g + a) going up, N = m(g − a) going down. If a > g (accelerating downward faster than free fall), N becomes zero — you lose contact with the floor.

---

### Speed: how fast?
*BB 703 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Speed tells you how fast something is moving. You calculate it by dividing the distance travelled by the time it took: speed = distance ÷ time. If you run 100 metres in 12 seconds, your speed is about 8.3 metres per second. The speedometer in a car shows instantaneous speed — how fast right now. Average speed accounts for speeding up, slowing down, and stopping.

**Concrete.** A car travels 240 km in 3 hours. Average speed = 240 ÷ 3 = 80 km/h. But the car probably went faster on the highway and slower in town. Instantaneous speed at any moment could be 120 km/h or 30 km/h — the average smooths out the variations. Usain Bolt's 100 m world record (9.58 s) gives an average speed of 100 ÷ 9.58 = 10.44 m/s = 37.58 km/h. His top speed during that race was about 44.7 km/h. Speed of sound: 343 m/s (1235 km/h). Speed of light: 300,000,000 m/s.

**Definition.** Speed = distance/time. Units: m/s (SI), km/h, mph. 1 m/s = 3.6 km/h. **Average speed:** v_avg = total distance / total time. **Instantaneous speed:** the speed at a single instant — what a speedometer shows. **Velocity** is speed with a direction (50 km/h north). Speed is a scalar (just a number); velocity is a vector (number + direction). In physics, "speed" and "velocity" are not the same — velocity cares which way you're going.

---

### Mass: how much stuff?
*BB 705 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Mass is how much "stuff" an object is made of. A brick has more mass than a sponge the same size. Mass is measured in kilograms. More importantly, mass measures inertia — an object's resistance to being accelerated. It's much harder to push a car than a bicycle because the car has more mass. In deep space, far from any gravity, a 1000 kg spacecraft still has 1000 kg of mass. It just weighs nothing.

**Concrete.** A 1 kg bag of sugar has the same mass everywhere in the universe. To accelerate it at 1 m/s² requires 1 N of force. A 1000 kg car requires 1000 N to accelerate at 1 m/s². The car has 1000× more inertia. Your own mass (perhaps 70 kg) is mostly water (about 60%) — roughly 42 litres of water's worth. The rest is protein, fat, and minerals. The Earth's mass is 6 × 10²⁴ kg — that's a 6 followed by 24 zeros. The Sun's mass is 2 × 10³⁰ kg — about 330,000 Earths.

**Definition.** Mass m is measured in kilograms (kg). It is a scalar — no direction. Mass is distinct from weight: mass is intrinsic (same everywhere), weight is the gravitational force on that mass (w = mg, different on different planets). **Inertia** is the tendency of mass to resist changes in motion — Newton's First Law. The more mass, the harder to accelerate (F = ma). In the British engineering system, mass is measured in slugs (1 slug ≈ 14.59 kg).

---

### Free-body diagrams: drawing the forces
*BB 709 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Before you solve any force problem, draw the object alone — isolated from everything around it — and draw every force acting ON it as an arrow. This is a free-body diagram. It turns a messy real-world situation (a box being pushed, a chandelier hanging from chains, a car on a slope) into a simple diagram you can solve with F = ma. This one skill separates people who can do physics from people who get lost in word problems.

**Concrete.** A 10 kg box rests on a table. Free-body diagram: (1) Draw the box as a dot or a simple rectangle. (2) Draw weight arrow pointing DOWN: mg = 10 × 9.8 = 98 N. (3) Draw normal force arrow pointing UP: N = 98 N (since it's not accelerating vertically). (4) If someone pushes it right with 30 N: draw that arrow. (5) If friction opposes with 15 N: draw that arrow left. Net horizontal force: 30 − 15 = 15 N right. Acceleration: a = 15/10 = 1.5 m/s² right.

A chandelier hanging from two chains at 30°: three forces — weight down, tension T₁ up-left, tension T₂ up-right. Solve using components: T₁ sin 30° + T₂ sin 30° = mg.

**Definition.** Every physics problem starts with a free-body diagram. It's the first thing an engineer draws before calculating whether a bridge will hold. It's how a structural analyst checks whether a building can withstand an earthquake. It's the first step in designing a roller coaster, a rocket, or a prosthetic limb. The rules are simple: isolate the object, draw ALL forces acting ON it (never include forces BY it), label them clearly, choose coordinate axes, and apply ΣF = ma. Master the free-body diagram, and you've mastered half of mechanics.

---

### Balanced forces: when nothing moves
*BB 710 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** When all the forces on an object cancel out perfectly, the net force is zero. The object doesn't accelerate — it stays at rest or moves in a straight line at constant speed. This is equilibrium. A chair holding a person, a bridge supporting cars, a picture hanging on a wall — all are in equilibrium. The forces are balanced, even though they're not zero individually.

**Concrete.** A 5 kg picture hangs from a single wire. Weight = 49 N downward. The wire pulls up with 49 N — equilibrium. A 80 kg person stands on a floor. Weight = 784 N down. Normal force = 784 N up. ΣF = 0 — equilibrium. A car moving at constant 100 km/h on a flat highway: engine force forward ≈ 500 N, air resistance backward ≈ 500 N. Net force = 0 — the car is in dynamic equilibrium (constant velocity). A skydiver at terminal velocity (about 55 m/s = 200 km/h): weight down = 700 N, air resistance up = 700 N. Net force = 0. The forces are balanced, so speed is constant.

**Definition.** **Equilibrium** means ΣF = 0 — the net force is zero. **Static equilibrium:** object at rest (book on a table). **Dynamic equilibrium:** object moving at constant velocity (car cruising, skydiver at terminal velocity). In both cases, a = 0. For problems: (1) Draw free-body diagram, (2) set ΣF_x = 0 and ΣF_y = 0, (3) solve for unknowns. Equilibrium is the simplest type of mechanics problem — all the forces add to zero. The moment an object accelerates, you need ΣF = ma instead.

---

### Unbalanced forces: when things speed up
*BB 711 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** When forces don't cancel, the object accelerates. A car speeding up, a plane taking off, a ball falling — all have unbalanced forces. The net force (what's left after adding all the forces as vectors) is not zero, and by F = ma, the object accelerates in the direction of the net force. This is dynamic motion — the most common situation in the real world.

**Concrete.** A 50 kg crate is pushed with 200 N across a floor. Friction pushes back with 80 N. Net force = 200 − 80 = 120 N forward. a = 120/50 = 2.4 m/s². After 3 seconds: v = at = 2.4 × 3 = 7.2 m/s (26 km/h). A skydiver has just jumped — weight down = 700 N, air resistance initially 0 N. Net force = 700 N down. a = 700/70 = 10 m/s² ≈ g. Speed increases rapidly. As speed builds, air resistance grows. At terminal velocity (55 m/s), air resistance = 700 N, net force = 0, a = 0 — back to equilibrium. A rocket at launch: thrust 1,000,000 N up, weight 500,000 N down. Net force 500,000 N up. a = 500,000/50,000 = 10 m/s² — about 1g. As fuel burns, mass decreases, acceleration increases.

**Definition.** Unbalanced forces drive every vehicle, every sport, every natural phenomenon. A sprinter bursts from the blocks: the ground pushes forward (friction) > air resistance backward — net force forward. A plane climbs: lift > weight, thrust > drag — net force upward and forward. An earthquake shakes a building: the ground acceleration creates unbalanced forces at every floor, and the structure must withstand them. The skill is always the same: find all the forces, add them as vectors, and if the sum isn't zero, divide by the mass to find the acceleration. F = ma — the equation that launched a thousand rockets.

---

### The three laws together: how forces rule motion
*BB 712 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Newton's three laws work together. The First Law defines the default: no force means no change. The Second Law quantifies change: F = ma relates force, mass, and acceleration. The Third Law ensures forces come in pairs: every push has an equal push back. Together, they explain every motion in the universe — from the fall of an apple to the orbit of a galaxy. These are the rules of the game.

**Concrete.** A rocket launch uses all three laws. First Law: the rocket sits on the pad — at rest, stays at rest — until the engines fire. Second Law: F = ma — thrust of 1,000,000 N on a 50,000 kg rocket gives a = 20 m/s² upward. Third Law: the engines push exhaust down; the exhaust pushes the rocket up. All three laws operate simultaneously, in every frame of the launch video. A car accelerating from a stoplight: engine pushes wheels backward (Third Law — wheels push ground, ground pushes car), net force forward (Second Law — a = F/m), and without the force it would stay at rest (First Law). They're not three separate ideas — they're three aspects of one unified description of how forces cause motion.

**Definition.** Every machine ever built obeys these three laws. An aircraft in flight: wings push air down (Third), air pushes wings up — when lift > weight, the plane accelerates upward (Second). In cruise, lift = weight and thrust = drag, so a = 0 (First — dynamic equilibrium). A bridge: every beam and cable is in static equilibrium — First Law, with forces resolved and summed to zero. A collision: two cars hitting each other — the forces are equal (Third), the accelerations depend on mass (Second), and after the crash, the wreckage slides to a stop as friction (an unbalanced force) decelerates it (Second again). Newton's three laws are not museum pieces — they are the operating system of the physical world.

---

### The forces toolkit
*BB 713 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** After 15 BBs, the foundation is laid. Force is a push or pull measured in newtons. The main forces are gravity (weight = mg), friction (opposes motion), normal (surface pushes back), and tension (rope pulls). Mass is how much stuff; weight is the pull of gravity on that stuff. Speed is distance/time; acceleration is change in speed/time. Newton's three laws unify everything: things keep doing what they're doing unless pushed, F = ma tells you how much they accelerate, and forces always come in equal opposite pairs.

**Concrete.** Identify the principle:

• You push a stalled car: you're applying a force. The car's mass resists (inertia). F = ma determines how fast it accelerates.

• A book on a table: weight down, normal up. Balanced — no acceleration.

• A skydiver: initially weight >> air resistance → accelerating down. Eventually equal → constant speed.

• A rocket: thrust up, weight down. If thrust > weight → accelerating up. Exhaust down = rocket up (Third Law).

• A car braking: friction between tyres and road decelerates the car. F = ma → a = F_friction/m.

**Definition.** **Forces toolkit:**

• **Force:** push or pull (N). **Weight:** w = mg — gravitational force.

• **Friction:** f = μN — opposes motion. Static (before motion) vs kinetic (during).

• **Normal:** N = mg cos θ — surface pushes perpendicular.

• **Tension:** T — rope pulls, never pushes.

• **Mass:** m (kg) — amount of matter. Inertia.

• **Speed:** v = d/t (m/s). **Acceleration:** a = Δv/Δt (m/s²).

• **Newton I:** ΣF = 0 ⇔ v constant. **Newton II:** F = ma. **Newton III:** F_AB = −F_BA.

• **Free-body diagram:** isolate object, draw ALL forces, ΣF = ma.

• **Equilibrium:** ΣF = 0. **Unbalanced:** ΣF ≠ 0 → a ≠ 0.

---

### How hard the stop is
*Card 08 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** When something moving comes to a stop, what hurts isn't the speed it had. It's how sharply that speed was taken away.

Drop the same speed to zero over two seconds and the stop is gentle. Drop it over a tenth of a second and it's brutal. The force depends on the rate the speed falls, and squeezing the same drop into less time makes that rate, and the force, far bigger.

**Concrete.** Jump off a chair onto a concrete floor and it jars your knees. Jump from the same height onto a thick crash mat and you feel almost nothing.

You hit at the same speed both times. The mat doesn't change your speed, it changes how quickly that speed is taken off you, spreading the drop over a longer moment. Slower drop, gentler force. Airbags, foam packaging, boxing gloves, a fielder drawing their hands back as they catch: each one stretches the stop, which softens the rate, which shrinks the force.

---

### Why crumple zones exist
*Card 09 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A car's nose is built to crumple on purpose. It seems backwards to design something to fall apart, but the crumpling is the point.

By folding, the nose stretches the stop from a sudden instant into a fraction of a second longer. That extra sliver of time is what drops the force from deadly to survivable. The passenger, held by the belt, is brought to rest over that stretched moment instead of all at once.

---

### Contact forces and field forces
*BB 110 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Push a book and your hand touches it. Drop the book and the Earth pulls it without touching it at all. Forces come in two kinds: those that need contact, and those that reach across empty space.

**Concrete.** Place a book on a table. The table pushes up on the book — a **contact force**. The Earth pulls the book down — a **field force** (gravity). Both act simultaneously. The book doesn't fall through the table because the two forces balance. Now tie a string to the book and pull — the string transmits your pull through tension, another contact force. Push a trolley — your hand exerts a normal contact force. Rub your hand across the table — friction resists, also contact.

**Definition.** **Contact forces** arise from physical contact between two bodies: normal force, friction, tension, air resistance, spring force. They result from electromagnetic interactions between the atoms at the surfaces, but at the macroscopic scale they are modelled as forces that act at the point of contact. **Field forces** (or action-at-a-distance forces) act without physical contact: gravitational force, electrostatic force, magnetic force. They are mediated by fields that permeate space.

**In action.** Every force in a mechanics problem is either contact or field. Gravity is always a field force. Normal forces, tension, and friction are always contact. When drawing a free body diagram, a field force (weight) acts at the centre of mass; contact forces act at the point of contact. This distinction matters because contact forces can disappear — remove the table and the normal force vanishes instantly — while field forces cannot be switched off.

---

### Free body diagrams: drawing every force
*BB 111 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A single object can have five or six forces acting on it at once — gravity pulling down, a surface pushing up, a string pulling sideways, friction dragging back. To sort out what happens, isolate the object and draw every force on it as an arrow. This is a free body diagram — the first step in solving almost any mechanics problem.

**Concrete.** A block rests on a rough slope at 30°. Draw the block alone — a dot or a box. Add arrows: weight mg straight down. The normal force N perpendicular to the slope, pushing the block away from the surface. Friction f pointing up the slope, stopping the block from sliding down. That's three forces. No other objects touch the block (no string, no push), so no more contact forces. The diagram is complete. The block is stationary, so the three arrows must sum to zero.

**Definition.** A **free body diagram** (FBD) is a sketch showing a single body isolated from its surroundings, with every force acting ON that body represented as a labelled arrow. Rules: (1) Only forces ON the body — never forces the body exerts on others. (2) Every force has an identifiable source — gravity from Earth, normal from the surface, tension from the string, etc. (3) Draw forces starting from the point where they act. (4) Choose a coordinate system, usually aligning one axis with the direction of acceleration.

**In action.** The FBD turns a messy physical situation into a clean maths problem. Once all forces are drawn, write Newton's second law for each axis: ΣFₓ = maₓ, ΣFᵧ = maᵧ. If the object is in equilibrium, both sums equal zero, and the forces simply balance. If it's accelerating, the net force in the direction of acceleration is non-zero. The FBD is not optional — skipping it is the most common cause of sign errors and missed forces in mechanics.

---

### Weight and the normal force
*BB 112 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Stand on a bathroom scale. It reads your weight — or so you think. The scale actually measures the normal force pushing up on your feet. If you're stationary on level ground, that normal force equals your weight. But step into a lift that accelerates upward, and the reading jumps — your weight hasn't changed, but the normal force has.

**Concrete.** Stand on a scale in a lift. At rest or moving at constant speed: the scale reads your true weight mg. As the lift accelerates upward at a = 2 m/s²: the scale must push harder to accelerate you upward, so it reads m(g + a) — you feel heavier. As the lift accelerates downward at 2 m/s²: the scale reads m(g − a) — you feel lighter. If the lift cable snaps and it falls freely (a = g), the scale reads zero — you are weightless, even though gravity is still pulling on you.

**Definition.** **Weight** W = mg is the gravitational force the Earth exerts on an object — it acts downward from the centre of mass and never disappears as long as the object is near Earth. The **normal force** N is the contact force a surface exerts perpendicular to itself, preventing an object from passing through it. The normal force adjusts to whatever is needed — it is not a fixed property of the object. **Apparent weight** is what a scale reads: the magnitude of the normal force acting on the object.

**In action.** The normal force is a constraint force — it takes whatever value is necessary to prevent penetration, up to the breaking point of the surface. On a flat surface with no other vertical forces, N = mg. Add a hand pushing down, and N = mg + F_push. In a turning car, the normal force from the seat tilts to provide centripetal acceleration. The normal force is not mg — it is the answer to "how hard does the surface have to push to keep this object where it is?"

---

### Tension: force through a string
*BB 113 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Tie a rope to a box and pull. The force travels from your hand, through the rope, to the box. The rope is the middleman — it transmits your pull without storing or losing any of it (if we idealise it as massless). This transmitted pull is called tension.

**Concrete.** Two blocks connected by a light string lie on a frictionless table. You pull the front block with a force F. The string tightens and pulls the rear block forward. If the string is massless, the tension is the same at both ends — the string pulls equally on both blocks. The whole system accelerates together. The tension is less than F because F pulls the front block directly AND indirectly (through the string) — the net force on each block, divided by its mass, must equal the common acceleration.

**Definition.** **Tension** is the pulling force transmitted through a string, rope, cable, or chain when it is pulled tight by forces acting from opposite ends. For an ideal massless and inextensible string: (1) tension is the same at every point along the string, (2) tension pulls equally on the objects at both ends, (3) the direction of tension is always along the string, pulling away from the object. If the string passes over a frictionless pulley, tension remains unchanged across the pulley.

**In action.** In a typical pulley problem — a mass hanging from a string looped over a frictionless pulley, with the other end attached to a mass on a table — tension is the unknown that links the two objects. Write F = ma for each mass with T in the equation, then solve simultaneously. The tension is always less than the hanging weight (if the system accelerates) — some of the gravitational force goes into accelerating the hanging mass itself, not just pulling the other one.

---

### Hooke's law: the spring that pushes back in proportion
*BB 114 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Pull a spring and it pulls back. Compress it and it pushes back. The harder you stretch or squash it, the harder it fights — and the relationship is perfectly proportional. Double the stretch, double the force. This is Hooke's law, and it makes the spring the simplest force-machine in physics.

**Concrete.** Hang a spring vertically. Attach a 1 kg mass and the spring stretches by 2 cm. Attach a 2 kg mass — it stretches by 4 cm. Attach 3 kg — 6 cm. Every extra kilogram adds the same extra stretch. The spring's behaviour is captured by one number: its **spring constant** k, the ratio of force to extension. For this spring, k = (1 kg × 10 m/s²) / 0.02 m = 500 N/m.

**Definition.** **Hooke's law** states that the force exerted by an ideal spring is proportional to its extension or compression from its natural length, and acts in the direction that restores the natural length: F = −kx. Here, x is the displacement from equilibrium, k is the **spring constant** (N/m), and the minus sign means the spring always pushes or pulls toward equilibrium. Hooke's law holds for most springs within their **elastic limit** — stretch too far and the spring deforms permanently, breaking the law.

**In action.** Hooke's law appears far beyond springs. A guitar string, a diving board, the suspension of a car, a beam in a bridge — all behave as springs for small displacements. The restoring force in simple harmonic motion (pendulums, vibrating molecules, AC circuits) springs directly from F = −kx. Even the forces between atoms in a solid approximate Hooke's law near equilibrium. Robert Hooke published it in 1676 as the anagram "ceiiinosssttuv" — he later revealed it unscrambled as "ut tensio, sic vis": as the extension, so the force.

---

### Momentum: the quantity Newton cared about
*BB 116 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A cricket ball and a truck, both moving at the same speed — which would you rather stop? The truck, obviously, because it has more mass. A cricket ball at 5 m/s and at 150 km/h — which is harder to catch? The fast one. The quantity that captures both mass and speed together is momentum.

**Concrete.** A 0.16 kg cricket ball travels at 40 m/s. Its momentum is 0.16 × 40 = 6.4 kg·m/s. A 2000 kg truck crawls at 0.0032 m/s. Its momentum is also 6.4 kg·m/s. They have the same momentum — it would take the same impulse (force × time) to stop either one. But the ball's energy (½mv² = 128 J) is vastly different from the truck's (½ × 2000 × 0.0032² ≈ 0.01 J). Momentum and energy are different quantities, and they answer different questions.

**Definition.** **Momentum** p⃗ of a particle is the product of its mass and its velocity: p⃗ = mv⃗. It is a vector — magnitude mv, direction same as velocity. The SI unit is kg·m/s. The momentum of a system of particles is the vector sum of the momenta of all particles. Momentum, unlike kinetic energy, has no separate form for rotation or heat — it is always just mass × velocity, summed over everything.

**In action.** Newton actually stated his second law in terms of momentum: "The rate of change of momentum of a body is proportional to the applied force and takes place in the direction of that force." F⃗ = dp⃗/dt. When mass is constant, this reduces to F⃗ = ma⃗. But for a rocket ejecting fuel — where mass changes — only the momentum form works. Momentum is also the central conserved quantity in collisions: the total momentum before a collision equals the total after, regardless of energy loss, crumpling, or fragmentation.

---

### Equilibrium: when all forces cancel
*BB 119 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A lamp hangs from the ceiling, motionless. A book sits on a table. A bridge holds trucks without budging. These are all in equilibrium — the forces on them sum to zero. Nothing accelerates. Nothing changes. It's not that no forces act; it's that they perfectly cancel.

**Concrete.** A traffic light weighing 100 N hangs from two cables angled at 45° to the horizontal. The light is stationary, so the net force is zero. Vertically: the upward components of both tensions must equal the weight downward. Horizontally: the leftward component of one cable must exactly cancel the rightward component of the other. Solving: each cable's tension is about 71 N — more than half the weight, because pulling at an angle is inefficient at providing vertical support.

**Definition.** A body is in **translational equilibrium** when the vector sum of all external forces acting on it is zero: ΣF⃗ = 0. This gives two scalar conditions: ΣFₓ = 0 and ΣFᵧ = 0 (three in 3D). A body in equilibrium has zero acceleration — it may be at rest (**static equilibrium**) or moving at constant velocity (**dynamic equilibrium**). Rotational equilibrium additionally requires the net torque to be zero: Στ = 0.

**In action.** Equilibrium problems follow a standard recipe: (1) draw the FBD, (2) choose axes, (3) resolve all forces into components, (4) set ΣFₓ = 0 and ΣFᵧ = 0, (5) solve the resulting equations. Most structures — bridges, cranes, shelves, tents — are designed to be in static equilibrium under expected loads. The analysis tells you the tension in each cable, the compression in each beam, and the load at each joint. When equilibrium fails, things accelerate — and the second law takes over.

---

### Why study physics? The world is a machine
*BB 638 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Every bridge, every engine, every electrical device, every building that doesn't collapse — all of them work because someone, somewhere, did the physics. Physics is not a subject. It's the rulebook the universe enforces, and engineering is the art of building things that obey the rules. If you want to build anything — a circuit, a structure, a machine — you need to speak the language of forces, energy, and motion. This is that language.

**Concrete.** A structural engineer designing a floor beam asks: what's the maximum load? Physics answers with stress = force/area. An electrical engineer designing a motor asks: what torque will it produce? Physics answers with τ = NIAB. A civil engineer designing a dam asks: what force does the water exert? Physics answers with F = ½ρgwh². Every one of these problems starts with measurement: how many metres? how many kilograms? how many seconds? The numbers matter. A beam designed with "about this thick" intuition collapses. A beam designed with σ = F/A and a safety factor stands for a century.

**Definition.** Physics is the study of matter, energy, and their interactions, expressed in quantitative laws. **Engineering** is the application of these laws to design and build. The foundation is **measurement:** every physical quantity has a numerical value and a unit. The SI system uses seven base units: metre (m, length), kilogram (kg, mass), second (s, time), ampere (A, current), kelvin (K, temperature), mole (mol, amount of substance), candela (cd, luminous intensity). All other units (newton, joule, watt, pascal) are combinations of these. **Significant figures** matter: writing 5.0 m means something different from 5.00 m — the zeros indicate measurement precision.

**In action.** Engineering failures are often unit errors. The Mars Climate Orbiter ($327 million) burned up in the Martian atmosphere in 1999 because one team used metric units (newton-seconds) and another used imperial (pound-seconds). The Gimli Glider (1983) ran out of fuel mid-flight because of a metric-to-imperial conversion error during refuelling — the pilot dead-sticked a 767 onto a disused runway. These disasters trace back to the same root cause: physics doesn't care which units you use, but it does demand consistency. The first lesson of applied physics is: always, always check your units.

---

### Newton's first law: why seatbelts exist
*BB 639 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A book on a table stays there. A curling stone on ice slides in a nearly straight line at nearly constant speed. Both are doing the same thing: obeying Newton's first law. An object at rest stays at rest; an object in motion stays in uniform motion — unless a force acts on it. This is inertia: the universe's stubborn refusal to change what it's already doing.

**Concrete.** You're in a car travelling at 60 km/h (16.7 m/s). The car hits a wall and stops in 0.10 seconds. Your body, obeying Newton's first law, continues forward at 16.7 m/s until the seatbelt exerts a force to stop you. Your deceleration: a = Δv/Δt = 16.7/0.10 = 167 m/s² ≈ 17g. The force on a 70 kg person: F = ma = 70 × 167 = 11,690 N — over a tonne of force. The seatbelt spreads this force across your chest and pelvis over about 0.3 m of webbing stretch, reducing peak force. Without the seatbelt, you hit the dashboard in ~0.01 s — the force is 10× higher, and the localised pressure ruptures organs.

On ice (nearly frictionless), a hockey puck slides at almost constant speed. The tiny friction from the ice (~0.005 × weight) slowly decelerates it. In deep space, far from any gravity, a spacecraft with engines off maintains exactly constant velocity — forever. Newton's first law is perfect in the absence of forces.

**Definition.** **Newton's First Law (Law of Inertia):** A body remains at rest or in uniform motion in a straight line unless acted upon by a net external force. **Inertia** is the tendency of an object to resist changes in its velocity — it is proportional to mass. The law defines **inertial reference frames:** frames in which the law holds (non-accelerating frames). In an accelerating car, a coffee cup slides backward across the dashboard even though no horizontal force acts on it — the car is not an inertial frame. The first law is not derivable from the second; it's a separate statement about the existence of inertial frames.

**In action.** Every safety device in a vehicle is an application of Newton's first law. Seatbelts, airbags, crumple zones — all exist to apply a controlled force over time to stop your body when the car stops. A rear-facing child seat is safer than forward-facing because the force is distributed across the entire back during a frontal collision. In space, Newton's first law means spacecraft don't need to burn fuel to keep moving — they coast. The Voyager probes, launched in 1977, are still moving at ~17 km/s, and will continue at essentially that speed for billions of years unless they hit something. Newton's first law is why the universe doesn't need a battery.

---

### Newton's second law: F = ma, the engine of physics
*BB 640 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Push a shopping cart: it accelerates. Push harder: it accelerates more. Fill it with bricks: the same push produces less acceleration. Newton's second law quantifies this: F = ma. The acceleration is proportional to the force and inversely proportional to the mass. This one equation predicts the motion of everything from falling apples to orbiting planets.

**Concrete.** A 1200 kg car accelerates from 0 to 100 km/h (27.8 m/s) in 8.0 seconds. Acceleration a = Δv/Δt = 27.8/8.0 = 3.47 m/s². Net force required: F = ma = 1200 × 3.47 = 4167 N — about 425 kg of thrust. The engine produces this force at the wheels via friction with the road. If the car's mass were 1800 kg (loaded with passengers): the same engine force would produce a = F/m = 4167/1800 = 2.31 m/s², and 0–100 would take 27.8/2.31 = 12.0 seconds.

In the British engineering system, mass is measured in slugs: 1 slug = 14.59 kg. A force of 1 pound (lb) accelerates 1 slug at 1 ft/s². An American car weighing 3200 lb has a mass of 3200/32.2 ≈ 99.4 slugs (since g = 32.2 ft/s²). Frye emphasises: pounds are force, not mass. Your "weight" in pounds is a force — the Earth pulling on your mass.

**Definition.** **Newton's Second Law:** The acceleration of an object is directly proportional to the net force acting on it and inversely proportional to its mass: a⃗ = F⃗_net/m, or equivalently **F⃗ = ma⃗**. Force is measured in newtons (N): 1 N = 1 kg·m/s². The law is vector: acceleration is in the direction of the net force. **Mass** (kg) measures inertia — resistance to acceleration. **Weight** (N) is the gravitational force on a mass: w = mg. Do not confuse them. In **engineering units:** 1 slug = 1 lb·s²/ft, 1 lb = 1 slug × 1 ft/s². The conversion: 1 lb ≈ 4.448 N.

**In action.** F = ma is the most-used equation in physics. It tells a rocket engineer how much thrust is needed to achieve a desired acceleration. It tells a structural engineer the maximum force a floor must withstand during an earthquake (a = ground acceleration, m = building mass). It tells a sports scientist the force a sprinter's legs exert during the starting block push. In accident reconstruction, F = ma works backward: measure the skid marks (→ deceleration from friction), find a, and you know the pre-braking speed. The same equation that launched Sputnik also determines whether your bookshelf bracket will hold.

---

### Newton's third law: you can't push without being pushed
*BB 641 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Lean against a wall. You push the wall, and the wall pushes you back with exactly the same force — that's why you don't fall through. Jump off a boat onto a dock, and the boat slides backward. Every force is part of a pair: A pushes B, B pushes A, equal in magnitude, opposite in direction. There are no solo forces in the universe.

**Concrete.** You stand on a bathroom scale. Your weight (686 N for a 70 kg person) pushes down on the scale. The scale pushes up on you with 686 N — that's the reading. The forces are equal and opposite, but they act on *different objects* (you on scale, scale on you), so they don't cancel in a free-body diagram of either object alone.

A rifle fires a 10 g bullet at 800 m/s. The bullet's momentum: p = 0.010 × 800 = 8.0 kg·m/s. The rifle (mass 4.0 kg) recoils with equal and opposite momentum: v_rifle = 8.0/4.0 = 2.0 m/s. The force on your shoulder: if the recoil is absorbed over 0.05 m, F = Δp/Δt. Δt ≈ 0.05/2.0 = 0.025 s, so F ≈ 8.0/0.025 = 320 N — a solid shove. The bullet and rifle experience equal and opposite forces for the same duration.

**Definition.** **Newton's Third Law:** When object A exerts a force on object B, object B simultaneously exerts a force on object A equal in magnitude and opposite in direction: F⃗_AB = −F⃗_BA. The forces act on *different* objects — they do not cancel. Common misconception: "If forces are equal, why does the bullet accelerate more than the rifle?" Because a = F/m, and the bullet's mass is 400× smaller. Same force, vastly different acceleration. The third law applies to all forces: contact (normal, friction, tension) and field (gravity, electric, magnetic). It is the foundation of conservation of momentum and the principle of rocket propulsion.

**In action.** Rockets are pure Newton's third law. The engine expels exhaust gases backward at high speed; the gases push the rocket forward with equal and opposite force. In the vacuum of space, there's nothing to push against — the rocket pushes against its own exhaust. A helicopter pushes air down; the air pushes the helicopter up. A squid jets through the ocean by expelling water. You walk by pushing the Earth backward with your foot — the Earth pushes you forward. The Earth does accelerate backward (F = ma), but its mass is 6 × 10²⁴ kg, so the acceleration is unmeasurably tiny. Every step you take literally moves the planet.

---

### Force, work, and power: the energy trilogy
*BB 642 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Force is a push or pull. Work is what happens when that push moves something: force × distance. Power is how fast that work gets done: work ÷ time. These three concepts — force, work, power — are the vocabulary of every machine ever built. A bulldozer exerts enormous force; the work it does is force × the distance it pushes the dirt; the power of its engine determines how quickly it does that work.

**Concrete.** A crane lifts a 2000 kg steel beam 30 m vertically in 15 seconds. The force needed: F = mg = 2000 × 9.8 = 19,600 N (the weight). Work done: W = Fd = 19,600 × 30 = 588,000 J = 588 kJ. Power output: P = W/t = 588,000/15 = 39,200 W = 39.2 kW (about 53 horsepower).

If the crane's motor is 80% efficient, the electrical power input is P_in = P_out/η = 39.2/0.80 = 49.0 kW. The wasted 9.8 kW becomes heat in the motor and gearbox.

Units: 1 joule (J) = 1 newton × 1 metre. 1 watt (W) = 1 J/s. 1 kilowatt-hour (kWh) = 3.6 × 10⁶ J. 1 horsepower (hp) = 746 W (approximately). An average human can sustain about 75 W of mechanical output — roughly 0.1 horsepower.

**Definition.** **Force** F is an interaction that changes an object's motion (N). **Work** W = Fd cos θ — the component of force parallel to displacement times displacement (J). Work is done only if there is displacement; holding a heavy box stationary does zero work (even though it's exhausting — your muscles are doing internal biological work). **Energy** is the capacity to do work. **Power** P = W/t = Fv (for constant force parallel to velocity). **Efficiency** η = useful work output / total energy input × 100%. The **principle of conservation of energy:** energy cannot be created or destroyed, only converted between forms.

**In action.** Power ratings determine cost. A 2 kW electric heater running for 5 hours consumes 10 kWh — at ₹8/kWh, that's ₹80. A 10 W LED bulb running for the same time costs ₹0.40. Electric vehicles are rated in kWh/100 km — a Tesla Model 3 uses about 15 kWh/100 km, costing roughly ₹120 for 100 km (at ₹8/kWh), compared to ₹600–800 for a petrol car. The difference is efficiency: electric motors are ~90% efficient; petrol engines are ~25%. The energy trilogy — force, work, power — is the accountant's ledger of the physical world.

---

### Kinetic and potential energy: the two bank accounts
*BB 643 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Energy comes in two fundamental mechanical forms: kinetic (the energy of motion) and potential (the energy of position). A swinging pendulum constantly converts one into the other — at the bottom, all kinetic; at the top, all potential. The total stays constant (ignoring friction). This is conservation of mechanical energy: the universe has a fixed energy balance, and energy moves between accounts but never disappears.

**Concrete.** A 0.50 kg mass swings on a 1.0 m string, released from a height 0.20 m above the lowest point. At release: v = 0, KE = 0. PE_grav = mgh = 0.50 × 9.8 × 0.20 = 0.98 J. Total mechanical energy E = 0.98 J. At the lowest point: all energy is kinetic. ½mv² = 0.98 → v = √(2 × 0.98/0.50) = √3.92 = 1.98 m/s. At any intermediate point, KE + PE = 0.98 J — the sum is invariant.

A 1000 kg car travelling at 20 m/s (72 km/h) has KE = ½mv² = ½ × 1000 × 400 = 200,000 J = 200 kJ. This is the energy the brakes must dissipate to stop. At 40 m/s (144 km/h): KE = 800 kJ — four times the energy for twice the speed. This is why high-speed crashes are so much more destructive: energy scales with v².

**Definition.** **Kinetic energy:** KE = ½mv² (J). Energy of motion. **Gravitational potential energy:** PE = mgh (near Earth's surface). Energy of position in a gravitational field. **Elastic potential energy:** PE_spring = ½kx². **Conservation of mechanical energy:** in the absence of non-conservative forces (friction, air resistance), KE + PE = constant. **Work-energy theorem:** W_net = ΔKE — the net work done on an object equals its change in kinetic energy. **Energy** is a scalar (no direction), measured in joules. 1 J = 1 kg·m²/s².

**In action.** Energy conservation is the universal accountant. A hydropower dam converts gravitational PE of water into KE of turbines into electrical energy. A roller coaster's first climb stores PE (the lift hill); the rest of the ride converts it to KE and back, with friction slowly draining the total. A regenerative braking system in an EV captures KE during deceleration and stores it back in the battery — reversing the usual waste. The v² in KE explains why speed limits save lives: a crash at 50 km/h has 56% more energy than at 40 km/h, not 25%. Energy is never lost — it just moves somewhere you didn't intend, usually as heat.

---

### Vectors: the arrow language of physics
*BB 644 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Some quantities are just numbers: mass (5 kg), temperature (25°C), time (10 s). These are scalars. Others have direction too: velocity (50 km/h north), force (100 N downward), displacement (3 m east). These are vectors. You can't add vectors like ordinary numbers — 3 N east + 4 N north is not 7 N. It's 5 N northeast. Vectors need their own arithmetic.

**Concrete.** You walk 3.0 km east, then 4.0 km north. Your displacement (straight-line distance from start to finish) is not 7 km. Draw a triangle: the east leg is 3, the north leg is 4. The hypotenuse (resultant) is √(3² + 4²) = 5.0 km, at an angle tan⁻¹(4/3) = 53° north of east. This is the **triangle method** of vector addition: place the tail of the second vector at the head of the first, and draw the resultant from start to finish.

Alternatively, the **parallelogram method:** draw both vectors from the same starting point, complete the parallelogram, and the diagonal from the start is the resultant. Both methods give the same answer. For more than two vectors: chain them head-to-tail.

**Definition.** **Scalars** have magnitude only (mass, time, temperature, energy). **Vectors** have magnitude and direction (displacement, velocity, acceleration, force, momentum). **Vector addition:** R⃗ = A⃗ + B⃗. Methods: triangle (tip-to-tail), parallelogram, or **resolution into components** (Aₓ = A cos θ, A_y = A sin θ). Then Rₓ = ΣAₓ, R_y = ΣA_y, R = √(Rₓ² + R_y²), θ = tan⁻¹(R_y/Rₓ). **Subtraction:** A⃗ − B⃗ = A⃗ + (−B⃗) — reverse B and add. **Scalar multiplication:** cA⃗ = vector with magnitude |c|A in the same (c > 0) or opposite (c < 0) direction. Vectors are the language of forces, velocities, and fields — you cannot do physics without them.

**In action.** Vectors resolve every directional problem. A plane flying at 500 km/h north into a 100 km/h east wind: the ground track is √(500² + (−100)² if wind is tailwind? Actually: plane's velocity relative to air is 500 N; wind velocity is 100 E. Resultant: √(500² + 100²) = 510 km/h at tan⁻¹(100/500) = 11.3° east of north. The pilot must aim slightly west of north to fly a true north track. A cable-stayed bridge: the tension in each cable is a vector — the vertical components support the deck, the horizontal components cancel. Every truss, every frame, every free-body diagram is vector addition. If you can't add vectors, you can't design a structure that stands up.

---

### Adding forces: the resultant
*BB 645 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A chandelier hangs from two chains. Each chain pulls at an angle. The chandelier doesn't move, so the net force is zero — the upward components of both chains exactly balance the weight. Finding that balance means resolving each force into components and adding them as vectors. The resultant is the single force that replaces all the individual forces and has the same effect.

**Concrete.** A 200 N sign hangs from two cables making 30° with the horizontal. Each cable's tension T pulls up and sideways. Vertical equilibrium: 2 × T sin 30° = 200 → 2T × 0.5 = 200 → T = 200 N. Each cable pulls with 200 N — the combined vertical component (200 N) balances the weight. The horizontal components (T cos 30° = 173 N each) pull in opposite directions and cancel.

Three people pull on a ring: 50 N east, 80 N north, 60 N at 45° south of west. Resolve: (50, 0) + (0, 80) + (−60 cos 45°, −60 sin 45°) = (50 − 42.4, 80 − 42.4) = (7.6, 37.6). Resultant = √(7.6² + 37.6²) = 38.4 N at tan⁻¹(37.6/7.6) = 78.6° north of east. A single 38.4 N force at 78.6° would replace all three.

**Definition.** The **resultant** of a system of forces is the single force that produces the same effect as all the individual forces acting together. **Equilibrium** exists when the resultant is zero: ΣF⃗ = 0. For a particle in 2D: ΣFₓ = 0 and ΣF_y = 0. Procedure: (1) Draw a free-body diagram. (2) Resolve all forces into x and y components. (3) Sum each column. (4) Resultant magnitude R = √(Rₓ² + R_y²), direction θ = tan⁻¹(R_y/Rₓ). **Tip:** choose coordinate axes to minimise the number of components — align one axis with the most complicated force or with the direction of motion.

**In action.** Force resolution is how every structure stays up. A crane's jib is in compression, its tie cable in tension — the forces must balance at every joint. A suspension bridge's main cable sags in a catenary curve; at each tower, the horizontal component of cable tension is constant, while the vertical component carries the weight of the deck. A rock climber hanging from two anchors uses a "load-distributing" or "equalised" anchor — the angle between the slings determines the force on each anchor. At 30°, each anchor sees ~52% of the load. At 120°, each anchor sees 100% — an American Death Triangle can multiply force catastrophically. Vector addition is not abstract — it's the difference between a safe anchor and a snapped rope.

---

### Moment of force: why wrenches have long handles
*BB 646 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Push on a door near the hinge and it barely moves. Push at the edge and it swings easily. The force is the same — the distance from the pivot makes the difference. A moment (or torque) is force × perpendicular distance from the pivot. The longer the lever arm, the greater the turning effect for the same force. This is why wrenches have long handles.

**Concrete.** A bolt requires 50 N·m of torque to tighten. With a 0.30 m wrench, the force needed: F = τ/d = 50/0.30 = 167 N (about 17 kg). With a 0.50 m wrench: F = 50/0.50 = 100 N. The longer handle saves 40% of the effort. A "cheater bar" (a pipe slipped over the wrench handle) extends the lever arm — 1.0 m needs only 50 N, easily applied with one hand. The danger: the bolt or wrench may snap because the torque exceeds the material strength.

A 3.0 m seesaw balances a 30 kg child on one end and a 45 kg adult on the other. For balance: the moments must be equal. 30g × d_child = 45g × d_adult. If d_adult = 1.0 m from the pivot, d_child = 45 × 1.0/30 = 1.5 m. The lighter child sits farther from the pivot.

**Definition.** A **moment** (torque) τ is the turning effect of a force: τ = Fd sin θ, where d is the distance from the pivot to the line of action of the force, and θ is the angle between the force and the lever arm. Units: N·m (not joules — torque is not energy).
