The **lever arm** (moment arm) is the perpendicular distance from the pivot to the line of action. **Rotational equilibrium:** Στ = 0 — the sum of clockwise and anticlockwise moments about any point is zero. **Principle of moments:** for a body in equilibrium, total clockwise moment = total anticlockwise moment. The moment of a couple (two equal, opposite, parallel forces) is Fd, independent of the pivot choice.

**In action.** Moments are why all tools are designed the way they are. A long screwdriver handle gives more torque. A ratchet with an extendable handle is a variable-torque tool. A cyclist's pedal crank (typically 170–175 mm) is a lever — standing on the pedal applies your full weight at that radius, generating torque to climb hills. A torque wrench clicks at a preset value — overtightening strips threads; undertightening risks failure. Centre of gravity is where the entire weight of an object can be considered to act — if the CG is outside the base of support, the object tips. A leaning tower topples when its CG moves beyond its base. The moment is the rotational analogue of force — and just as universal.

---

### Centre of gravity: where the weight lives
*BB 647 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Balance a ruler on your finger. The point where it doesn't tip is its centre of gravity — the single point where the entire weight of the object can be considered to act. For symmetrical objects, it's at the geometric centre. For irregular ones, it's where the weight is evenly distributed. An object tips over when its CG moves outside its base of support.

**Concrete.** A uniform 5.0 m plank of mass 20 kg rests on two supports, one at each end. A 60 kg person stands 1.5 m from the left end. Find the reaction forces at the supports. Take moments about the left support: clockwise moment = (60g × 1.5) + (20g × 2.5) = 900g + 500g = 1400g N·m. This must equal the anticlockwise moment = R_right × 5.0. R_right = 1400g/5.0 = 280g = 2744 N. R_left = (80g) − 2744 = 784 − 2744 = 5096 N? Actually weight = 80 × 9.8 = 784 N. Wait — let me recalculate. 20 kg plank + 60 kg person = 80 kg total, total weight 784 N. R_right = (60 × 9.8 × 1.5 + 20 × 9.8 × 2.5)/5.0 = (882 + 490)/5 = 274.4 N. R_left = 784 − 274.4 = 509.6 N. The left support carries ~65% of the load.

**Definition.** The **centre of gravity** (CG) of an object is the point where the entire weight can be considered to act. For a uniform gravitational field, CG = centre of mass (COM). The CG is found by: (1) symmetry — at the geometric centre for uniform symmetric objects, (2) calculation — x_CG = Σ(m_i x_i)/Σm_i for discrete masses, (3) experiment — suspend the object from two different points; the CG lies at the intersection of the vertical lines. **Stability:** an object is stable if its CG is within its base of support. The **stability margin** is the distance the CG must move to reach the edge. A lower CG and wider base increase stability.

**In action.** The CG determines whether a vehicle rolls over. An SUV has a higher CG than a sedan — in a sharp turn, the centrifugal force effectively shifts the CG outward, and if it moves beyond the wheels, the vehicle flips. Double-decker buses have a low CG (heavy engine and chassis at the bottom) despite their height, keeping them stable. A forklift carries its load low and close to the mast. In construction, a crane's load chart shows the maximum safe load at each radius — beyond that, the CG of the crane-load system shifts beyond the outriggers, and the crane tips. The Leaning Tower of Pisa (CG about 2.3 m from centre) has been stabilised by adding counterweights and extracting soil — keeping its CG inside its base for a few more centuries.

---

### Acceleration: how speed changes
*BB 648 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Speed tells you how fast you're going. Acceleration tells you how quickly that speed is changing. If you go from 0 to 100 km/h in 8 seconds, your acceleration is about 3.5 m/s² — every second, your speed increases by 3.5 m/s. Acceleration can be positive (speeding up), negative (slowing down — deceleration), or zero (cruising at constant speed).

**Concrete.** A car accelerates uniformly from rest to 25 m/s (90 km/h) in 10 seconds. a = Δv/Δt = 25/10 = 2.5 m/s². Distance travelled: s = v_avg × t = (0 + 25)/2 × 10 = 125 m. Using s = ut + ½at²: s = 0 + ½(2.5)(100) = 125 m ✓. The velocity-time graph is a straight line from (0,0) to (10,25); the area under it (triangle) = ½ × 10 × 25 = 125 m — the displacement.

Graphical interpretation: on a v-t graph, the slope = acceleration (steeper = faster acceleration). On an s-t graph, the slope = velocity (steeper = faster). A curved s-t graph means changing velocity — accelerating or decelerating. A straight s-t line means constant velocity.

**Definition.** **Acceleration** a = Δv/Δt (m/s²). **Uniform acceleration** means a is constant. The **kinematic equations** for constant a: (1) v = u + at, (2) s = ut + ½at², (3) v² = u² + 2as, (4) s = ½(u+v)t. These are the SUVAT equations (u = initial velocity, v = final, a = acceleration, s = displacement, t = time). They work only when a is constant. **Graphical:** a-t graph is a horizontal line; v-t graph is a straight line with slope a; s-t graph is a parabola (curved). **Average velocity:** v_avg = (u+v)/2 (only for constant a). **Instantaneous velocity:** the slope of the tangent to the s-t curve. **Acceleration due to gravity:** g = 9.8 m/s² downward (near Earth's surface).

**In action.** Acceleration numbers define vehicle performance. A family car: 0–100 km/h in ~10 s (a ≈ 2.8 m/s², ~0.3g). A sports car: ~4 s (a ≈ 7 m/s², ~0.7g). A Formula 1 car: ~2.5 s (a ≈ 11 m/s², ~1.1g). A top-fuel dragster: 0–530 km/h in 3.7 s (a ≈ 40 m/s², ~4g). Fighter pilots in hard turns experience 7–9g — at 9g, a 70 kg pilot weighs 630 kg equivalent; blood drains from the brain, and a G-suit inflates to push it back. The human tolerance limit is about 10g sustained; John Stapp survived 46g for 1.1 seconds on a rocket sled in 1954, proving seatbelts save lives. Acceleration is not just a number — it's the physical stress your body endures.

---

### Projectiles: the path of a cannonball
*BB 649 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Fire a cannonball at an angle. It curves up, slows, stops rising, and curves down — tracing a parabola. The magic of projectile motion: the horizontal and vertical motions are completely independent. Gravity pulls down; nothing pushes horizontally (ignoring air). So the horizontal speed stays constant while the vertical speed changes exactly like free fall. Combine them and you get that graceful arc.

**Concrete.** A projectile is launched at 50 m/s at 40° above horizontal. Initial components: v₀x = 50 cos 40° = 38.3 m/s, v₀y = 50 sin 40° = 32.1 m/s. Time to reach peak (v_y = 0): t_peak = v₀y/g = 32.1/9.8 = 3.28 s. Maximum height: h_max = v₀y²/(2g) = 32.1²/(19.6) = 52.6 m. Total flight time: t_total = 2t_peak = 6.55 s. Range: R = v₀x × t_total = 38.3 × 6.55 = 251 m.

Maximum range occurs at 45° (on level ground, no air resistance). At 30° or 60°, range is the same — they are complementary angles. R_max = v₀²/g = 50²/9.8 = 255 m. With air resistance, the optimum is lower — about 30–35° for a rifle bullet.

**Definition.** **Projectile motion** under uniform gravity (no air resistance): horizontal motion is constant velocity (a_x = 0, v_x = v₀x = constant, x = v₀x t). Vertical motion is constant acceleration (a_y = −g, v_y = v₀y − gt, y = v₀y t − ½gt²). The trajectory is a parabola: y = x tan θ − gx²/(2v₀² cos²θ). **Range:** R = v₀² sin(2θ)/g. **Maximum height:** H = v₀² sin²θ/(2g). **Time of flight:** T = 2v₀ sin θ/g. The independence of horizontal and vertical motions is Galileo's principle of compound motion.

**In action.** Projectile calculations design artillery, sports, and fireworks. A cricket ball thrown from the boundary at 30° and 25 m/s travels about 55 m — a direct hit. A goalkeeper's punt at 45° and 20 m/s reaches ~41 m — half the pitch. A golf drive at 70 m/s and 12° (optimised for backspin lift) carries ~270 m. In military ballistics, a 155 mm howitzer shell at 800 m/s and 45° would theoretically reach 65 km — in practice, air resistance limits it to ~30 km. Firework shells burst at the peak of their trajectory, calculated to explode exactly at apogee for maximum visual spread. The projectile equations are exact only in a vacuum — the real world adds drag, wind, and spin, but the parabola is the starting point for every trajectory.

---

### Centripetal force: the inward pull that keeps things turning
*BB 650 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Tie a stone to a string and whirl it in a circle. The stone wants to fly off in a straight line — Newton's first law. The string pulls it inward, constantly changing its direction. This inward pull is centripetal force: F = mv²/r. The faster you whirl, the harder the string must pull. Let go, and the stone flies off *tangent* to the circle — not outward, but straight ahead from the point of release.

**Concrete.** A 0.50 kg stone on a 1.0 m string is whirled at 3.0 revolutions per second. Angular velocity ω = 2πf = 2π × 3.0 = 18.85 rad/s. Linear speed v = ωr = 18.85 × 1.0 = 18.85 m/s. Centripetal force: F = mv²/r = 0.50 × 18.85²/1.0 = 0.50 × 355.3 = 177.7 N — about 18 kg of tension. Double the speed to 6 rev/s: v doubles, v² quadruples, F = 710 N — a force that would snap most strings.

A 1200 kg car rounds a curve of radius 50 m at 20 m/s (72 km/h). Required centripetal force: F = mv²/r = 1200 × 400/50 = 9600 N. This force comes from friction between tyres and road. Maximum friction: f_max = μ_s N = μ_s mg. If μ_s = 0.80 on dry asphalt: f_max = 0.80 × 1200 × 9.8 = 9408 N — barely enough. On wet asphalt (μ_s ≈ 0.50): f_max = 5880 N — the car slides off the road at this speed.

**Definition.** **Centripetal force** is the net force directed toward the centre of a circular path, required to keep an object moving in that circle: F_c = mv²/r = mω²r. It is not a new type of force — it is provided by tension (string), gravity (orbit), friction (car tyre), or normal force (banked curve). **Centripetal acceleration:** a_c = v²/r = ω²r, always directed toward the centre. **Centrifugal force** is a fictitious force experienced in the rotating frame — the "outward push" you feel in a turning car is your body's inertia resisting the centripetal acceleration, not a real force. **Banking angle:** tan θ = v²/(rg) — the ideal angle at which no friction is needed.

**In action.** Every curve you drive is a centripetal-force problem. Highway off-ramps are banked to reduce reliance on friction. A velodrome's banked turns (up to 45°) let cyclists corner at high speed without sliding. Washing machines use centripetal force to spin water out of clothes — the drum walls provide the force on the clothes, but the water escapes through holes and flies off tangentially. A centrifuge separates blood components: the denser red cells experience greater centripetal force requirements and migrate outward. At 3000 RPM in a 10 cm radius centrifuge, the acceleration is ~10,000g — a 1 g sample effectively weighs 10 kg.

---

### Newton's second law in engineering: slugs, pounds, and poundals
*BB 651 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** In SI, it's simple: force in newtons, mass in kilograms, acceleration in m/s². In the old British engineering system, it's a mess: there are pounds-force (lbf), pounds-mass (lbm), slugs, and poundals. A pound-force is the weight of a pound-mass at standard gravity. But F = ma only works directly if you use slugs for mass or poundals for force. Engineers in the US and UK used these units for centuries — and some still do.

**Concrete.** The British gravitational system: the unit of force is the pound-force (lbf), defined as the weight of a standard pound-mass at g = 32.174 ft/s². The unit of mass is the SLUG: 1 slug = 1 lbf·s²/ft. A mass of 1 slug accelerates at 1 ft/s² under 1 lbf. A 150 lb person has a mass of 150/32.174 ≈ 4.66 slugs. In F = ma: F (lbf) = m (slugs) × a (ft/s²).

The British absolute system: the unit of mass is the pound-mass (lbm). The unit of force is the POUNDAL: 1 pdl = 1 lbm·ft/s². 1 lbf = 32.174 pdl. Conversions: 1 lbf ≈ 4.448 N. 1 slug ≈ 14.59 kg. 1 lbm ≈ 0.4536 kg. 1 ft/s² = 0.3048 m/s².

**Definition.** Three unit systems for mechanics:

• **SI:** mass (kg), force (N = kg·m/s²). g = 9.8 m/s². Clean — no conversion factor.

• **British gravitational:** force (lbf), mass (slug = lbf·s²/ft). F (lbf) = m (slug) × a (ft/s²). Confusion: "pound" means both force and mass in everyday speech.

• **British absolute:** mass (lbm), force (poundal = lbm·ft/s²). 1 lbf = g_c × 1 lbm, where g_c = 32.174 lbm·ft/lbf·s². Many US engineering texts write F = ma/g_c to handle mixed units.

The key: always check whether "pound" means force or mass. If it's a weight, it's force. If it's a quantity of material, it's mass.

**In action.** The unit confusion has real consequences. The Mars Climate Orbiter disaster: Lockheed Martin used lbf·s for thruster impulses; NASA's JPL used N·s. The conversion factor of 4.448 was missing from the software. Each thruster firing was 4.448× too small, and the spacecraft slowly drifted off course over months. The $327 million probe burned up. In aviation, fuel is measured in pounds (force — it's the weight of fuel, not its mass, because lift must overcome weight). Pilots calculate weight and balance in pounds. An aircraft's maximum takeoff weight is a force limit, not a mass limit — but the two are interchangeable at 1g. Knowing which unit system you're in is not pedantry — it's the difference between orbit and oblivion.

---

### Conservation of momentum: the crash investigator's tool
*BB 652 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** When two objects collide — cars, billiard balls, atoms — the total momentum before the collision equals the total momentum after, provided no external forces act. This is conservation of momentum, a direct consequence of Newton's third law. It's the single most powerful tool for analysing crashes, explosions, and any interaction where forces are brief and intense.

**Concrete.** A 1500 kg car travelling at 20 m/s (72 km/h) rear-ends a stationary 1000 kg car. They lock together (perfectly inelastic collision). Momentum before: p_before = 1500 × 20 + 1000 × 0 = 30,000 kg·m/s. After: combined mass 2500 kg moves at v. 30,000 = 2500v → v = 12 m/s (43 km/h). The collision dissipated kinetic energy: KE_before = ½(1500)(400) = 300 kJ. KE_after = ½(2500)(144) = 180 kJ. Lost: 120 kJ — went into crumpling metal, heat, and sound.

A 50 g bullet at 400 m/s embeds in a 2.0 kg wooden block at rest on a frictionless surface. Momentum: 0.050 × 400 = 20 kg·m/s. After: (2.05)v = 20 → v = 9.76 m/s. The block slides away at ~10 m/s. This is a ballistic pendulum — measuring v tells you the bullet's speed.

**Definition.** **Momentum** p⃗ = mv⃗ (kg·m/s). **Conservation of momentum:** in the absence of external forces, total momentum is constant — Σp⃗_before = Σp⃗_after. **Impulse** J = F_avg Δt = Δp — the change in momentum equals the area under the force-time curve. **Elastic collision:** both momentum and kinetic energy conserved. **Inelastic collision:** momentum conserved, KE not conserved (converted to heat, sound, deformation). **Perfectly inelastic:** objects stick together after collision. The law applies in all directions independently: Σp_x,before = Σp_x,after, Σp_y,before = Σp_y,after.

**In action.** Conservation of momentum reconstructs accidents. A car skids into a wall: measure the skid marks to find pre-impact speed. A two-car collision: the post-impact trajectories (from road markings, CCTV, dashcam) give velocities; work backward to find who was speeding. In particle physics, momentum conservation reveals invisible particles — a neutrino is detected by the missing momentum in a decay. Rocket propulsion: the rocket's forward momentum equals the exhaust's backward momentum. A 500-tonne Saturn V at liftoff expelled 15 tonnes of exhaust per second at 2500 m/s, generating 37.5 MN of thrust. Every action has an equal and opposite reaction — that's conservation of momentum, not just a slogan.

---

### The Newtonian mechanics toolkit
*BB 653 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** After 15 BBs of Newtonian mechanics through the Frye lens, here is the unified picture. Classical mechanics rests on three laws, two conservation principles (energy and momentum), and one universal force (gravity). Every machine, every structure, every vehicle is an application of these few principles. The equations are simple; the skill is knowing which one to use.

**Concrete.** Identify the principle for each scenario:

1. Seatbelt stops you in a crash — Newton I (inertia) + Newton II (F = ma, the belt force).

2. Rocket launch — Newton III (action-reaction) + conservation of momentum.

3. Crane lifting a beam — Work = Fd, Power = W/t, Efficiency = P_out/P_in.

4. Pendulum swing — KE ↔ PE conversion, conservation of mechanical energy.

5. Boat crossing a river — vector addition (boat velocity + current velocity).

6. Tightening a bolt — torque τ = Fd, lever arm principle.

7. Car rounding a curve — centripetal force F = mv²/r, friction provides it.

8. Cannonball trajectory — projectile motion, x = v₀ₓt, y = v₀yt − ½gt².

**Definition.** **Newtonian mechanics master principles:**

• **Newton I:** ΣF = 0 ⇒ v = constant. Inertial frames.

• **Newton II:** F = ma = dp/dt. N = kg·m/s². Engineering: lbf = slug × ft/s².

• **Newton III:** F_AB = −F_BA. Action-reaction pairs on different objects.

• **Work-Energy:** W = Fd cos θ, KE = ½mv², PE = mgh, P = W/t. Conservation: KE + PE = constant (no friction).

• **Momentum:** p = mv, Σp_before = Σp_after. Impulse J = Δp = F_avg Δt.

• **Kinematics:** v = u + at, s = ut + ½at², v² = u² + 2as. Projectile: x = v₀ₓt, y = v₀yt − ½gt².

• **Vectors:** R = √(Rₓ² + R_y²). Add tip-to-tail. Resolve into components.

• **Rotation:** τ = Fd, Στ = 0 for equilibrium. CG: point where weight acts.

**In action.** This is the operating system of the visible world. Everything from a falling leaf to a skyscraper obeys these laws. The beauty is that the same equations predict a cricket ball's trajectory, a satellite's orbit, and a bridge's deflection. The numbers change — the principles don't. Frye's contribution is to show that these are not abstract theorems to be admired from a distance. They are tools — as practical and reliable as a spanner. Learn to reach for the right one, and you can analyse any mechanical system you encounter.

---

### Static friction: the force that holds things still
*BB 120 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Push gently on a heavy box and it doesn't budge. Push harder — still nothing. Push hard enough and suddenly it lurches forward. The force holding it still is static friction. It doesn't have one fixed value — it grows to match your push, up to a hard limit. Push past that limit and the box moves.

**Concrete.** A 10 kg block sits on a horizontal table. The maximum static friction before it slides is measured at 49 N. That means the coefficient of static friction μₛ = 49 / (10 × 9.8) = 0.50. Push with 10 N and friction pushes back with exactly 10 N — the block stays still. Push with 30 N and friction pushes back with 30 N. Push with 50 N and friction can only return 49 N — the net force is 1 N, and the block begins to slide.

**Definition.** **Static friction** fₛ is the tangential contact force that prevents two surfaces from sliding relative to each other. It is a self-adjusting force: 0 ≤ fₛ ≤ fₛ_max. The maximum value is **limiting friction**: fₛ_max = μₛ N, where μₛ is the **coefficient of static friction** (dimensionless, depends only on the two surfaces) and N is the normal force. Static friction acts parallel to the contact surface, opposing the attempted relative motion.

**In action.** Static friction is what lets you walk — your foot pushes backward on the ground, and static friction pushes you forward. It's what keeps a car's tyres gripping the road instead of spinning. It's why a ladder leaning against a wall doesn't slide down. Without static friction, nothing would stay in place — every object on a slope would slide, every screw would loosen, and walking would be impossible. The limiting friction formula tells you the steepest slope, the tightest turn, and the hardest push before things give way.

---

### Kinetic friction: when sliding takes over
*BB 121 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Once a box starts sliding, the friction doesn't disappear — it just gets weaker. The force opposing the slide, kinetic friction, is usually less than the maximum static friction that was holding it still. This is why it's harder to get something moving than to keep it moving.

**Concrete.** The same 10 kg block from before, once sliding, experiences a kinetic friction of about 39 N (using μₖ = 0.40, which is less than μₛ = 0.50). While static friction could hold back up to 49 N, kinetic friction only pushes back with 39 N regardless of speed — it doesn't grow or shrink. Push with 50 N while it's sliding, and the net force is 50 − 39 = 11 N forward — the block accelerates. The friction is a constant drag, not a self-adjusting opponent.

**Definition.** **Kinetic friction** (or sliding friction) fₖ is the tangential contact force that opposes the relative motion of two surfaces that are already sliding. Unlike static friction, it is approximately constant: fₖ = μₖ N, where μₖ is the **coefficient of kinetic friction**. For most material pairs, μₖ < μₛ — it takes less force to keep something sliding than to start it sliding. Kinetic friction is independent of speed (for moderate speeds) and independent of contact area.

**In action.** The difference between static and kinetic friction is why anti-lock braking systems (ABS) exist. When a wheel locks up and skids, the friction drops from static to kinetic — the car slides further. ABS pulses the brakes, keeping the tyre at the edge of static friction without crossing into a skid. The same principle explains why a cricket fielder drags their foot to stop a rolling ball rather than stomping on it: static friction (rolling without slipping) is stronger than kinetic (skidding).

---

### The angle of repose: when friction balances gravity
*BB 122 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Tip a plank slowly upward with a block sitting on it. At first the block stays put — static friction holds it. At some critical angle, the block suddenly slides. That angle is the angle of repose, and it is determined entirely by the coefficient of static friction.

**Concrete.** A block rests on a plank inclined at angle θ. The component of weight pulling it down the slope is mg sin θ. Static friction pushes up the slope, with maximum value μₛ mg cos θ. The block slides when mg sin θ exceeds μₛ mg cos θ — that is, when tan θ > μₛ. For μₛ = 0.50, the critical angle is tan⁻¹(0.50) ≈ 26.6°. Tilt beyond 26.6° and the block goes.

**Definition.** The **angle of repose** θᵣ is the steepest angle of an inclined plane at which an object remains at rest without sliding. It satisfies tan θᵣ = μₛ. At this angle, the downhill component of weight exactly equals the maximum static friction. The angle of repose is independent of the object's mass — a pebble and a boulder with the same μₛ will both start sliding at the same tilt angle.

**In action.** The angle of repose is why sand piles form cones with a fixed slope — about 30–35° for dry sand. Pour more sand on top and it avalanches down, maintaining the same angle. In civil engineering, the angle of repose determines the steepest stable slope for road cuttings, embankments, and stockpiles of grain or gravel. A slope steeper than the angle of repose will collapse — nature enforces the friction limit relentlessly.

---

### Angular displacement and angular velocity
*BB 123 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** When an object moves in a circle, its position isn't described by x and y — it's described by an angle. As it turns, the angle changes. How fast that angle changes is the angular velocity — the spinning analogue of ordinary speed.

**Concrete.** A fan blade completes one full rotation in 0.2 seconds. That's 2π radians in 0.2 s, so the angular velocity ω = 2π / 0.2 = 10π ≈ 31.4 rad/s. A point on the blade 0.3 m from the centre traces a circle of circumference 2π × 0.3 ≈ 1.88 m in the same 0.2 s — a linear speed of v = 1.88 / 0.2 = 9.42 m/s. And indeed v = ωr = 31.4 × 0.3 = 9.42 m/s. Angular velocity and linear speed are linked by the radius.

**Definition.** **Angular displacement** Δθ is the angle swept by a radius vector in a given time interval, measured in radians. **Angular velocity** ω = lim(Δt→0) Δθ/Δt = dθ/dt, measured in rad/s. It is a vector (actually a pseudovector) pointing along the axis of rotation per the right-hand rule. The linear speed v of a point at distance r from the axis is v = ωr. One full revolution = 2π radians; the time for one revolution is the **period** T, and ω = 2π/T = 2πf, where f is the frequency.

**In action.** A car's speedometer and tachometer measure two different things: the speedometer reads v (linear speed of the car), the tachometer reads ω (angular speed of the engine crankshaft). The gearbox converts one to the other. In a CD player, the disc spins at a varying ω so that v = ωr stays constant at the laser pickup — faster near the centre, slower at the edge. Every rotating machine, from a hard drive to a wind turbine, is designed around the relationship v = ωr.

---

### Centripetal acceleration: why turning is accelerating
*BB 124 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Drive around a curve at constant speed. Your speedometer doesn't budge, but you're pushed against the door. You're accelerating — not because your speed is changing, but because your direction is. Circular motion is accelerated motion, even when the speed is perfectly steady.

**Concrete.** A particle moves at constant speed v around a circle of radius r. At one instant it's at the top, velocity v⃗₁ pointing right. A moment later at the right edge, velocity v⃗₂ points down. Both have the same length v, but different directions. The change Δv⃗ = v⃗₂ − v⃗₁ points toward the centre. The acceleration a = Δv/Δt also points toward the centre, and its magnitude is v²/r. For a car at 20 m/s around a 50 m radius curve, a = 400/50 = 8 m/s² — nearly 1 g of sideways push.

**Definition.** **Centripetal acceleration** a_c is the acceleration of an object moving at constant speed in a circle. Its magnitude is a_c = v²/r = ω²r, and it always points toward the centre of the circle — perpendicular to the velocity. It is not a separate force; it is the acceleration required for circular motion. Any object moving in a circle, at any speed, has this acceleration. If the speed also changes, there is an additional tangential acceleration a_t = dv/dt.

**In action.** The centripetal acceleration of the Earth around the Sun is tiny: v ≈ 30,000 m/s, r ≈ 1.5 × 10¹¹ m, so a_c ≈ (3 × 10⁴)² / (1.5 × 10¹¹) ≈ 0.006 m/s² — barely perceptible. A washing machine spinning at 1200 rpm with a drum radius of 0.25 m: ω = 1200 × 2π/60 ≈ 126 rad/s, so a_c = (126)² × 0.25 ≈ 4,000 m/s² — about 400 g. The clothes are pressed against the drum with 400 times their own weight. That's why they dry — the water is flung out through the holes.

---

### Banking of roads: using the normal force to turn
*BB 126 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A flat road relies entirely on friction to turn a car. On a wet or icy day, that friction may not be enough. The solution: tilt the road, so the normal force itself provides part of the centripetal force. This is banking — and at one special speed, no friction is needed at all.

**Concrete.** A curve of radius 100 m is banked at 15°. At the optimum speed, the horizontal component of the normal force alone provides the centripetal force — the car could turn on glare ice. Resolving: N sin θ = mv²/r horizontally, and N cos θ = mg vertically. Dividing: tan θ = v²/(rg). So v = √(rg tan θ) = √(100 × 9.8 × tan 15°) ≈ √(100 × 9.8 × 0.268) ≈ 16.2 m/s ≈ 58 km/h. At this speed, the car needs no friction to hold the curve.

**Definition.** For a curve of radius r banked at angle θ, the **optimum speed** v₀ at which no friction is required is given by v₀ = √(rg tan θ). Below this speed, friction must act up the slope to prevent sliding down. Above it, friction must act down the slope to prevent sliding up and out. The maximum safe speed with friction is v_max = √[rg (μₛ + tan θ) / (1 − μₛ tan θ)]. Banking reduces the reliance on friction — it doesn't eliminate the need for it at all speeds.

**In action.** Motorway exit ramps, velodrome tracks, and railway curves are all banked. On a velodrome, the banking can exceed 40° — at racing speeds, the cyclist rides perpendicular to the track surface, not to the ground. The same physics governs an aeroplane banking into a turn: the lift force from the wings tilts, and its horizontal component provides the centripetal force. Banking is the universal trick for turning without slipping, whether the surface is road, track, or air.

---

## 3. Energy & momentum

### What is energy? A first feel
*BB 714 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Energy is the ability to make things happen — to move something, heat something, light something. A moving car has energy because it can crush a barrier. A stretched spring has energy because it can launch a toy. A hot cup of tea has energy because it can warm your hands. You eat food to get energy for your muscles. The unit is the joule. 1 joule is roughly the energy needed to lift a small apple from the floor onto a table (about 1 metre up).

**Concrete.** Lift a 100 g apple 1 metre: that takes 1 joule of energy. A 60 W light bulb uses 60 joules every second. A chocolate bar (200 kcal) contains roughly 840,000 joules — enough energy to lift an 84 kg person 1000 metres vertically. A litre of petrol contains about 35,000,000 joules (35 MJ). The same energy would keep a 60 W bulb lit for 162 hours. The human body at rest burns about 100 W — 100 joules per second — just to stay alive (heart, brain, temperature). Energy is everywhere, always being converted from one form to another.

**Definition.** Energy is physics's main currency. It can't be created or destroyed — only changed from one form to another. In a car engine: chemical energy (petrol) → thermal energy (explosion) → kinetic energy (motion). In a solar panel: light energy → electrical energy. In a battery: chemical energy → electrical energy. In a loudspeaker: electrical energy → sound energy. Every machine, every living thing, every star is an energy converter. The total energy before and after is always the same. This is the law of conservation of energy — the most powerful, most universal principle in all of science.

---

### Elastic potential energy: springs and stretch
*BB 721 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Stretch a rubber band or squash a spring. You've stored energy. Let go, and the energy is released — the rubber band flies across the room, the spring snaps back. This is elastic potential energy. The formula: E = ½kx², where k is the spring's stiffness (how hard it is to stretch) and x is how far you've stretched it. Like kinetic energy, it depends on the square — double the stretch, and you store four times the energy.

**Concrete.** A spring with stiffness k = 200 N/m is stretched 0.1 m. Energy stored = ½ × 200 × 0.1² = 1 J. Same spring stretched 0.3 m: E = ½ × 200 × 0.09 = 9 J — nine times as much for three times the stretch. A car suspension spring (k ≈ 20,000 N/m) compressed by 0.1 m when you hit a bump: E = ½ × 20,000 × 0.01 = 100 J. A bow (archery): draw weight ~200 N at 0.7 m draw length. Average force ~100 N. Energy stored ≈ 100 × 0.7 = 70 J — enough to launch a 20 g arrow at about 84 m/s (300 km/h). A retractable pen has a tiny spring with k ≈ 10 N/m — stretch it 2 cm and you've stored 0.002 J, which is still enough to click the pen.

**Definition.** Elastic energy is everywhere. Shock absorbers in cars use springs and dampers to convert bumps into heat safely. Catapults and trebuchets stored elastic or gravitational energy to hurl projectiles — medieval engineering at its finest. A wind-up toy stores energy in a coiled spring. Even your arteries are elastic — they stretch when the heart pumps and recoil between beats, smoothing the blood flow. The pogo stick, the trampoline, the diving board — all trade elastic energy for kinetic, over and over. k measures stiffness: a steel spring has high k, a rubber band has low k. The stiffer the spring, the more energy it stores for the same stretch.

---

### The energy toolkit so far
*BB 723 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Energy is the single most useful idea in physics. It's measured in joules. Energy can be kinetic (motion), gravitational (height), elastic (stretched springs), chemical (fuel, food), thermal (heat), electrical, and nuclear. Work transfers energy: W = Fd. Power is the rate of transfer: P = W/t. Energy is conserved: it never disappears, only transforms. Efficiency measures how much of the input becomes useful output. The tools are simple, but they explain everything from a bouncing ball to a power grid.

**Concrete.** Key numbers to carry:

• 1 joule: lifting a small apple 1 metre.

• 1 watt: 1 joule per second.

• Human at rest: ~100 W (same as a bright old-style bulb).

• Human sprinting: ~1000 W peak.

• Car engine: ~100 kW (petrol), ~200 kW (electric).

• Car efficiency: ~25% (petrol), ~90% (electric).

• Human muscle efficiency: ~20%.

• 1 litre of petrol: ~35 MJ (35,000,000 J).

• 1 food calorie: 4184 J.

• Hoover Dam: enough for ~1.3 million homes.

**Definition.** **Energy toolkit summary:**

• **Energy:** ability to do work. Measured in J (joules), kWh (3.6 MJ), cal (4.184 J), eV (1.6×10⁻¹⁹ J).

• **Work:** W = Fd cos θ. Energy transferred by a force.

• **Kinetic energy:** KE = ½mv². Energy of motion.

• **Gravitational potential:** GPE = mgh. Energy of position.

• **Elastic potential:** EPE = ½kx². Energy of deformation.

• **Power:** P = W/t = Fv. Rate of energy transfer.

• **Efficiency:** η = E_out/E_in. Always < 1.

• **Conservation:** E_total is constant in a closed system.

• **Work-energy theorem:** W_net = ΔKE.

---

### Energy
*Card 10 · physics · energy*

**Idea.** Energy is the reason for existence. For anything to exist, it has to do something, and what matter does is consume energy and release it.

That is the closest thing matter has to a purpose: to take energy in, and give it back out.

**Concrete.** The formal definition: energy is the capacity to do work. "Work" here means a force moving something over a distance. If something can push, lift, heat, or move anything else, it has energy. Energy is measured in joules.

*See diagram below*

**Definition.** Energy comes in a handful of forms, and they turn into one another.

Kinetic energy is the energy of motion. Potential energy is stored energy, waiting, like a weight held up high. Heat is the energy of jiggling particles. Chemical energy sits in the bonds between atoms, in food and fuel. Light carries energy too.

Different names, but it's all the same thing in different clothing.

---

### Energy is never lost
*Card 11 · physics · energy*

**Idea.** A boulder at the top of a hill holds stored energy, waiting. Let it roll and that stored energy turns into the energy of motion. None of it appears from nowhere, and none of it vanishes.

You cannot make energy from nothing, and you cannot destroy what's there. You can only turn it from one form into another. The total always stays the same.

---

### Work: force times displacement (and when it's zero)
*BB 127 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** In everyday language, holding a heavy bag still is hard work. In physics, it's zero work. Physics defines work narrowly: a force does work only when it moves something — and only the part of the force along the direction of motion counts.

**Concrete.** Push a box with a force of 50 N and it slides 3 m across the floor. The work done is 50 × 3 = 150 J. Now push at a 60° angle to the horizontal — only the horizontal component (50 cos 60° = 25 N) moves the box, so the work is 25 × 3 = 75 J. Now hold the box stationary above your head — the force is real (you're fighting gravity), but the displacement is zero, so the work is zero. Carry the box horizontally at constant speed — the upward force you apply is perpendicular to the motion, so again, zero work.

**Definition.** **Work** W done by a constant force F⃗ on an object that undergoes displacement s⃗ is W = F⃗ · s⃗ = F s cos θ, where θ is the angle between the force and displacement vectors. Work is a scalar, measured in joules (J = N·m). Work is positive when the force helps the motion (θ 90°), and zero when the force is perpendicular to the motion (θ = 90°) or when there is no displacement.

**In action.** The work definition's "cos θ" catches a subtlety: only the force component along the displacement does work. A waiter carrying a tray across a room does zero work on the tray (the upward normal force is perpendicular to the horizontal motion) — the tray's gravitational potential energy doesn't change. A friction force always does negative work because it always opposes motion (θ = 180°, cos 180° = −1). The sign of work tracks whether energy is being added to or drained from the object.

---

### The work-energy theorem
*BB 128 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Work is not just a bookkeeping number. The total work done on an object — by all forces combined — equals the change in its kinetic energy. Push on something and make it speed up: the work you did became extra energy of motion. This is the work-energy theorem, and it's one of the most direct links between force and motion.

**Concrete.** A 2 kg block at rest on a frictionless table is pushed by a 10 N force over 4 m. The work done: W = 10 × 4 = 40 J. The block's final speed: using F = ma, a = 10/2 = 5 m/s². With v² = 0 + 2(5)(4) = 40, so v = √40 ≈ 6.32 m/s. Its kinetic energy: ½mv² = ½ × 2 × 40 = 40 J — exactly the work done. The 40 J of work became 40 J of kinetic energy.

**Definition.** The **work-energy theorem** states that the net work done by all forces acting on a particle equals the change in its kinetic energy: W_net = ΔK = ½mv²_f − ½mv²_i. This is not a new law — it follows directly from Newton's second law integrated over a displacement. It holds for any net force, constant or variable, conservative or non-conservative. The theorem turns a force-and-distance problem into an energy-difference problem, often bypassing the need to find acceleration.

**In action.** The work-energy theorem is most useful when forces vary or when the path is complicated — you don't need to integrate the equations of motion. A block sliding down a curved frictionless track: the only force doing work is gravity (the normal force is always perpendicular to the motion). W_net = mgh (the work done by gravity), so ½mv² = mgh, and v = √(2gh) — the same speed as if it fell straight down. The path shape doesn't matter, only the height change. This is the seed of energy conservation.

---

### Kinetic energy: ½mv², where the formula comes from
*BB 129 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A moving object carries energy by virtue of its motion — kinetic energy. Knock down a pin with a bowling ball and you see it directly: the ball's motion becomes the pin's motion. The formula is ½mv². Why the half? Why the square? Both answers drop out of the work-energy theorem.

**Concrete.** To accelerate a 1 kg object from rest to 10 m/s, you must do work. From the SUVAT equation v² = u² + 2as, the distance covered is s = v²/(2a). The force is F = ma. Work W = F × s = ma × v²/(2a) = ½mv² = ½ × 1 × 100 = 50 J. So the work you put in — 50 J — becomes the kinetic energy the object now carries. The ½ and the v² both come from the kinematics: v² = 2as gives the v², and the 2 in the denominator gives the ½.

**Definition.** **Kinetic energy** K of a particle of mass m moving at speed v is K = ½mv². It is the energy associated with the particle's motion — always non-negative, zero when v = 0. The SI unit is the joule. For a system of particles, the total kinetic energy is the sum of individual ½mv² terms — it is NOT ½MV² where M is total mass and V is the speed of the centre of mass, because the particles may also be moving relative to the centre of mass.

**In action.** Kinetic energy depends on the square of speed, which has a striking consequence: doubling the speed quadruples the energy. A car at 60 km/h has four times the kinetic energy of the same car at 30 km/h — which is why braking distance is roughly four times longer, not twice. A 1-gram insect at 1 m/s has 0.0005 J of kinetic energy. A 1000 kg car at 20 m/s has 200,000 J — 400 million times more. Speed is squared: small changes at the high end matter enormously.

---

### Potential energy: stored by position
*BB 130 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Lift a book from the floor to a shelf. You do work against gravity. That work hasn't disappeared — it's stored in the book's position. Drop the book and the stored energy pours back out as kinetic energy. Energy stored by position is potential energy.

**Concrete.** Lift a 2 kg book from the floor to a shelf 1.5 m high. The work done against gravity is mgh = 2 × 9.8 × 1.5 = 29.4 J. That 29.4 J is now stored as gravitational potential energy. Drop the book: just before it hits the floor, its kinetic energy equals ½mv² = mgh = 29.4 J, so v = √(2 × 9.8 × 1.5) ≈ 5.4 m/s. The energy flowed from your muscles → stored position → motion, without any loss in the ideal case.

**Definition.** **Potential energy** U is energy stored in a system due to the positions of its parts. It is defined only for **conservative forces** — forces for which the work done around any closed path is zero. For gravity near Earth's surface: U = mgh (taking h = 0 at an arbitrary reference level). For a spring obeying Hooke's law: U = ½kx² (taking x = 0 at the natural length). For Newtonian gravity: U = −GMm/r. Only **changes** in potential energy are physically meaningful.

**In action.** The choice of reference level for potential energy is arbitrary because only differences matter. In a rollercoaster problem, you can set U = 0 at the ground, at the top of the first hill, or at the centre of the Earth — the predicted speeds and motions won't change. This is because the work done by gravity depends only on height differences, not absolute heights. The same is true for any conservative force: the potential energy is a bookkeeping tool that tracks how much work the force can do as positions change.

---

### Power: the rate of doing work
*BB 131 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Two engines lift the same load to the same height. Both do the same work. But one does it in 5 seconds, the other in 30. The faster engine is more powerful — not because it does more work, but because it does the same work faster. Power is the rate of doing work.

**Concrete.** Lift a 50 kg crate vertically at a steady 2 m/s. The force required is mg = 50 × 9.8 = 490 N. Each second, the crate rises 2 m, so the work done per second is 490 × 2 = 980 J. That's 980 W, or about 1.3 horsepower. If the same crate were lifted at 0.5 m/s, the power would be 490 × 0.5 = 245 W — a quarter of the power, even though the total work (over the same height) is identical. The more powerful motor gets it done sooner.

**Definition.** **Power** P is the rate at which work is done: P = dW/dt. For a constant force F⃗ acting on an object moving with velocity v⃗, the instantaneous power is P = F⃗ · v⃗ = F v cos θ. The SI unit is the **watt** (W = J/s). Common multiples: kilowatt (10³ W), megawatt (10⁶ W), gigawatt (10⁹ W). An older unit, the horsepower (hp), equals approximately 746 W. Power should not be confused with force or energy — it's how fast energy is transferred.

**In action.** A car's engine power determines its top speed, not its acceleration. At top speed, the forward force from the engine balances air resistance. Since P = Fv, and air resistance grows roughly as v², the power required grows as v³. To double a car's top speed takes roughly eight times the engine power. A 100 W light bulb converts 100 J of electrical energy into light and heat every second. The Sun outputs about 3.8 × 10²⁶ W — the ultimate power rating in our neighbourhood.

---

### Conservation of linear momentum
*BB 133 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** When two objects interact — collide, push apart, or pull together — with no outside forces involved, the total momentum before equals the total momentum after. This is conservation of momentum, and it is one of the most unbreakable laws in physics. It holds in every collision, every explosion, every interaction, no matter how messy.

**Concrete.** A 5 kg cart moves at 3 m/s and collides with a stationary 2 kg cart. They stick together (perfectly inelastic collision). Momentum before: 5 × 3 + 2 × 0 = 15 kg·m/s. Momentum after: (5 + 2) × v = 7v. Equating: 7v = 15, so v ≈ 2.14 m/s. The speed dropped — the combined mass is larger. Kinetic energy before: ½ × 5 × 9 = 22.5 J. After: ½ × 7 × (2.14)² ≈ 16.1 J. Energy is lost (to heat, sound, deformation), but momentum is exactly conserved.

**Definition.** **Conservation of linear momentum:** If the net external force on a system is zero, the total linear momentum of the system remains constant. Σ p⃗_i (initial) = Σ p⃗_f (final). This follows directly from Newton's second and third laws: internal forces come in equal-and-opposite pairs, so they cannot change the total momentum — only external forces can. The law is a vector conservation law — it holds independently for each component (x, y, z).

**In action.** Momentum conservation powers everything from rocket propulsion to billiards. A gun recoils because the bullet and gun start with zero total momentum — the bullet goes forward, the gun goes backward with equal and opposite momentum. A rocket in deep space accelerates by ejecting exhaust backward — the forward momentum gained equals the backward momentum of the exhaust. In particle physics, conservation of momentum is how we detect invisible particles like neutrinos: if the visible particles' momenta don't add up, something unseen carried away the balance.

---

### Impulse and the impulse-momentum theorem
*BB 134 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A large force acting for a short time can produce the same change in momentum as a small force acting for a long time. The product of force and the time it acts — the impulse — is what changes momentum. A kick and a gentle shove can have the same effect if their impulses match.

**Concrete.** A 0.15 kg cricket ball arrives at 30 m/s and is struck back at 40 m/s in the opposite direction. Change in momentum: final momentum = 0.15 × (−40) = −6 kg·m/s (taking the return direction as negative). Initial momentum = 0.15 × 30 = 4.5 kg·m/s. Δp = −6 − 4.5 = −10.5 kg·m/s. If the bat-ball contact lasts 0.01 s, the average force is F = Δp/Δt = 10.5 / 0.01 = 1050 N — over 700 times the ball's weight. A shorter contact time would mean an even larger force for the same impulse.

**Definition.** **Impulse** J⃗ is the product of a force and the time interval over which it acts: J⃗ = ∫ F⃗ dt. For a constant force, J⃗ = F⃗ Δt. The **impulse-momentum theorem** states that the impulse of the net force equals the change in momentum: J⃗ = Δp⃗ = mv⃗_f − mv⃗_i. This is just Newton's second law integrated over time. Impulse has the same units as momentum: kg·m/s, or equivalently N·s.

**In action.** The impulse-momentum theorem explains why airbags, crash mats, and catching a ball with "soft hands" work. A given Δp is fixed — the speed change from moving to stopped cannot be altered. But by increasing Δt (the time over which the stop occurs), the average force F = Δp/Δt is reduced. An airbag stretches a 0.01 s dashboard collision into a 0.1 s cushion — reducing the force on the passenger by a factor of 10. This is the same physics as Card 08 but now in the formal language of impulse and momentum change.

---

## 4. Rotational dynamics

### The nature of force: what a push actually is
*BB 250 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Pick up a book. Your hand exerts a force. The Earth exerts a force on the same book from 6,371 km away, through empty space, with no strings attached. A force is a push or a pull — but that's the effect, not the cause. At the deepest level, every force in the universe is one of four: gravity, electromagnetism, the strong nuclear force, or the weak nuclear force. The contact forces you feel — a table pushing up, friction dragging back, tension in a rope — are all electromagnetic at root. Electrons in your hand repel electrons in the book. That repulsion IS the contact force.

**Concrete.** Push a book across a table. Four forces act on the book: (1) Gravity — the Earth's mass pulls the book's mass. This is a field force, acting at a distance. (2) Normal force — the table's atoms repel the book's atoms. This is a contact force, ultimately electromagnetic. (3) Your hand's push — another contact force, electromagnetic at root. (4) Friction — the table resists sliding, also a contact electromagnetic force. The book accelerates because the net of these four vectors is not zero. If you stop pushing, friction alone decelerates the book to rest. Remove the table and only gravity acts — the book falls. Every force has an identifiable source (Earth, table, hand) and an identifiable type (gravity, electromagnetic). If you can't name the source, you haven't found the force — you've imagined one.

**Definition.** A **force** is an interaction that, when unopposed, changes the motion of an object. It is a vector quantity — magnitude, direction, point of application. SI unit: **newton** (N). 1 N = the force needed to accelerate 1 kg at 1 m/s². The **four fundamental forces** of nature: (1) **Gravity** — between masses, weakest but infinite range. (2) **Electromagnetism** — between charges, holds atoms and molecules together. (3) **Strong nuclear force** — binds protons and neutrons in the nucleus, strongest but ~10⁻¹⁵ m range. (4) **Weak nuclear force** — responsible for certain types of radioactive decay. Every macroscopic force you experience — tension, normal, friction, spring — is electromagnetic at the atomic level.

**In action.** Knowing the four fundamental forces reframes every mechanics problem. Gravity always acts, is always attractive, and is always proportional to mass. Electromagnetism is behind every contact force — and it's why you don't fall through your chair. The electrons in the chair and the electrons in you repel each other with staggering force at tiny distances. The strong force explains why atomic nuclei don't fly apart despite the electrostatic repulsion of protons — it's ~100× stronger than electromagnetism at nuclear distances. The weak force explains why the Sun shines — it mediates the first step of the proton-proton chain that powers stellar fusion. Forces are not a list to memorise. They are the four ways the universe talks to itself, and everything you push, pull, or feel is one of these four conversations.

---

### Forces on an inclined plane: the complete attack
*BB 251 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** The inclined plane is the perfect testing ground for force analysis. Gravity pulls straight down — always. But the slope only cares about the component along its surface. So you resolve weight into two pieces: mg sin θ pulling the block down the slope, and mg cos θ pressing it into the slope. The normal force equals mg cos θ (not mg — the slope matters). Friction opposes motion (or impending motion) along the slope. Draw the free body diagram, resolve carefully, write ΣF = ma along and perpendicular to the slope — and the inclined plane surrenders every time.

**Concrete.** A 10 kg block rests on a slope at 30°. μₛ = 0.5, μₖ = 0.3. Will it slide? Weight: mg = 98 N. Resolve: mg sin 30° = 98 × 0.5 = 49 N (down slope). mg cos 30° = 98 × 0.866 = 84.9 N (into slope). Normal N = 84.9 N (perpendicular equilibrium). Maximum static friction: fₛ_max = μₛN = 0.5 × 84.9 = 42.5 N. Down-slope pull (49 N) > max static friction (42.5 N) → it slides. Acceleration: net force down slope = 49 − μₖN = 49 − 0.3×84.9 = 49 − 25.5 = 23.5 N. a = F/m = 23.5/10 = 2.35 m/s². Now add a string pulling UP the slope with tension T = 60 N. Net force down slope = 49 − 60 = −11 N (i.e. 11 N UP the slope). Friction now opposes this upward tendency — it acts DOWN the slope. The block may or may not move depending on whether 11 N exceeds fₛ_max. This is the inclined plane puzzle: mg sin θ, mg cos θ, normal, friction, and any applied forces — five vectors that must be correctly resolved and summed.

**Definition.** For a block of mass m on a slope of angle θ: (1) Resolve weight into **mg sin θ** (parallel, down slope) and **mg cos θ** (perpendicular, into slope). (2) Normal force **N = mg cos θ** (if no other perpendicular forces). (3) Friction acts along the slope, opposing motion or impending motion: maximum static friction fₛ_max = μₛN; kinetic friction fₖ = μₖN. (4) Write ΣF_∥ = ma and ΣF_⊥ = 0 (no acceleration perpendicular to the slope). The angle of repose: tan θ = μₛ — the slope angle where the block just begins to slide.

**In action.** The inclined plane is the universal force-analysis workout. Add an external force at an arbitrary angle — resolve IT into slope-parallel and slope-perpendicular components too. Add a second mass hanging over a pulley at the top of the slope — now tension joins the party. The procedure never changes: (1) Draw the FBD. (2) Choose axes — ALWAYS align one axis with the slope, one perpendicular. (3) Resolve EVERY force that isn't already aligned. (4) Write ΣF = ma for each axis. (5) Solve. The inclined plane trains the skill that every complex mechanics problem demands: systematic resolution, careful sign conventions, and the discipline to account for every force before writing equations. Master the inclined plane, and you can handle any static or dynamic force configuration.

---

### Connected bodies: tension, pulleys, and linked motion
*BB 252 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Two masses connected by a string. Pull one, and the other moves. They share a tension, they share an acceleration magnitude, but they may have different forces acting on them. The trick: treat each mass as a separate free body. Write F = ma for each one. The tension appears in both equations, tying them together. Solve simultaneously. The string is the courier — it transmits force without storing it (if massless and inextensible). A pulley changes the direction of tension without changing its magnitude (if frictionless). Connected-body problems are F = ma applied twice, linked by a constraint.

**Concrete.** Atwood's machine: two masses, m₁ = 3 kg and m₂ = 5 kg, connected by a light string over a frictionless pulley. Both accelerate — the heavier falls, the lighter rises — with the same magnitude a (the string doesn't stretch). FBD for m₁ (rising): T − m₁g = m₁a. FBD for m₂ (falling): m₂g − T = m₂a. Add the equations to eliminate T: m₂g − m₁g = (m₁+m₂)a → a = g(m₂−m₁)/(m₂+m₁) = 9.8(2)/8 = 2.45 m/s². Tension: T = m₁(g+a) = 3(9.8+2.45) = 36.75 N (or T = m₂(g−a) = 5(9.8−2.45) = 36.75 N — consistent). Check extremes: if m₁=m₂, a=0, T=mg — balanced. If m₂≫m₁, a≈g, T≈2m₁g — the lighter mass limits the tension. If the pulley has mass and friction, we need torque — that's the bridge to rotational dynamics (coming in Part 3).

**Definition.** For **connected bodies** linked by an ideal (massless, inextensible) string over a frictionless, massless pulley: (1) Tension T is the same on both sides of the pulley. (2) The magnitudes of acceleration of both masses are equal (|a₁| = |a₂| = a) because the string length is constant. (3) Write F = ma for each body separately, with T as a linking variable. (4) Solve the simultaneous equations. For a mass on a horizontal surface connected to a hanging mass: a = m_hanging g / (m_total). For an inclined plane with a hanging mass: the hanging weight fights mg sin θ ± friction. The general principle: isolate, apply F=ma, link via constraints.

**In action.** Connected-body problems are the prototype for every multi-object system in physics. An elevator: the cabin is one body, the counterweight another, linked by a cable over a pulley. A train: locomotive pulls carriages, each coupling transmits tension to the next — a chain of connected bodies. In biomechanics: muscles pull bones via tendons — tension elements connecting body segments. The method is always the same: separate FBDs, F=ma for each, constraints linking them. The constraints encode the geometry (string length, pulley radius, linkage length) and the physics (tension uniform for ideal string, acceleration equal). When you add pulleys with mass — as in real machinery — the rotational dynamics of the pulley itself (τ = Iα) must be included. The connected-body method is the scaffold on which all multi-object dynamics is built.

---

### Systems of particles: the whole is F = ma on the COM
*BB 253 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** So far, F = ma has been applied to single particles. But what about a system — a swarm of particles, a rigid body, a solar system? The miracle: the centre of mass of ANY system moves as if all the mass were concentrated there and all the EXTERNAL forces acted there. Internal forces — the forces particles exert on each other — cancel out in pairs (Newton's third law) and do not affect the COM's motion. You can treat a spinning, tumbling, exploding object as a single point particle for the purpose of its overall trajectory.

**Concrete.** A firework rocket explodes in mid-air. The fragments spray in all directions. But the centre of mass of all the fragments continues on exactly the same parabolic trajectory the rocket was following before the explosion. The explosion was internal forces — fragments pushed on each other. Those forces cancel for the COM. Gravity, the only external force, continues to pull the COM down in its perfect parabola. A high-jumper arches over the bar — their body contorts, but their centre of mass passes UNDER the bar even as their body passes OVER it (the Fosbury Flop). The COM doesn't care about the body's acrobatics; it follows the projectile parabola determined by the takeoff velocity and gravity. In a collision: m₁v₁ + m₂v₂ = (m₁+m₂)V_COM — the COM velocity is constant throughout (if no external forces).

**Definition.** For a system of particles: (1) The **centre of mass** r⃗_COM = (Σ mᵢr⃗ᵢ) / M. (2) Newton's second law for the system: **ΣF⃗_ext = M a⃗_COM** — the sum of external forces equals total mass times COM acceleration. Internal forces sum to zero and do not appear. (3) Total momentum P⃗ = M v⃗_COM. If ΣF_ext = 0, v⃗_COM is constant — the COM moves with constant velocity. (4) The motion of the system can be decomposed into: COM translation + motion relative to COM (rotation, vibration). The COM equation handles the translation. For rotation about the COM, we need torque and angular momentum — the subject of Parts 2–4 of this course.

**In action.** The COM principle is the great simplifier. A wrench thrown spinning through the air — its COM traces a perfect parabola, even as the ends whirl around it. A binary star system — two stars orbiting each other — has a COM that moves in a straight line through the galaxy (or an orbit around the galactic centre). In rocket propulsion: the COM of the rocket + ejected exhaust continues with constant velocity (no external forces in deep space) even as the rocket accelerates forward. The exhaust goes backward, the rocket goes forward, the COM stays put. The COM theorem turns a chaotic many-body problem into a single-particle problem — find the COM, sum the external forces, and F = ma takes care of the rest. The internal forces can do whatever they like; the COM doesn't care.

---

### What is torque? The force that twists
*BB 254 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Push on a door near the hinge — it barely moves. Push with the same force at the handle — it swings easily. The force is the same. The distance from the hinge is different. This is torque: the rotational equivalent of force. Torque depends on three things: how hard you push, how far from the pivot you push, and the angle between your push and the lever. Push perpendicular to the door at the handle — maximum torque. Push parallel to the door (into the hinge) — zero torque. Torque = force × perpendicular distance from the pivot. It's what makes things spin.

**Concrete.** A wrench loosening a bolt: apply F = 50 N at the end of a 0.3 m wrench, perpendicular to the handle. Torque: τ = F × r_⊥ = 50 × 0.3 = 15 N·m. Now apply the same force at a 30° angle to the handle. Only the perpendicular component counts: F sin 30° = 50 × 0.5 = 25 N. Torque = 25 × 0.3 = 7.5 N·m — half as effective. The best technique: push perpendicular at the very end of the wrench. Worst: push along the handle (θ = 0°, sin 0° = 0, τ = 0). The same principle governs seesaws: a child of mass 20 kg sitting 2 m from the pivot exerts torque τ = (20×9.8) × 2 = 392 N·m. A 40 kg child can balance at 1 m: τ = (40×9.8) × 1 = 392 N·m — equal torque, equilibrium.

**Definition.** **Torque** τ (also called moment of force) is the rotational analogue of force. For a force F applied at a distance r from a pivot point O: **τ = r × F** (cross product). Magnitude: **τ = r F sin θ**, where θ is the angle between the position vector r⃗ (from pivot to point of application) and the force vector F⃗. **Perpendicular distance** (lever arm): d = r sin θ. So τ = F × d — force times lever arm. Units: **N·m** (same dimensions as energy, but conceptually different — torque is a vector, energy is a scalar). Direction: given by the right-hand rule (fingers curl from r⃗ to F⃗, thumb points along τ⃗). By convention: anticlockwise torque is positive; clockwise is negative.

**In action.** Torque is the universal language of turning. Every wrench, screwdriver, steering wheel, door handle, and tap is a torque device. The longer the lever arm, the less force needed — this is mechanical advantage in its purest form. A longer wrench multiplies your force. A gear system trades torque for speed (or vice versa): a small gear driving a large gear multiplies torque and reduces speed. In the human body: muscles attach close to joints (short lever arm), so they must exert enormous forces to produce modest torques. The bicep attaches ~5 cm from the elbow pivot; holding a 10 kg mass at 30 cm from the elbow requires bicep tension of ~600 N — six times the weight. Torque explains why your back hurts when you lift with bad form: the lever arm of the weight about your spine is large, demanding enormous muscle forces.

---

### Calculating torque: the cross product in practice
*BB 255 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Torque is a cross product: τ⃗ = r⃗ × F⃗. Its magnitude is rF sin θ. Its direction is perpendicular to both r⃗ and F⃗ — along the axis of rotation. For most problems, you don't need the full 3D vector treatment. You need the magnitude (r F sin θ) and the sign (anticlockwise = +, clockwise = −). But when multiple forces act on an extended body, you must compute torque about a CHOSEN pivot point — and the choice of pivot matters. Choose wisely and the problem simplifies.

**Concrete.** A uniform beam of length 4 m and weight 200 N rests on two supports at its ends. A 600 N weight sits 1 m from the left end. Find the reaction forces at the supports. Choose the left support as the pivot. Calculate torque about this point: (1) R₁ (left support): r = 0, so τ = 0 — this is why you choose a support as pivot, to eliminate an unknown. (2) Beam weight (200 N at 2 m from left — the centre): τ = −200 × 2 = −400 N·m (clockwise). (3) Load (600 N at 1 m): τ = −600 × 1 = −600 N·m (clockwise). (4) R₂ (right support, at 4 m): τ = +R₂ × 4 (anticlockwise). For rotational equilibrium, Στ = 0: 4R₂ − 400 − 600 = 0 → R₂ = 250 N. For translational equilibrium, ΣF_y = 0: R₁ + R₂ − 200 − 600 = 0 → R₁ = 800 − 250 = 550 N. Check: choosing the RIGHT support as pivot should give the same R₁. Try it — it does. The choice of pivot is a strategy, not a physical constraint.

**Definition.** To calculate torque about a point O: (1) For each force, find the **position vector** r⃗ from O to the point of application. (2) Compute τ = rF sin θ, or equivalently τ = F × d, where d = r sin θ is the **lever arm** — the perpendicular distance from O to the line of action of the force. (3) Assign sign: anticlockwise = positive, clockwise = negative (convention must be consistent). (4) Sum all torques. For a **couple** (two equal, opposite, parallel forces with different lines of action): τ_couple = F × d, where d = perpendicular distance between the lines of action. A couple produces pure rotation — no net force, so the COM doesn't accelerate.

**In action.** The pivot-choice strategy is the torque equivalent of choosing axes in force problems. Choose the pivot where an unknown force acts — that unknown disappears from the torque equation. Choose the pivot where multiple unknown forces act — they all disappear. In a ladder-against-wall problem: choose the pivot at the bottom of the ladder. The normal force and friction at the bottom produce zero torque, leaving only the wall's normal force and the ladder's weight. In a crane or extended structure: choose the pivot to eliminate the maximum number of unknowns. The torque equation is one equation — use it strategically to extract one unknown at a time. Combined with ΣF_x = 0 and ΣF_y = 0, you have three equations for 2D static equilibrium problems — enough to solve for three unknowns. If there are more unknowns, the problem is statically indeterminate (you need material properties, not just force/torque balance).

---

### Couples and pure rotation
*BB 256 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Take a bicycle wheel by the axle and spin it. Your two hands apply equal and opposite forces — one pushing up, one pushing down. The net force is zero — the wheel's centre of mass doesn't accelerate. But the net torque is NOT zero — the wheel spins. This is a couple: two equal, opposite, parallel forces offset by a distance. It produces pure rotation with no translation. A couple is the rotational equivalent of a single force: as far as rotation is concerned, a couple is the fundamental building block.

**Concrete.** Turning a steering wheel: your hands apply equal and opposite forces at the rim. If each hand pushes with 20 N and the wheel diameter is 40 cm (radius 20 cm), the couple torque = F × (distance between forces) = 20 × 0.4 = 8 N·m. If you push with one hand and pull with the other, the forces are in the same rotational sense and add — still a couple of 8 N·m. Opening a tap: your thumb and finger form a couple on the handle. Twisting a screwdriver: your hand applies a couple to the handle. The key: the net force is zero, so the object's centre of mass stays put (or continues with constant velocity). But the net torque is non-zero, so the angular velocity changes. A couple cannot be replaced by a single force — it IS the simplest force configuration that produces rotation without translation. The torque of a couple is independent of the choice of pivot — it's the same about any point. τ_couple = Fd, always.

**Definition.** A **couple** is a pair of equal, opposite, parallel forces with different lines of action. Properties: (1) Net force = 0 — no translation of the COM. (2) Net torque τ = **F × d**, where d is the perpendicular distance between the force lines. (3) The torque of a couple is **independent of the pivot** — it's a free vector. (4) A couple cannot be reduced to a single force — it is the simplest system that produces pure rotation. Any system of forces can be reduced to a single resultant force (acting at the COM for translation) PLUS a resultant couple (for rotation). This is the fundamental theorem of statics.

**In action.** The couple is the reason your washing machine walks across the floor if the load is unbalanced. The spinning drum's centre of mass is offset from the rotation axis — the centrifugal force on the COM plus the reaction at the bearing form a couple (or, equivalently, a rotating unbalanced force). This couple produces a net torque on the machine, which rocks and walks. Electric motors work by producing a couple: the magnetic forces on opposite sides of the armature are equal and opposite, producing a torque on the rotor. In structural engineering, a couple is the internal resistance of a beam to bending — tension on one side, compression on the other, separated by the beam's depth, forming a couple that resists the applied bending moment. The couple is not an odd special case — it's half of mechanics. Forces handle translation; couples handle rotation.

---

### Rotational equilibrium: ΣF = 0 AND Στ = 0
*BB 257 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A point particle is in equilibrium when the net force is zero. An extended body needs TWO conditions: ΣF = 0 (no translation) AND Στ = 0 (no rotation). A ruler balanced on your finger satisfies both — the normal force up equals weight down (ΣF_y = 0), and the weight acts through the pivot so torque is zero (Στ = 0). If ΣF = 0 but Στ ≠ 0, the body spins in place. If Στ = 0 about one point but ΣF ≠ 0, the body accelerates without spinning. Both must be satisfied for static equilibrium.

**Concrete.** A uniform ladder of length 5 m and weight 200 N leans against a frictionless wall at 60° to the horizontal. The ground has friction. Find the forces. Forces: weight W = 200 N down at the centre (2.5 m along the ladder from either end). Normal from wall N_w (horizontal, to the left — the wall is frictionless so no vertical force). Normal from ground N_g (vertical, up). Friction from ground f (horizontal, to the right — prevents the ladder's foot from sliding left). Four unknowns: N_w, N_g, f, and (implicitly) the angle. Three equations: ΣF_x = 0 → N_w = f. ΣF_y = 0 → N_g = W = 200 N. Στ = 0 about the foot of the ladder. Take torques about the foot: W produces torque −200 × (2.5 cos 60°) = −200 × 1.25 = −250 N·m (clockwise). N_w produces torque +N_w × (5 sin 60°) = +N_w × 4.33 (anticlockwise). Στ = 0: 4.33 N_w = 250 → N_w = 57.7 N. So f = 57.7 N. The ladder stays put as long as f ≤ μₛ N_g — i.e., 57.7 ≤ μₛ × 200 → μₛ ≥ 0.29. If the floor is more slippery than μₛ = 0.29, the ladder slides.

**Definition.** A rigid body is in **static equilibrium** when: (1) **ΣF⃗ = 0** — vector sum of all external forces is zero (no translational acceleration). (2) **Στ⃗ = 0** about ANY point — vector sum of all external torques is zero (no angular acceleration). The torque condition must hold about EVERY point; if it holds about one point and ΣF = 0, it automatically holds about all points. In 2D, this gives three scalar equations: ΣF_x = 0, ΣF_y = 0, Στ_z = 0 — enough to solve for three unknowns. For more unknowns, the structure is statically indeterminate — internal forces and deformations must be considered.

**In action.** The two equilibrium conditions are the foundation of all structural engineering. A bridge doesn't move or twist — every beam, cable, and joint satisfies ΣF = 0 and Στ = 0 under its design loads. A crane lifting a load: the counterweight is positioned so that the net torque about the base is zero — without it, the crane topples. Your body standing on one foot: the ankle, knee, and hip joints adjust moments to keep your COM over the contact patch. A picture frame hanging from two nails — the tensions adjust so that the weight is shared and the frame doesn't rotate. The ladder problem is the classic workout: choose the pivot at the foot (eliminates N_g and f), compute torques, find the wall force, then use ΣF = 0 for the rest. The pattern — choose pivot to eliminate unknowns, write Στ = 0, then ΣF = 0 — solves every static equilibrium problem.

---

### Moment of inertia: the rotational mass
*BB 258 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Push an empty shopping trolley — it accelerates easily. Push a fully loaded one — it resists. Mass is linear inertia. Now spin a bicycle wheel — it resists being spun up AND resists being stopped. That's rotational inertia — moment of inertia, I. And it's not just about how much mass, but about WHERE the mass is. Mass far from the axis contributes far more to I than mass near the axis. I = Σmr² — every particle's mass multiplied by the square of its distance from the axis. A disc with mass at the rim has higher I than the same mass concentrated at the centre. Moment of inertia is the rotational mass.

**Concrete.** Two cylinders of equal mass (1 kg) and equal radius (0.1 m): one is a solid cylinder, one is a thin cylindrical shell (all mass at the rim). I_solid = ½MR² = 0.5 × 1 × 0.01 = 0.005 kg·m². I_shell = MR² = 1 × 0.01 = 0.01 kg·m². The shell has TWICE the moment of inertia. Roll both down an incline — the solid cylinder wins because less of the gravitational potential energy goes into rotation and more goes into translation. Ice skater spinning: arms outstretched → mass far from axis → large I, slow spin. Pull arms in → mass close to axis → small I, fast spin. Same angular momentum L = Iω, so ω increases as I decreases. This is the skater effect — conservation of angular momentum in action (coming in BB-NEW-290).

**Definition.** **Moment of inertia** I of a rigid body about an axis is **I = Σ mᵢ rᵢ²** (discrete) or **I = ∫ r² dm** (continuous). Units: kg·m². It depends on: mass, mass distribution, and the chosen axis. Common values about the centre of mass: thin rod (length L, about centre): I = ML²/12; about end: I = ML²/3. Solid cylinder/disc (radius R, about central axis): I = ½MR². Thin spherical shell: I = ⅔MR². Solid sphere: I = ⅖MR². **Parallel axis theorem**: I = I_COM + Md², where d is the perpendicular distance between the two parallel axes. **Radius of gyration** k: I = Mk² — the distance from the axis where all the mass would need to be concentrated to give the same I.

**In action.** Moment of inertia is the design parameter for everything that rotates. A flywheel stores rotational energy (½Iω²) — large I, high ω = massive energy storage, used to smooth power delivery in engines. A racing car wheel has low I (lightweight materials, mass concentrated near the hub) for fast acceleration. A cyclist's wheels: reducing rim mass improves acceleration more than reducing frame mass because of the r² factor — mass at the rim contributes fully to I, mass at the hub contributes almost nothing. A tightrope walker's long pole: by holding a pole with mass far from the rotation axis (their body), they enormously increase I, which makes them harder to rotate — giving them time to correct their balance. In all cases, I is the resistance to angular acceleration. The larger I is, the more torque is needed to achieve the same angular acceleration. τ = Iα — the rotational F = ma. That's next.

---

### Newton's second law for rotation: τ = Iα
*BB 259 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** F = ma governs translation. τ = Iα governs rotation. Torque is the rotational force. Moment of inertia is the rotational mass. Angular acceleration (α) is the rotational acceleration. The equation is the exact analogue: net torque = moment of inertia × angular acceleration. Just as a net force produces linear acceleration, a net torque produces angular acceleration. And just as a=0 means constant velocity, α=0 means constant angular velocity. The parallel is perfect.

**Concrete.** A solid disc (M = 5 kg, R = 0.2 m, I = ½MR² = 0.5 × 5 × 0.04 = 0.1 kg·m²) is free to rotate about its central axis. A constant torque of 2 N·m is applied. Angular acceleration: α = τ/I = 2/0.1 = 20 rad/s². After 3 seconds: ω = αt = 60 rad/s ≈ 573 rpm. Number of revolutions: θ = ½αt² = ½ × 20 × 9 = 90 rad = 90/(2π) ≈ 14.3 revolutions. Now wind a string around the disc's rim and hang a 2 kg mass (weight = 19.6 N). The tension T in the string provides torque τ = TR = T × 0.2. The mass accelerates down: mg − T = ma. The disc rotates: τ = Iα = TR. And a = αR (the string unwinds without slipping). Three equations: mg − T = ma, TR = Iα, a = αR. Substitute a = αR into the first: mg − T = mαR. From the second: T = Iα/R. Substitute: mg − Iα/R = mαR → α = mgR/(mR² + I) = (2×9.8×0.2)/(2×0.04 + 0.1) = 3.92/0.18 = 21.8 rad/s². Linear acceleration a = αR = 4.36 m/s² — less than g because some of gravity's work goes into spinning the disc. This is why a real pulley (with mass) reduces acceleration compared to an ideal massless pulley.

**Definition.** Newton's second law for rotation: **Στ = Iα**, where Στ = net torque about the rotation axis, I = moment of inertia about that axis, and α = angular acceleration. The equation holds for a rigid body rotating about a FIXED axis. Analogous to ΣF = ma: τ ↔ F, I ↔ m, α ↔ a. For a body both translating and rotating, use ΣF = ma for the COM motion AND Στ = Iα for rotation about the COM. The torque and I must be about the SAME axis. The condition for rolling without slipping: a = αR, where a is the COM acceleration and α is the angular acceleration about the COM.

**In action.** τ = Iα is the engine of rotational dynamics. Every motor — from a phone's vibration motor to a factory's induction motor — is specified by its torque and its rotor's moment of inertia. The start-up time to reach operating speed is t = ω/α = Iω/τ. A hard disk drive spins up in seconds because τ is high and I is small. A loaded cement mixer takes minutes because I is enormous. In biomechanics: your arm is a lever with a moment of inertia about the shoulder. The deltoid muscle provides torque τ = Iα to raise the arm. Holding a weight in your hand increases I dramatically (the weight is far from the shoulder joint, and r² amplifies its contribution) — the muscle must produce far more torque for the same α. The τ = Iα framework, combined with F = ma for translation, is the complete set of equations for any rigid body motion — translation plus rotation. The two worlds are controlled by two equations, operating in parallel.

---

### Angular momentum: the spinning quantity that is conserved
*BB 260 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Linear momentum p = mv is conserved when there's no external force. Angular momentum L = Iω is conserved when there's no external torque. This is the rotational twin of momentum conservation — and it's arguably even more dramatic. The ice skater pulling in their arms: I decreases, ω increases, L = Iω stays constant. The spinning neutron star (pulsar): a star collapses from ~10⁶ km to ~10 km radius — I decreases by a factor of ~10¹⁰, so ω increases by the same factor. A star that rotated once a month now rotates hundreds of times per second. Angular momentum conservation is ruthless — it demands that the product Iω remains unchanged, and nature obeys.

**Concrete.** A student sits on a freely rotating stool holding two 3 kg masses at arm's length (0.8 m from the axis). Their body's moment of inertia (without masses) is 4 kg·m². Masses at 0.8 m: I_masses = 2 × 3 × (0.8)² = 2 × 3 × 0.64 = 3.84 kg·m². Total I_initial = 4 + 3.84 = 7.84 kg·m². The stool is spun to ω = 2 rad/s. L = Iω = 7.84 × 2 = 15.68 kg·m²/s. The student pulls the masses in to 0.2 m. I_masses_new = 2 × 3 × (0.2)² = 2 × 3 × 0.04 = 0.24 kg·m². I_new = 4 + 0.24 = 4.24 kg·m². With L conserved: ω_new = L/I_new = 15.68/4.24 ≈ 3.70 rad/s — nearly double. The student feels themselves spinning faster — the rotational equivalent of an ice skater. The torque required to pull the masses inward does work, which increases the rotational kinetic energy (½Iω²_new > ½Iω²_old). Where did the extra energy come from?
