The student's muscles doing work against the centrifugal force. Energy is NOT conserved in this process (the student did work); angular momentum IS conserved because no external torque acted on the stool+student+masses system.

**Definition.** **Angular momentum** L of a rigid body rotating about a fixed axis: **L = Iω**. It is a vector directed along the rotation axis (right-hand rule). Units: kg·m²/s. For a particle: L⃗ = r⃗ × p⃗ = r⃗ × (mv⃗). **Conservation of angular momentum**: if the net external torque on a system is zero (Στ_ext = 0), the total angular momentum of the system is conserved — L_initial = L_final. This follows from τ = dL/dt: if τ = 0, then dL/dt = 0, so L = constant. Angular momentum is conserved independently in each direction — L_x, L_y, L_z are each conserved if τ_x, τ_y, τ_z are zero respectively.

**In action.** Angular momentum conservation is why the solar system is flat. The original gas cloud had some tiny net angular momentum from random turbulence. As it collapsed, conservation amplified that rotation — the cloud flattened into a disc, and the planets orbit in roughly the same plane. It's why helicopters need tail rotors: the main rotor's angular momentum change produces a torque on the helicopter body — the tail rotor counters it. It's why gyroscopes resist tipping: changing the direction of L requires a torque (τ = dL/dt), and a large L resists change. It's why a cat dropped upside-down can land on its feet — by twisting its body in a specific sequence, it changes its moment of inertia distribution to rotate without violating angular momentum conservation (the cat's total L remains zero — it rotates its front and rear halves in opposite directions, then adjusts). Angular momentum is conserved, period. No exceptions in classical mechanics. If it looks like L changed, you missed a torque or you missed where the compensating angular momentum went.

---

### Rolling motion: translation + rotation, locked together
*BB 261 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A rolling object is doing two things at once: its centre of mass is translating, and it's rotating about its centre of mass. When it rolls without slipping, these two motions are locked: the distance the COM travels equals the arc length unrolled — x = Rθ. Differentiate: v = Rω. Differentiate again: a = Rα. The point of contact with the ground is instantaneously at REST relative to the ground. This is the magic of rolling: the bottom point stops, the top point moves at 2v_COM. And it's friction that makes it happen — static friction, not kinetic — because the contact point doesn't slide.

**Concrete.** A solid sphere (M = 2 kg, R = 0.1 m, I = ⅖MR² = 0.4 × 2 × 0.01 = 0.008 kg·m²) rolls down a 1 m high incline without slipping. Energy conservation: mgh = ½mv² + ½Iω². With v = ωR → ω = v/R: mgh = ½mv² + ½I(v²/R²) = ½v²(m + I/R²). For the solid sphere: I/R² = ⅖M. So mgh = ½v²(m + ⅖m) = ½v²(7/5)m. v² = (10/7)gh = (10/7) × 9.8 × 1 = 14. v = √14 ≈ 3.74 m/s. Compare to a frictionless sliding block: mgh = ½mv² → v = √(2gh) = √19.6 ≈ 4.43 m/s. The rolling sphere is slower because some energy goes into rotation. Compare different shapes rolling: solid sphere (I = ⅖MR²) → v = √(10gh/7). Solid cylinder (I = ½MR²) → v = √(4gh/3). Hollow cylinder (I = MR²) → v = √(gh). The solid sphere wins — least energy "wasted" on rotation. For a given shape, the speed at the bottom depends ONLY on height, not on mass or radius — Galileo would have loved this.

**Definition.** For **rolling without slipping**: (1) **v_COM = ωR** — the translational speed equals the rotational speed times radius. (2) **a_COM = αR**. (3) The instantaneous point of contact with the surface is at REST — static friction acts there (not kinetic). (4) Total kinetic energy: **KE = ½Mv²_COM + ½I_COM ω²** — translation plus rotation. (5) On an incline: a = g sin θ / (1 + I/(MR²)). Objects with smaller I/(MR²) accelerate faster. A frictionless object would slide with a = g sin θ — faster than any rolling object. Static friction provides the torque for rolling but does NO work (the contact point doesn't move).

**In action.** Rolling explains the "which reaches the bottom first?" demonstration. Release a solid cylinder, a hollow cylinder, and a solid sphere simultaneously from the top of an incline. The sphere wins, the solid cylinder comes second, the hollow cylinder last — and they always finish in that order, regardless of mass or radius. It's purely I/(MR²) — the smaller the ratio, the less energy diverted into rotation. In vehicle dynamics: a car wheel that locks and skids (kinetic friction) has less stopping power than a wheel rolling at the edge of slipping (static friction). Anti-lock brakes (ABS) keep the wheels rolling — static friction, maximum braking. In bowling: the ball initially slides, then starts rolling as friction converts sliding to rolling. The transition point is when v = ωR. Rolling is the bridge from translational dynamics to rotational dynamics — F = ma and τ = Iα, linked by a = αR. The two worlds, operating in synchrony.

---

### Conservation of angular momentum: the universe's spin budget
*BB 262 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Linear momentum is conserved when ΣF_ext = 0. Angular momentum is conserved when Στ_ext = 0. This is not an analogy — it's the same principle, rotational symmetry, expressed two ways. If the universe has no preferred direction (it doesn't), angular momentum is conserved. This single law explains why galaxies spin, why planets orbit in planes, why neutron stars are the fastest-spinning objects known, and why you can't tip a spinning gyroscope without it fighting back in a surprising direction.

**Concrete.** A neutron star is born when a massive star's core collapses. The original star (radius ~10⁶ km, rotation period ~30 days) collapses to a neutron star (radius ~10 km). I ∝ R², so I decreases by (10⁶/10)² = 10¹⁰. Since L = Iω is conserved, ω increases by 10¹⁰. Period goes from 30 days to ~0.26 seconds — about 230 rotations per second. The fastest known pulsar spins at 716 Hz — 716 rotations per second. Its surface speed is about ¼ the speed of light. All from angular momentum conservation. In our solar system: the original gas cloud had some tiny angular momentum. As it contracted, the spin increased. The cloud flattened into a disc, and the material in the disc coalesced into planets — all orbiting in the same direction, in nearly the same plane. The Sun contains 99.8% of the solar system's mass but only ~0.5% of its angular momentum — the planets, especially Jupiter, carry most of the spin. This distribution is evidence for how the solar system formed.

**Definition.** **Conservation of angular momentum**: For a system with zero net external torque, L⃗_total = constant — both magnitude and direction. For a single rigid body: I₁ω₁ = I₂ω₂ (if I changes and no external torque acts). For a system of particles: Σ r⃗ᵢ × mᵢv⃗ᵢ = constant. Key consequences: (1) As I decreases, ω increases. (2) The direction of L is fixed — a spinning object resists changes to its spin axis (gyroscopic stability). (3) Angular momentum is a vector — it has three independent components, each conserved if the corresponding torque component is zero. (4) Angular momentum conservation is a consequence of the isotropy of space (no preferred direction) via Noether's theorem.

**In action.** Angular momentum conservation is the universe's spin accountant. In a diving competition: the diver leaves the board with some angular momentum (from the takeoff). In the air, they tuck into a ball — I decreases, ω increases, they spin fast. Before entry, they extend — I increases, ω decreases, they enter the water with minimal splash. Total L throughout the dive is constant (ignoring small air resistance torques). A falling cat: starting from rest (L=0), it can rotate its front and rear halves in opposite directions, keeping total L=0, to land on its feet. A helicopter with a single main rotor: the rotor's angular momentum change produces a torque on the fuselage (τ = dL/dt). Without a tail rotor to counter this torque, the fuselage would spin opposite to the main rotor. Gyroscopic navigation: a gyroscope maintains its spin axis direction in inertial space. Aircraft and spacecraft use gyroscopes to know their orientation even when GPS and visual references are unavailable — because L is conserved, the gyro's axis is a fixed reference.

---

### Levers and mechanical advantage: trading force for distance
*BB 263 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** "Give me a place to stand and I will move the Earth," Archimedes said. He was talking about levers. A lever is a rigid bar pivoted at a fulcrum. A small force applied far from the fulcrum balances a large force close to the fulcrum. The ratio of the distances is the mechanical advantage: MA = d_effort / d_load = F_load / F_effort. Push with 100 N at 2 m from the fulcrum and you can lift 400 N at 0.5 m — an MA of 4. You move your end 4× farther; the load moves 4× less. Force and distance trade off. The product F × d is the same at both ends — torque balance. Every lever in the world works on this one principle.

**Concrete.** Three classes of levers, classified by the relative positions of fulcrum (F), load (L), and effort (E). **Class 1 (FLE):** fulcrum in the middle — seesaw, crowbar, scissors. Can have MA > 1, MA = 1, or MA 1 (effort arm > load arm). A wheelbarrow: the wheel is the fulcrum, the load is in the bin (close to the wheel), your hands are on the handles (far from the wheel). You lift a heavy load with modest upward force — the trade-off is that your hands travel farther than the load rises. **Class 3 (FLE with E in middle):** effort in the middle — tweezers, fishing rod, human forearm. Always MA < 1 — the effort arm is shorter than the load arm. Your bicep pulls ~5 cm from the elbow to lift a weight 30 cm from the elbow. MA ≈ 5/30 = 1/6 — you must pull with 6× the weight's force. Why would nature design such an inefficient lever? Because a small muscle contraction produces a large hand movement — speed and range of motion, not force, is the priority. Tweezers: a small squeeze at the middle produces a precise grip at the tip. Class 3 levers trade force for speed and precision.

**Definition.** A **lever** is a rigid bar that rotates about a fixed point (fulcrum). **Mechanical advantage**: **MA = F_load / F_effort = d_effort / d_load**. MA > 1: force multiplier (small effort lifts large load). MA < 1: speed/distance multiplier (small effort movement produces large load movement). MA = 1: direction changer only. The lever principle follows directly from torque equilibrium about the fulcrum: F_effort × d_effort = F_load × d_load. The three classes: Class 1 (F-L-E), Class 2 (F-E-L actually load in middle), Class 3 (effort in middle).

**In action.** Levers are the simplest machines and the most ubiquitous. Every joint in your body is a lever system. Your neck is a Class 1 lever — the skull's centre of mass is forward of the spine (fulcrum), and the neck muscles at the back pull down to keep your head from nodding forward. Your jaw is a Class 2 or Class 3 lever depending on which teeth you're using and which muscle group dominates. A nutcracker (Class 2) multiplies your grip force enormously. A pair of pliers combines two Class 1 levers with a shared fulcrum. Bolt cutters use compound levers — one lever drives another — to achieve astonishing MA, cutting through hardened steel with moderate hand force. The cost is always the same: distance. Your hands move ten centimetres; the cutting edges move one millimetre. Force × distance is conserved (ignoring friction). The lever doesn't create work — it redistributes it across a different distance and force.

---

### Gyroscopes and precession: when angular momentum fights back
*BB 264 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Spin a wheel, hold the axle horizontal, and try to tip it. It doesn't tip the way you expect. It turns — precesses — at right angles to your push. A gyroscope seems to defy gravity, staying horizontal when it should fall. It's not defying physics; it's obeying angular momentum with absolute fidelity. τ = dL/dt. The torque you apply changes the direction of L, and the gyroscope moves in the direction of the torque, not the force. This is precession — one of the most counterintuitive and beautiful phenomena in classical mechanics.

**Concrete.** A bicycle wheel is mounted on an axle with handles. Spin the wheel to high speed (L large, horizontal). Hold one handle and let go of the other. Gravity produces a torque about the held handle — you expect the wheel to tip down. Instead, it precesses — the axle rotates horizontally around the pivot. Why? L is a vector pointing along the axle. The torque from gravity is perpendicular to L (horizontal, perpendicular to the axle). τ = dL/dt means the CHANGE in L is in the direction of τ. Since torque is perpendicular to L, the change in L is perpendicular to L — it ROTATES L rather than changing its magnitude. The axle sweeps out a horizontal circle. This is precession. The precession angular velocity: Ω = τ/L = mgr/(Iω). The faster the wheel spins (larger ω, larger L), the slower the precession. The heavier the wheel or longer the axle (larger τ), the faster the precession.

**Definition.** When a torque τ⃗ is applied to a spinning body with angular momentum L⃗, and τ⃗ is perpendicular to L⃗, the angular momentum vector precesses — its tip traces a circle. The **precession angular velocity** is **Ω = τ / L** (for τ perpendicular to L). The direction of precession: L⃗ chases τ⃗ (Ω⃗ is perpendicular to both L⃗ and τ⃗). This follows directly from τ = dL/dt: in time dt, dL = τ dt — a small vector in the direction of τ, added perpendicularly to L, rotating L without changing |L|. **Nutation** is the wobbling (nodding) motion that accompanies precession when the torque is not perfectly steady or the initial conditions include a small tilt.

**In action.** Gyroscopic precession is everywhere in rotating systems. The Earth precesses: its axis traces a circle in the sky every ~26,000 years, because the Sun and Moon's gravity torques on the Earth's equatorial bulge. Right now Polaris is the North Star; in 12,000 years, Vega will be. A spinning top: as it slows (ω decreases, L decreases), precession speeds up (Ω = τ/L increases) until the top wobbles and falls — the familiar death spiral. A rifle bullet is spin-stabilised: the rifling imparts enormous spin (ω ~ 3000 rev/s). Aerodynamic torques that would tumble a non-spinning bullet instead cause precession — the bullet's axis stays roughly aligned with its trajectory. Motorcycles: at speed, the spinning wheels provide gyroscopic stability that helps keep the bike upright. You steer a motorcycle at speed not by turning the handlebars the way you want to go, but by countersteering — a torque input that precesses the bike into a lean. Gyroscopes are used in inertial navigation systems on submarines, spacecraft, and aircraft — a spinning mass maintains its orientation in inertial space, providing a reference direction independent of GPS or magnetic compass.

---

### The complete mechanics toolkit: F = ma and τ = Iα, working together
*BB 265 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** You now have the complete classical mechanics toolkit. For translation: ΣF = ma, with F = ma as the special case for constant mass. For rotation: Στ = Iα. For combined motion: both equations, linked by constraints like a = αR for rolling or by tension in connected-body systems. Plus conservation laws: energy, linear momentum, angular momentum. This toolkit — Newton's laws in both linear and rotational form, plus the three great conservation laws — solves essentially every problem in classical mechanics. The art is choosing the right tool for the problem, and knowing when to switch.

**Concrete.** Problem: A solid cylinder (M, R) rolls without slipping down an incline (θ), pulled by a string wound around its axle. The string goes over a frictionless pulley and attaches to a hanging mass m. Find the acceleration. Strategy: Three objects — cylinder, hanging mass, pulley (massless here, so no rotational dynamics for the pulley). For the hanging mass (translating): mg − T = ma. For the cylinder (translating + rotating): Translation along slope: T − Mg sin θ − f = Ma_COM. Rotation about COM: τ = fR = Iα = ½MR²α. Constraint (rolling): a_COM = αR. Constraint (connected): a = a_COM (string doesn't stretch). Four equations, four unknowns (a, T, f, α). Solve: from rotation, f = ½MRα = ½MR(a_COM/R) = ½Ma. Substitute into translation: T − Mg sin θ − ½Ma = Ma → T = Mg sin θ + (3/2)Ma. Substitute into hanging mass equation: mg − [Mg sin θ + (3/2)Ma] = ma → mg − Mg sin θ = (m + 3M/2)a → a = g(m − M sin θ)/(m + 3M/2). If m > M sin θ, the system accelerates with the hanging mass descending. If the cylinder's mass were concentrated at the rim (I = MR²), the denominator would be m + 2M instead of m + 1.5M — the hoop accelerates slower. The rotational inertia directly affects the translational acceleration. Every choice of shape (solid cylinder, hoop, sphere) changes the result through I. This is the power of the toolkit — F = ma for each translating object, τ = Iα for each rotating object, constraints linking them, solve simultaneously.

**Definition.** The complete classical mechanics problem-solving strategy: (1) Identify EVERY object. (2) For each translating object, draw FBD, write ΣF = ma (vector equation, 2 or 3 scalar equations). (3) For each rotating object, compute I about the rotation axis, write Στ = Iα. (4) Identify constraints: strings (a₁ = a₂), pulleys (a = αR for pulley rotation, or T₁ = T₂ for ideal pulley), rolling (v = ωR → a = αR). (5) Count equations and unknowns — they must match. (6) Solve simultaneously. (7) Check limiting cases (mass → 0, angle → 0, friction → 0) to verify the result makes physical sense. The conservation laws (energy, momentum, angular momentum) are shortcuts when applicable — they reduce equation count. But when in doubt, F = ma and τ = Iα always work.

**In action.** This toolkit scales from a single block on a table to the most complex machinery. An engine: pistons translate, crankshaft rotates, connected by a linkage constraint. A robot arm: each joint has a motor providing torque τ = Iα, while the arm's links translate. A satellite: translation of COM under gravity, rotation about COM under thruster torques — two independent sets of equations. The hardest part is never the equations — it's the geometry of the constraints. What's connected to what? Which way does the string pull? Which way does friction act? That's why the FBD is always step one — if you get the arrows right, the equations follow mechanically. And if you're stuck, try the energy method — it avoids forces and torques entirely by tracking energy conversions. The toolkit is not a list to memorise. It's a problem-solving rhythm: isolate, draw forces, write equations, link constraints, solve, check. Every mechanics problem, from a falling apple to a Mars rover, uses the same rhythm. The physics doesn't change — only the complexity of the system does.

---

## 5. Gravitation & orbits

### Everything pulls on everything
*Card 16 · physics · gravity*

**Idea.** Remember the third law: push on a wall and it pushes back, equally. Pulls work the same way.

Imagine everything carries a quiet pull, as if it were a magnet, though it isn't really one. You pull on your chair, and the chair pulls back on you, exactly as hard. Equal and opposite, just like the wall.

**Concrete.** Now scale it up. The Earth pulls on the Moon, and the Moon pulls back on the Earth, just as hard.

They've been holding onto each other across a quarter of a million miles of empty space for billions of years.

**Definition.** And it isn't only the big things. Every two objects in the universe pull on each other. You and the person beside you. Your phone and the wall. Always, everywhere.

This pull is called gravity. It is its own force, not actually magnetism, and unlike a magnet it only ever attracts. It never pushes away.

---

### Newton's law of gravitation: the inverse-square force
*BB 377 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Every particle in the universe attracts every other particle. The force is proportional to both masses, inversely proportional to the square of the distance between them, and directed along the line joining them. This single law explains falling apples, orbiting moons, and the structure of galaxies.

**Concrete.** Two 1 kg masses placed 1 metre apart attract each other with a force of just 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ N — the weight of a single bacterium. The same law applied to the Earth (5.97 × 10²⁴ kg) and a 70 kg person at the Earth's surface (r = 6.37 × 10⁶ m) gives F = (6.67 × 10⁻¹¹)(5.97 × 10²⁴)(70) / (6.37 × 10⁶)² = 686 N — your weight. Gravity is weak, but planets are enormous. The Earth–Moon force: 1.98 × 10²⁰ N. The Earth–Sun force: 3.54 × 10²² N.

**Definition.** **Newton's law of universal gravitation:** The gravitational force between two point masses m₁ and m₂ separated by distance r is F = G m₁ m₂ / r², directed along the line joining them and attractive for both. G = 6.674 × 10⁻¹¹ N·m²/kg² (the universal gravitational constant). The law applies to spherically symmetric bodies as if all mass were concentrated at the centre (Newton proved this). The inverse-square dependence means doubling the distance quarters the force; tripling reduces it to one-ninth. The force obeys the superposition principle — net force on a mass is the vector sum of forces from all other masses.

**In action.** Newton didn't discover that things fall — he discovered that the same force pulling an apple downward also pulls the Moon into its orbit. The Moon is 60 Earth-radii away, so gravity there should be 1/60² = 1/3600 of surface gravity. And indeed: the Moon's centripetal acceleration (v²/r ≈ 0.00272 m/s²) is exactly g/3600 ≈ 9.8/3600. This calculation — done by Newton around 1666 — was the first proof that celestial and terrestrial physics are the same. The inverse-square law is universal: it governs falling stones, orbiting satellites, binary stars, and the expansion of the universe itself.

---

### G: the gravitational constant and the Cavendish experiment
*BB 378 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Newton knew the form of the gravitational law — F = Gm₁m₂/r² — but he didn't know the value of G. Measuring the gravitational attraction between two ordinary objects is absurdly difficult because gravity is so weak. It took 111 years after Newton's death before anyone succeeded.

**Concrete.** Henry Cavendish (1798) used a torsion balance: a light rod with two small lead spheres (0.73 kg each) suspended by a thin wire. Two larger spheres (158 kg each) were brought near the small ones. The gravitational attraction twisted the wire by a tiny angle. By measuring the twist and knowing the wire's stiffness, Cavendish calculated the force. With the masses and distance known, G = Fr²/m₁m₂ ≈ 6.74 × 10⁻¹¹ N·m²/kg² — within 1% of the modern value. From G, Cavendish could also "weigh the Earth": M_Earth = gR²/G ≈ 5.97 × 10²⁴ kg.

**Definition.** **G = 6.67430 × 10⁻¹¹ N·m²/kg²** is the universal gravitational constant — the same value everywhere in the universe. It is the fundamental constant of gravitational interaction, analogous to k in Coulomb's law. G is extremely small compared to the electromagnetic force constant (k ≈ 9 × 10⁹), which is why gravity is negligible between everyday objects but dominates at astronomical scales. Modern measurements use improved torsion balances and atom interferometry, reaching precision of about 22 parts per million. G is the least precisely known fundamental constant because gravity is so hard to isolate from other forces.

**In action.** Knowing G lets us calculate the mass of anything that has something orbiting it. Mass of Earth: measure g and R, then M = gR²/G. Mass of Sun: measure Earth's orbital period (1 year) and orbital radius (1 AU), then M_Sun = 4π²r³/GT² ≈ 2.0 × 10³⁰ kg. Mass of Jupiter: measure the period and radius of one of its moons. Mass of the Milky Way's central black hole: measure the orbits of stars around it (they whip around at 5000 km/s at a distance of about 100 AU — implying M ≈ 4 million solar masses). Every mass in astronomy traces back to G.

---

### Gravitational field strength g: not just 9.8
*BB 379 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Place a 1 kg mass anywhere in space and measure the gravitational force on it. That force, in newtons per kilogram, is the gravitational field strength at that point. At Earth's surface, it's 9.8 N/kg. The field is a way of describing "how hard gravity pulls" at every location, even without a test mass being there.

**Concrete.** At Earth's surface: g = GM/R² = (6.67×10⁻¹¹)(5.97×10²⁴)/(6.37×10⁶)² = 9.80 N/kg. On the Moon's surface: g_Moon = GM_Moon/R_Moon² = (6.67×10⁻¹¹)(7.35×10²²)/(1.74×10⁶)² = 1.62 N/kg — about one-sixth of Earth's. On Mars: g_Mars ≈ 3.71 N/kg. On Jupiter's cloud tops: g_Jupiter ≈ 24.8 N/kg. A 70 kg person weighs 686 N on Earth, 113 N on the Moon, and 1736 N on Jupiter — same mass, completely different weight because g is different.

**Definition.** The **gravitational field strength** at a point is the gravitational force per unit mass experienced by a small test mass placed at that point: g⃗ = F⃗/m. For a point mass M (or spherically symmetric body), g = GM/r² directed radially inward. The field is a vector field — it has a magnitude and direction at every point in space. The SI unit is N/kg, which is dimensionally equivalent to m/s². Near Earth's surface, the field is approximately uniform (parallel, equal magnitude) at 9.8 N/kg downward. The field concept, introduced by Faraday for electromagnetism, eliminates "action at a distance" — masses create fields, and fields exert forces on other masses.

**In action.** The gravitational field is what lets you calculate the force on any mass without re-deriving. Once you know g⃗ at a point, the force on a mass m at that point is simply F⃗ = mg⃗. This is true on any planet. The "surface gravity" of exoplanets — planets orbiting other stars — is estimated from their mass and radius. Kepler-10c, a "mega-Earth," has about 17 Earth masses and 2.35 Earth radii, giving g ≈ 30 N/kg — three times Earth's. An astronaut there would weigh triple but could never stand up. The field concept is also what lets us map the gravity of the Earth itself — the GRACE satellites measure tiny variations in g to track groundwater, ice melt, and tectonic shifts.

---

### Weight = mg: derived, not assumed
*BB 380 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Your weight is not a fundamental property — it's the gravitational force the Earth exerts on your mass. Weight = mg is not an independent fact; it follows directly from Newton's law of gravitation when you're near the surface of a planet.

**Concrete.** Start with Newton's law: F = GMm/R² for a mass m at the surface of a planet of mass M and radius R. Factor out m: F = m × (GM/R²). The term in brackets is the gravitational field strength g. So weight w = mg. For Earth: g = 9.80 N/kg. Your 70 kg body experiences w = 70 × 9.80 = 686 N. But what about apparent weight? In an accelerating lift, the scale reads normal force N. If the lift accelerates upward at 2 m/s², the net force is N − mg = ma, so N = m(g + a) = 70(9.8 + 2) = 826 N — you feel heavier. If the cable snaps (a = −g), N = 0 — you're weightless while falling.

**Definition.** **Weight** is the gravitational force exerted on a body by the planet (or other astronomical body) it rests on or near: w⃗ = mg⃗. Weight has units of newtons (N), not kilograms — kilograms measure mass, which is invariant. **Apparent weight** is the normal force exerted by a supporting surface (a scale, a floor, a seat). In a non-accelerating frame on the surface, apparent weight = true weight = mg. Under acceleration, apparent weight differs from mg. In free fall, apparent weight = 0 even though true weight = mg still acts. Mass is intrinsic; weight depends on where you are.

**In action.** The distinction between mass and weight is deadly serious. A 1 kg bag of sugar has mass 1 kg everywhere — on Earth, on the Moon, in deep space. Its weight on Earth is 9.8 N; on the Moon, 1.6 N; in deep space, nearly 0. Engineers designing spacecraft, roller coasters, and aircraft calculate in newtons, not kilograms, because the forces — not the masses — determine whether structures hold. Astronauts train in "zero-g" aircraft that fly parabolic arcs: during the 25-second free-fall segment, apparent weight drops to zero, but their mass and true weight haven't changed. They're falling with the plane.

---

### Variation of g with height: why gravity weakens as you climb
*BB 381 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Climb a mountain and you weigh slightly less. At the altitude of the International Space Station, gravity is still about 90% of surface value — astronauts float not because gravity is gone, but because they're in free fall.

**Concrete.** At height h above Earth's surface: g(h) = GM/(R + h)² = g₀ × R²/(R + h)², where g₀ = 9.80 N/kg. At the top of Mount Everest (h ≈ 8.85 km): g ≈ 9.80 × (6371/6380)² ≈ 9.77 N/kg — a drop of 0.3%, reducing a 70 kg person's weight from 686 N to about 684 N. At the ISS altitude (h ≈ 400 km): g ≈ 9.80 × (6371/6771)² ≈ 8.67 N/kg — still 88% of surface g! At 10,000 km: g ≈ 9.80 × (6371/16371)² ≈ 1.48 N/kg. For h ≪ R, use the binomial approximation: g(h) ≈ g₀(1 − 2h/R). At 100 km: g ≈ 9.80(1 − 200/6371) ≈ 9.49 N/kg.

**Definition.** **Variation of g with height:** For a spherical Earth, g(h) = GM/(R + h)² = g₀ [R/(R + h)]², where g₀ = GM/R² is the surface value. For h ≪ R (up to ~100 km), the binomial approximation gives g(h) ≈ g₀(1 − 2h/R). The fractional decrease is Δg/g₀ ≈ −2h/R. At 1 km altitude, g drops by about 0.03%. At geostationary orbit (h = 35,786 km), g ≈ g₀(6371/42157)² ≈ 0.224 N/kg — just 2.3% of surface gravity. The inverse-square law means g falls off rapidly at first, then more gradually at large distances.

**In action.** This explains the most common misunderstanding about space. "Zero gravity" doesn't exist in low Earth orbit — the ISS experiences 88% of surface gravity. Astronauts float because the ISS is constantly falling toward Earth, and they're falling with it. The same principle applies to the Vomit Comet training aircraft and to skydivers before the parachute opens. The variation with height also matters for precision: a pendulum clock calibrated at sea level runs slow in Denver (the "mile-high city," h ≈ 1600 m, g about 0.05% smaller). GPS satellites must account for both the weaker gravity at 20,200 km altitude and relativistic time dilation — without these corrections, GPS would drift by about 11 km per day.

---

### Variation of g with depth and Earth's rotation
*BB 382 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** If you could drill into the Earth, gravity would decrease — not increase. At the centre, g = 0. And because the Earth spins, you weigh slightly less at the equator than at the poles. These are two independent effects that both modify surface g.

**Concrete.** At depth d below the surface: only the mass of the sphere of radius (R − d) contributes to gravity — the outer shell cancels (Newton's shell theorem). For uniform density, g(d) = g₀(1 − d/R). At d = R/2 (halfway to centre): g = g₀/2. At the centre (d = R): g = 0. Now the rotation effect: at latitude λ, the centripetal acceleration is ω²R cos λ. The effective g' = g − ω²R cos²λ. At the equator (λ = 0°): g' = g₀ − ω²R = 9.80 − (7.27×10⁻⁵)²(6.37×10⁶) = 9.80 − 0.034 = 9.766 N/kg. At the poles (λ = 90°): g' = 9.832 N/kg. The equator-to-pole difference is about 0.066 N/kg — about 0.7% of g₀.

**Definition.** **Variation with depth:** For a uniform sphere, g(d) = g₀(1 − d/R) inside the Earth. This follows from the shell theorem: the gravitational force inside a uniform spherical shell is zero. Only the mass inside radius (R − d) contributes. **Variation with rotation:** g' = g − ω²R cos²λ, where ω = 2π/(86400 s) = 7.27 × 10⁻⁵ rad/s is Earth's angular velocity, R is Earth's radius, and λ is latitude. The correction is maximum at the equator (cos λ = 1) and zero at the poles. The actual Earth is not a perfect sphere (it bulges at the equator), which adds a further latitude-dependent correction.

**In action.** The depth variation of g was confirmed in mine shafts and the deep Homestake gold mine in South Dakota (g decreases with depth — the shell theorem works). The equatorial bulge means Earth's radius is about 21 km larger at the equator than at the poles, combining with rotation to make equatorial g about 0.5% smaller than polar g. This matters for everything from pendulum clocks to sports records — a pole-vaulter weighs less at the equator, so in principle they should jump higher there. In reality, the effect is swamped by wind, temperature, and technique, but the physics is real. The fact that g = 0 at Earth's centre means that if you fell through a tunnel through the Earth, you'd oscillate back and forth — simple harmonic motion — with a period of about 84 minutes.

---

### Gravitational potential energy: U = −GMm/r
*BB 383 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** You learned gravitational potential energy as mgh — the energy of height. That formula only works near Earth's surface, where g is roughly constant. The true potential energy of two masses separated by distance r is U = −GMm/r — negative, because gravity is attractive, and zero only at infinite separation.

**Concrete.** For a 70 kg person at Earth's surface: U = −GMm/R = −(6.67×10⁻¹¹)(5.97×10²⁴)(70)/(6.37×10⁶) = −4.38 × 10⁹ J. That's negative four billion joules — the energy you'd need to provide to escape Earth's gravity entirely. Compare with mgh: if you lift the same person by h = 100 m, mgh = 70 × 9.8 × 100 = 68,600 J. The change in U using the full formula: ΔU = −GMm/(R+h) + GMm/R. For h ≪ R, this simplifies to mgh. The mgh formula is the first-order approximation — good for skyscrapers, useless for satellites.

**Definition.** **Gravitational potential energy** for two point masses: U(r) = −GMm/r, where r is the separation and the zero of potential energy is chosen at r → ∞. The negative sign means the system is bound — energy must be added to separate the masses. For a mass m in the field of a spherical body M, the same formula applies with r measured from the centre. The change in potential energy when moving from r₁ to r₂ is ΔU = −GMm(1/r₂ − 1/r₁). For small height changes near the surface (Δr = h ≪ R), this reduces to ΔU ≈ mgh, recovering the familiar near-Earth formula.

**In action.** The negative potential energy is not a mathematical quirk — it's the reason satellites stay in orbit. A satellite with total energy E = K + U < 0 is bound to Earth; with E ≥ 0, it escapes. The transition from bound (elliptical orbit) to unbound (hyperbolic trajectory) happens at E = 0, which corresponds to the escape speed. This also explains why rockets need so much fuel: to go from Earth's surface (deep in the negative potential well) to infinity takes about 63 MJ per kilogram — the specific orbital energy. Interplanetary missions budget every metre per second of Δv against this potential well. The gravitational well of the Sun is even deeper — escaping the solar system from Earth's orbit requires 42.1 km/s relative to the Sun.

---

### Escape velocity: fast enough to leave forever
*BB 384 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Throw a ball upward and it falls back. Throw it at 11.2 km/s and it never returns. Escape velocity is the minimum speed an object needs to break free of a planet's gravity — to coast to infinity with zero remaining speed.

**Concrete.** Derivation: at escape, the total mechanical energy at infinity is zero (K = 0, U = 0). At launch (r = R, speed = v_e): ½mv_e² + (−GMm/R) = 0. Solving: v_e = √(2GM/R). For Earth: v_e = √(2 × 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 5.97×10²⁴ / 6.37×10⁶) = √(1.25×10⁸) = 11.2 km/s = 40,320 km/h. Using g: v_e = √(2gR) = √(2 × 9.8 × 6.37×10⁶) = 11.2 km/s. Escape velocity from the Moon: 2.38 km/s. From Mars: 5.03 km/s. From Jupiter: 59.5 km/s. From the Sun's surface: 618 km/s. From a black hole's event horizon: c (300,000 km/s).

**Definition.** **Escape velocity** v_e is the minimum initial speed required for an object to escape a planet's (or other body's) gravitational field — that is, to reach r → ∞ with v → 0, starting from the surface. It is given by v_e = √(2GM/R) = √(2gR). Escape velocity does *not* depend on the mass of the escaping object, nor on the direction of launch (as long as the trajectory doesn't intersect the planet). It assumes no further propulsion after launch (ballistic escape) and no atmospheric drag. The object's kinetic energy at launch must equal its gravitational binding energy: ½mv_e² = GMm/R.

**In action.** Escape velocity determines the economics of spaceflight. Low Earth orbit requires about 7.8 km/s (orbital velocity); escape from Earth requires an additional 3.4 km/s — the difference is the "escape increment." The Apollo missions accelerated to nearly Earth escape velocity, then used the Moon's gravity to slow down. The Voyager probes, launched in 1977, are on hyperbolic trajectories — they exceeded solar escape velocity from Earth's orbit and are now interstellar. For a black hole, escape velocity exceeds c at the event horizon (Schwarzschild radius r_s = 2GM/c²). Nothing, not even light, can escape from within.

---

### Kepler's First Law: planets move in ellipses
*BB 385 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** For nearly 2000 years, everyone believed orbits were circles — the "perfect" shape. Kepler, analysing Tycho Brahe's meticulous data on Mars (1609), discovered the truth: planetary orbits are ellipses, with the Sun at one focus. The circle was a beautiful lie.

**Concrete.** An ellipse with semi-major axis a and eccentricity e: the distance from the centre to each focus is ae. For Earth's orbit: a ≈ 1.496 × 10¹¹ m (1 AU), e ≈ 0.0167. The Sun is offset from the centre by ae ≈ 2.5 × 10⁹ m — only 1.67% of a. Earth is closest to the Sun (perihelion) at 147.1 million km in early January and farthest (aphelion) at 152.1 million km in early July. For Mars: e ≈ 0.0934 — more noticeably elliptical. For Halley's Comet: e ≈ 0.967 — a highly elongated ellipse, coming within 0.59 AU of the Sun and receding to 35 AU, beyond Neptune.

**Definition.** **Kepler's First Law (Law of Ellipses):** Every planet moves in an elliptical orbit with the Sun at one focus. An ellipse is defined by: the sum of distances from any point on the curve to two fixed points (foci) is constant (= 2a, the major axis). The eccentricity e measures the deviation from a circle: e = 0 gives a circle; 0 1 gives a hyperbola (flyby trajectory). Newton later derived that the 1/r² force law necessarily produces conic-section orbits — Kepler's first law is a consequence of the inverse-square attraction.

**In action.** The elliptical shape of orbits has practical consequences. Earth's Southern Hemisphere receives about 6.8% more solar radiation in its summer (perihelion occurs near the December solstice) than the Northern Hemisphere in its summer. But because the Southern Hemisphere is mostly ocean, the climate effect is moderated. Exoplanet hunters use eccentricity to infer planetary system history — high eccentricities suggest past gravitational encounters. Halley's Comet, last seen in 1986, has been observed every 75–76 years since 240 BC because its elliptical orbit is precisely determined by the same Kepler-Newton laws. It returns next in 2061.

---

### Kepler's Second Law: equal areas in equal times
*BB 386 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A planet moves faster when it's closer to the Sun and slower when it's farther away. The line from the Sun to the planet sweeps out equal areas in equal times — a natural consequence of angular momentum conservation.

**Concrete.** Earth's orbital speed varies from 30.3 km/s at perihelion (closest to Sun, early January) to 29.3 km/s at aphelion (farthest, early July). The area swept per unit time — the areal velocity — is constant: dA/dt = L/2m, where L is the angular momentum. For Earth: L/m ≈ 4.46 × 10¹⁵ m²/s, so dA/dt ≈ 2.23 × 10¹⁵ m²/s. The total area of the orbit (πab) swept in one year is consistent: π(1.496×10¹¹)(1.496×10¹¹√(1−0.0167²)) ≈ 7.03×10²² m². At 2.23×10¹⁵ m²/s, that takes 3.16×10⁷ s ≈ 365.25 days.

**Definition.** **Kepler's Second Law (Law of Equal Areas):** The radius vector from the Sun to a planet sweeps out equal areas in equal intervals of time. The **areal velocity** dA/dt = ½r²(dθ/dt) = ½r²ω is constant. This is a direct consequence of conservation of angular momentum: in a central force field (force directed toward a fixed point), torque τ⃗ = r⃗ × F⃗ = 0, so L⃗ = r⃗ × p⃗ = constant. The magnitude L = mr²ω is constant, so r²ω = constant, and dA/dt = L/2m = constant. The second law does not require an inverse-square force — it holds for *any* central force.

**In action.** Kepler's second law is why Northern Hemisphere winters are about 2 days shorter than summers — Earth moves faster near perihelion (which coincides with early January). It's also why comets seem to appear suddenly: Halley's Comet spends most of its 76-year period in the outer reaches of its orbit, then whips around the Sun in a few months. The equal-area law also governs binary star systems, where two stars orbit their common centre of mass — both sweep equal areas in equal times about the COM. And it's the principle behind gravitational slingshots: a spacecraft approaching a planet gains speed from the planet's orbital motion through the precise geometry of the encounter.

---

### Kepler's Third Law: T² ∝ a³
*BB 387 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** The square of a planet's orbital period is proportional to the cube of its semi-major axis. Planets farther from the Sun take disproportionately longer to orbit — not just because the path is longer, but because they move slower. Kepler found this pattern in 1619; Newton derived it from the inverse-square law in 1687.

**Concrete.** For a circular orbit of radius r: centripetal force = gravitational force, so mv²/r = GMm/r², giving v = √(GM/r). The period T = circumference/speed = 2πr/v = 2πr √(r/GM) = 2π √(r³/GM). Squaring: T² = (4π²/GM) r³. So T² ∝ r³ — Kepler's third law. For Earth: T = 365.25 days, r = 1 AU. For Mars: r = 1.524 AU, so T = (1.524)^(3/2) = 1.881 years — matching observation. For Jupiter: r = 5.203 AU, T = (5.203)^(3/2) = 11.86 years. For elliptical orbits, replace r with the semi-major axis a. The constant 4π²/GM depends only on the central mass — it's the same for all objects orbiting the Sun.

**Definition.** **Kepler's Third Law (Law of Periods):** T² ∝ a³, where T is the orbital period and a is the semi-major axis. For the special case of circular orbits, T² = (4π²/GM) r³. For elliptical orbits, T² = (4π²/GM) a³. The proportionality constant depends only on the central mass M and G. For the Solar System: T²(in years) = a³(in AU) when orbiting the Sun. In a two-body system, the reduced mass correction gives T² = 4π²a³/G(M + m). The third law is derivable from Newton's law of gravitation and is not independent — it confirms the inverse-square nature of gravity.

**In action.** Kepler's third law is the workhorse of astronomy. Measure a moon's orbital period and radius around its planet, and you get the planet's mass. Measure a binary star's period and separation, and you get the stars' masses. The supermassive black hole at the centre of the Milky Way (Sagittarius A*) was "weighed" by tracking the star S2, which orbits it with a period of 16 years and a semi-major axis of about 970 AU — giving M ≈ 4.1 million solar masses. The third law also predicted the existence of Neptune: perturbations in Uranus's orbit led Le Verrier and Adams to calculate where an eighth planet should be — and Galle found it in 1846, within 1° of the prediction.

---

### Orbital velocity and time period
*BB 388 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** To stay in orbit, a satellite must move fast enough that the Earth's surface curves away beneath it as fast as it falls. The orbital velocity depends only on the distance from Earth's centre — closer means faster. This is why the ISS zips around Earth in 90 minutes while the Moon takes a month.

**Concrete.** For a circular orbit: mv²/r = GMm/r² → v_orb = √(GM/r). For the ISS at h = 400 km (r = 6771 km): v_orb = √(6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 5.97×10²⁴ / 6.771×10⁶) = 7.67 km/s. Period: T = 2πr/v = 2π(6.771×10⁶)/7670 ≈ 5550 s ≈ 92.5 minutes. At the Moon's distance (r = 384,400 km): v_orb = √(GM/r) ≈ 1.02 km/s. Period: T = 2πr/v ≈ 27.3 days. At geostationary orbit (r = 42,164 km): v_orb ≈ 3.07 km/s, T = 23 h 56 min 4 s — exactly one sidereal day. Fact: orbital velocity at the surface (ignoring atmosphere) would be √(gR) = √(9.8 × 6.37×10⁶) = 7.91 km/s.

**Definition.** **Orbital velocity** v_orb for a circular orbit of radius r around a body of mass M: v_orb = √(GM/r) = √(gR²/r), where R is the planet's radius and g is surface gravity. The orbital period is T = 2πr/v_orb = 2π √(r³/GM). Note that v_esc = √2 × v_orb — escape velocity is always √2 ≈ 1.414 times the circular orbital speed at the same radius. For a satellite: v_orb decreases with increasing r (closer = faster). This is counterintuitive: to reach a higher orbit, you accelerate, but once there, you move slower than before.

**In action.** The relationship between orbital velocity and altitude governs all satellite operations. The ISS at 400 km completes 15.5 orbits per day. GPS satellites at 20,200 km orbit twice per day. The James Webb Space Telescope sits at the L2 Lagrange point, 1.5 million km from Earth, where it orbits the Sun in sync with Earth. A satellite in low Earth orbit (~200 km) orbits in about 88 minutes. Hohmann transfer orbits — the most fuel-efficient way to move between two circular orbits — use precisely calculated velocity changes (Δv) at each end: fire engines to enter an elliptical transfer orbit, then fire again to circularise at the new altitude.

---

### Energy of an orbiting satellite
*BB 389 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A satellite in a circular orbit has negative total energy — it's gravitationally bound. Remarkably, its kinetic energy is exactly half the magnitude of its potential energy. And to move to a higher orbit, you need to add energy — despite the fact that the satellite ends up moving slower.

**Concrete.** For a circular orbit of radius r: KE = ½mv² = ½m(GM/r) = GMm/2r. PE = −GMm/r. Total energy: E = KE + PE = −GMm/2r. So |KE| = |E| and |PE| = 2|E|. For the ISS (m ≈ 420,000 kg, r ≈ 6771 km): KE ≈ ½(4.2×10⁵)(7670)² ≈ 1.24 × 10¹³ J. PE ≈ −2.47 × 10¹³ J. E ≈ −1.24 × 10¹³ J. To boost the ISS from 400 km to 500 km: ΔE = E₂ − E₁ = −GMm/2r₂ + GMm/2r₁ = (GMm/2)(1/r₁ − 1/r₂). With r₁ = 6771 km, r₂ = 6871 km: ΔE ≈ +3.6 × 10¹¹ J — about 100,000 kWh, or the energy in about 8.6 tonnes of rocket fuel.

**Definition.** For a satellite of mass m in a **circular orbit** of radius r around a body of mass M (M ≫ m):

KE = ½mv² = GMm/2r

PE = −GMm/r

E_total = −GMm/2r

The total energy is negative — the satellite is bound. KE = |E|, PE = −2KE. For **elliptical orbits**, the total energy is E = −GMm/2a, where a is the semi-major axis. This is independent of eccentricity — two orbits with the same semi-major axis have the same total energy. A satellite with E 0 gives a hyperbolic flyby.

**In action.** The total energy formula explains why satellites don't stay in low Earth orbit forever — atmospheric drag does negative work, reducing E (making it more negative), which means r must decrease. The satellite spirals inward, converting gravitational potential energy to kinetic energy and heat. This is also why launching to geostationary orbit is a two-step process: first launch to a low parking orbit, then fire engines at perigee to enter an elliptical transfer orbit (raising apogee to GEO altitude), then fire again at apogee to circularise. Each burn adds energy, and the satellite ends up with higher (less negative) total energy — even though its final orbital speed at GEO (3.07 km/s) is slower than at LEO (7.67 km/s). The paradox dissolves when you realise KE is only half the story.

---

### Geostationary satellites: parked in the sky
*BB 390 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Imagine a satellite that hovers motionless in the sky — always above the same point on the equator, 24 hours a day. This isn't science fiction. It happens when the satellite's orbital period exactly matches Earth's rotation period, at a very specific altitude: 35,786 km.

**Concrete.** Condition: orbital period T = 23 h 56 min 4 s = 86,164 s (one sidereal day). From Kepler's third law: r = (GMT²/4π²)^(1/3) = [(6.67×10⁻¹¹)(5.97×10²⁴)(86164)² / 4π²]^(1/3) = (7.53×10²²)^(1/3) = 4.2164 × 10⁷ m = 42,164 km from Earth's centre. Altitude h = r − R = 42,164 − 6371 = 35,793 km ≈ 35,800 km. Orbital speed: v = 2πr/T = 2π(4.2164×10⁷)/86,164 ≈ 3,075 m/s = 3.07 km/s. The orbit must be equatorial (inclination = 0°) and circular (e = 0), with the satellite moving west to east — matching Earth's rotation direction.

**Definition.** A **geostationary orbit** (GEO) is a circular equatorial orbit with a period equal to one sidereal day, so the satellite appears stationary in the sky to an observer on the ground. The orbital radius is r = 42,164 km (altitude ~35,786 km). A **geosynchronous orbit** has the same period but may be inclined and/or elliptical — the satellite returns to the same position in the sky each day but traces a figure-8 analemma from the ground. GEO satellites are placed in the "Clarke belt" (named after Arthur C. Clarke, who proposed them in 1945). The finite capacity of the GEO arc has led to international coordination through the ITU.

**In action.** Geostationary satellites are everywhere in modern life — you just don't see them. TV broadcast satellites (DTH — direct-to-home), weather satellites (INSAT, GOES, Meteosat) that provide the cloud images on news forecasts, and some communication relays all use GEO. A single GEO satellite covers about one-third of Earth's surface; three equally spaced satellites cover nearly the entire globe (except the poles, which are out of line-of-sight). The downside: the 35,800 km distance creates a ~240 ms signal delay (round trip), which is why satellite internet has lag and why Starlink uses much lower orbits (~550 km). Launching to GEO costs significantly more than LEO — the rocket must provide enough energy to climb out of the deep part of the gravity well.

---

### Weightlessness: what it actually is
*BB 391 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Astronauts on the ISS aren't in "zero gravity" — gravity there is 88% of surface strength. They float because they are in continuous free fall. Weightlessness is not the absence of gravity; it's the absence of a supporting force. You and your spacecraft are falling together.

**Concrete.** The ISS at 400 km altitude experiences g = 8.67 N/kg. A 70 kg astronaut's true weight is still 607 N. But the scale reads zero because the scale is falling at the same acceleration as the astronaut — the scale can't push up on them because it's not supported by anything either. Apparent weight w_app = m(g − a). When a = g (free fall), w_app = 0. The same effect happens in a freely falling lift, a parabolic-flight aircraft (the "Vomit Comet"), or during the first moments of a skydive. The sensation isn't "no gravity" — it's "no normal force." Your internal organs float because they're no longer compressed against each other.

**Definition.** **Weightlessness** (or microgravity) is the condition where the apparent weight of an object is zero or nearly zero. It occurs when an object is in free fall — accelerating at g⃗ with no other forces (like normal force or tension) acting perpendicular to the fall direction. True weight w = mg still acts; the object is weightless only in the operational sense (a scale reads zero). In orbit, weightlessness is not because r is large (gravity still ~90% of surface) but because the spacecraft and everything in it are in continuous free fall around Earth. The term "microgravity" reflects the fact that tiny residual forces (atmospheric drag, spacecraft vibrations, gravity gradient across the craft) produce apparent accelerations on the order of 10⁻⁶ g.

**In action.** Weightlessness is both a challenge and an opportunity. Astronauts' bones lose density (~1% per month) because the skeleton is no longer resisting weight — the body adapts to the absence of load. Muscles atrophy. The vestibular system (inner ear balance) goes haywire, causing space sickness. But weightlessness also enables unique science: protein crystals grow larger and more perfectly without convection; combustion behaves differently without buoyancy-driven airflow; fluids form perfect spheres. The ISS is a national laboratory for microgravity research. And the romantic vision of "floating in space" is real — just not for the reason most people think. You float because you are falling, endlessly, and so is your ship.

---

### Tidal forces: why the ocean has two bulges
*BB 392 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** The Moon pulls on the Earth — but it pulls harder on the near side than on the far side, and harder on the centre than on the far side. This differential stretching is the tidal force. It produces two ocean bulges: one facing the Moon (pulled toward it) and one on the opposite side (left behind as Earth is pulled away).

**Concrete.** The gravitational force from the Moon at Earth's centre: F_c = GM_m M_E / r², where r = 384,400 km. At the near point (r − R_E): F_near = GM_m M_E / (r − R_E)². The tidal force is the difference: ΔF = F_near − F_c ≈ 2GM_m M_E R_E / r³. Numerically: the Moon's tidal force on Earth is about 1.13 × 10⁻⁷ N/kg per metre — tiny, but applied across an ocean basin thousands of kilometres wide, it adds up. The Sun's tidal force is about 0.46 times the Moon's (the Sun is 27 million times more massive but 390 times farther away — and tidal forces fall off as 1/r³, not 1/r²). Spring tides (Sun and Moon aligned) produce the highest tides; neap tides (Sun and Moon at right angles) produce the lowest.

**Definition.** **Tidal forces** arise from the *difference* in gravitational pull across an extended body. For a body of size d at distance r from a mass M, the tidal acceleration scales as Δa ≈ 2GMd/r³. Unlike the direct gravitational force (∝ 1/r²), tidal forces fall off as **1/r³**. The two-bulge pattern is a consequence of the differential: the near side is pulled more strongly toward M than the centre is; the far side is pulled less strongly than the centre, so relative to the centre it appears pushed outward. The result is stretching along the Earth-Moon line and compression perpendicular to it.

**In action.** Tidal forces shape the universe. The Moon's tidal friction slows Earth's rotation (the day lengthens by about 1.7 ms per century) and pushes the Moon outward (about 3.8 cm/year — measured by lunar laser ranging). Io, Jupiter's innermost large moon, is heated by tidal flexing so intensely that it's the most volcanically active body in the Solar System. Tidal forces can tear a moon apart if it ventures inside its planet's Roche limit — the rings of Saturn may be the debris of a destroyed moon. On a more extreme scale, a black hole's tidal forces (spaghettification) would stretch an approaching astronaut into a thin strand long before they reached the event horizon. The 1/r³ scaling means small r packs a devastating differential.

---

## 6. Materials, fluids & machines

### Why a crowbar works: the lever
*BB 575 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** You already know how a crowbar works. You wedge it under a heavy rock, push down on the long end, and the rock lifts. You don't need physics to use one — but physics tells you exactly how much easier the job gets. A lever trades distance for force: push a long way with a small force, and the short end moves a short way with a big force. The ratio is the mechanical advantage.

**Concrete.** You use a 1.5 m crowbar to lift a 300 kg rock. The fulcrum (the pivot point) is 0.15 m from the rock. The effort arm (your end to the fulcrum) is 1.35 m. The load arm (rock to fulcrum) is 0.15 m. The mechanical advantage = effort arm ÷ load arm = 1.35 ÷ 0.15 = 9. So you only need to push with 300 ÷ 9 = 33.3 kg of force — about the weight of a large bag of dog food. Without the lever, you'd need to lift the full 300 kg. The price: you have to push your end down 9 cm to lift the rock 1 cm. Force × distance is conserved — the work in equals the work out (ignoring friction).

**Definition.** A **lever** is a rigid bar that rotates around a fixed point — the **fulcrum**. The **mechanical advantage** (MA) is the ratio of the output force to the input force: MA = F_out / F_in. For an ideal lever (no friction): MA = effort arm / load arm. Three classes: Class 1 (fulcrum between effort and load — crowbar, seesaw), Class 2 (load between fulcrum and effort — wheelbarrow, bottle opener), Class 3 (effort between fulcrum and load — tweezers, cricket bat). Class 3 levers *reduce* force but increase speed and range — your forearm is a Class 3 lever, and that's why you can throw a ball fast.

**In action.** Everyday levers include scissors (two Class 1 levers joined at the fulcrum), a bottle opener (Class 2 — the fulcrum is the far edge of the cap, the load is the cap itself), and your own jaw (the temporomandibular joint is a Class 3 lever with the muscle providing the effort between the joint and the teeth). The principle scales: a construction crane is a lever system; the boom is the effort arm, the counterweight balances the load. The ancient Egyptians used levers to position stones weighing tonnes. Archimedes, who formalised the lever principle around 250 BC, famously said: "Give me a place to stand, and I will move the Earth." He was exaggerating — the effort arm would need to be quadrillions of kilometres long — but the principle was sound.

---

### Pulleys: trading rope for muscle
*BB 576 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A single pulley just changes the direction of your pull — no mechanical advantage. Add a second pulley, and suddenly you can lift twice the weight with the same effort. A third pulley triples it. Pulleys multiply force by sharing the load across multiple rope segments. Every extra strand of rope supporting the load is an extra helper sharing the weight.

**Concrete.** You need to lift a 200 kg engine block. With a simple pulley hung from the ceiling: you pull down on one rope, the engine goes up — MA = 1, no advantage, just a direction change. With two pulleys (one at the ceiling, one attached to the engine): the rope goes from ceiling to engine to ceiling to your hand. The engine is now supported by *two* rope segments, so each carries half the weight. You pull with 100 kg of force, but you have to pull 2 metres of rope to lift the engine 1 metre. With a four-pulley block-and-tackle: four rope segments support the load, MA = 4, you pull 4 metres to lift 1 metre. The trade is always the same: less force for more rope.

**Definition.** A **pulley** is a wheel on an axle designed to support movement of a rope or cable. A **fixed pulley** changes direction only (MA = 1). A **movable pulley** is attached to the load and provides mechanical advantage equal to the number of rope segments supporting the load. The **block and tackle** combines multiple fixed and movable pulleys. For an ideal system (no friction): MA = number of supporting rope strands. With friction, actual MA is less. The **velocity ratio** (VR) is the distance the effort moves divided by the distance the load moves — always equal to the number of rope strands. Efficiency = MA / VR × 100%. Pulleys are levers in disguise: each pulley redirects the rope tension, effectively giving you multiple "effort arms" working simultaneously.

**In action.** Cranes use multi-pulley systems to lift tonnes with a manageable motor. A theatre's fly system (the ropes and pulleys above the stage) lets a single stagehand raise and lower heavy backdrops. Elevators use pulleys and counterweights — the counterweight balances the car, so the motor only has to overcome the difference. Sailing ships use pulleys (blocks) everywhere — the mainsheet, the halyard, the boom vang. The phrase "block and tackle" comes from sailing. In rock climbing, a Z-pulley system (three pulleys arranged in a Z-shape) gives MA = 3 and can haul an injured climber out of a crevasse using only human muscle. The oldest pulley systems date to Archimedes and the ancient Greeks.

---

### The inclined plane and the screw
*BB 577 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Pushing a heavy barrel up a ramp is easier than lifting it straight up. The longer and gentler the ramp, the less force you need. A screw is an inclined plane wrapped around a cylinder — every turn of the screw advances it by one thread's width. Both trade distance for force: push further, push easier.

**Concrete.** You roll a 100 kg barrel onto a truck bed 1.2 m high using a 4.8 m ramp. Without the ramp: you'd have to lift 100 kg straight up 1.2 m — work = 100 × 9.8 × 1.2 = 1176 J. With the ramp: the force needed to push it up (ignoring friction) = weight × height / ramp length = 980 × 1.2 / 4.8 = 245 N — about 25 kg of effort. MA = ramp length / height = 4.8 / 1.2 = 4. You push four times further, but with a quarter of the force.

A screw with pitch 2 mm (the distance between adjacent threads) turned by a screwdriver handle of radius 20 mm: in one full turn, the screwdriver rim travels 2π × 20 = 125.7 mm, while the screw advances only 2 mm. MA = distance effort moves / distance load moves = 125.7 / 2 ≈ 63. A screw gives enormous mechanical advantage — which is why a small screw can hold hundreds of kilograms.

**Definition.** An **inclined plane** gives MA = length of slope / height. The shallower the angle, the greater the MA. A **screw** is an inclined plane wrapped around a cylinder: MA = 2πr / pitch, where r is the handle radius and pitch is the distance between adjacent threads. The **jackscrew** (car jack) combines a lever (the handle) with a screw — total MA = (2π × handle length / pitch) × (any lever advantage). The **wedge** is a double inclined plane — an axe splits wood by converting a downward blow into outward force. All three are "force multipliers" that convert a small input force applied over a long distance into a large output force over a short distance.

**In action.** The screw is everywhere. A C-clamp uses a screw to exert hundreds of kilograms of clamping force from a few finger-turns. A car jack lifts a tonne with a handle you can turn with one hand. Wood screws and machine bolts hold your furniture, your bicycle, and the wings on an aeroplane together — each one is a tiny inclined plane converting torque into immense axial force. The thread pitch determines the tradeoff: fine threads (small pitch) give higher MA but are slower; coarse threads (large pitch) are faster but hold less. The Romans used wooden screws to drain mines; Archimedes' screw, invented around 250 BC, is still used today to pump water in irrigation systems.

---

### Efficiency: why some machines waste your effort
*BB 578 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** No machine is perfect. Some of the work you put in is always lost — to friction, to heat, to bending and stretching. Efficiency tells you how much of your input actually becomes useful output. A 100% efficient machine is impossible; a 90% efficient machine is excellent; a 50% efficient machine wastes half your effort. The rest becomes heat.

**Concrete.** You use a pulley system to lift a 500 N weight. The theoretical MA is 4, so the ideal effort is 500/4 = 125 N. But when you pull, you actually need 160 N — the extra 35 N overcomes friction in the pulley bearings and the stiffness of the rope. Efficiency = (ideal effort / actual effort) × 100% = (125/160) × 100% = 78%. Alternatively: output work = 500 N × 1 m = 500 J (if you lift 1 m). Input work = 160 N × 4 m = 640 J (because MA = 4, you pull 4 m of rope). Efficiency = output/input × 100% = 500/640 × 100% = 78%. Same number, two routes. The 140 J difference becomes heat — you can feel it if you touch the pulley after heavy use.

**Definition.** **Efficiency** η = (useful work output / total work input) × 100% = (actual MA / ideal MA) × 100%. For any machine: η < 100% because of friction. In a series of machines (compound machine): total efficiency = η₁ × η₂ × η₃ × ... — losses multiply. For a lever, efficiency is typically 90–98% (very high, only pivot friction). For a screw jack, 30–50% (high friction between threads). For a car engine, ~25–30% (most energy lost as heat in the exhaust and coolant). The second law of thermodynamics guarantees that some energy is always degraded into unusable heat — 100% efficiency is forbidden by physics, not just by engineering.

**In action.** Efficiency determines fuel economy. A petrol engine at 25% efficiency means 75% of the energy in every litre of fuel becomes heat, not motion. An electric motor is 85–95% efficient — one reason EVs use less energy per kilometre. A bicycle chain drive is ~98% efficient — almost all your leg power reaches the wheel. An incandescent light bulb is ~5% efficient at producing visible light; an LED is ~40%. The entire industrial revolution was a story of improving efficiency — Watt's steam engine (1776) was about 3% efficient; modern combined-cycle gas turbines exceed 60%. Efficiency is the accountant of physics: it tells you where the energy really went.

---

### The hydraulic press: multiplying force with fluid
*BB 579 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Push on a small piston with your hand, and a large piston on the other side lifts a car. No levers, no pulleys — just fluid in a sealed container. Pascal's principle: pressure applied to a confined fluid is transmitted equally in all directions. A small force on a small area creates a pressure; that same pressure on a large area creates a large force.

**Concrete.** A hydraulic press has a small piston of diameter 2 cm (area = π × 1² ≈ 3.14 cm²) and a large piston of diameter 20 cm (area = π × 10² ≈ 314 cm²). You push the small piston with 100 N (about 10 kg). The pressure created: P = F/A = 100 N / 3.14 cm² = 31.8 N/cm². This same pressure acts on the large piston: F_out = P × A_large = 31.8 × 314 ≈ 10,000 N — enough to lift about 1000 kg. The mechanical advantage = A_large / A_small = 314 / 3.14 = 100. But you have to push the small piston 100 cm to lift the large piston 1 cm. Volume is conserved: A₁ × d₁ = A₂ × d₂.

**Definition.** **Pascal's principle:** Pressure applied to an enclosed fluid is transmitted undiminished to every part of the fluid and to the walls of the container. For a hydraulic system with two pistons: P₁ = P₂ → F₁/A₁ = F₂/A₂ → F₂ = F₁ × (A₂/A₁). The **mechanical advantage** = A₂/A₁ (area ratio). The **distance trade:** d₂ = d₁ × (A₁/A₂). The **volume** of fluid displaced on one side equals the volume received on the other: A₁d₁ = A₂d₂. The fluid is typically oil (incompressible, lubricates, doesn't corrode). Hydraulic systems are force multipliers that use fluid instead of rigid linkages — they can transmit force around corners and through flexible hoses.

**In action.** Hydraulics are everywhere force needs to be multiplied. A car brake system: your foot pushes a small master cylinder piston (~2 cm²); the pressure travels through brake fluid to calliper pistons at each wheel (~20 cm² total), multiplying the force roughly 10×. A hydraulic car jack lifts a tonne with a few pumps of a small lever. Excavators and bulldozers use hydraulic rams — the engine drives a pump, and the pressurised fluid moves pistons that can exert tonnes of force. Aircraft landing gear, suspension bridges, and even your office chair (the height adjustment) use hydraulics. The principle scales: the largest hydraulic press ever built, at the Shibaura plant in Japan, exerts 80,000 tonnes of force — enough to forge a ship's propeller shaft from a single steel ingot.

---

### Archimedes' principle: why things float
*BB 580 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Put a stone in water and it sinks. Put a log in water and it floats. The difference is density — but the mechanism is buoyancy. Any object submerged in a fluid experiences an upward force equal to the weight of the fluid it pushes aside. If that upthrust is greater than the object's weight, it floats. If not, it sinks. Archimedes figured this out in his bathtub around 250 BC — and according to legend, ran naked through the streets shouting "Eureka!"

**Concrete.** A block of wood 0.30 m × 0.20 m × 0.10 m (volume = 0.006 m³) is placed in water (density ρ = 1000 kg/m³). When fully submerged, it displaces 0.006 m³ of water. The weight of displaced water = ρVg = 1000 × 0.006 × 9.8 = 58.8 N. This is the buoyant force. The wood's mass is 3.6 kg (density 600 kg/m³), so its weight is 3.6 × 9.8 = 35.3 N. Since buoyant force (58.8 N) > weight (35.3 N), the wood rises until it's only partially submerged — at equilibrium, the submerged volume is just enough to displace water weighing 35.3 N. Submerged fraction = density_object / density_fluid = 600/1000 = 0.60 — so 60% of the block is underwater, 40% above.

**Definition.** **Archimedes' principle:** A body wholly or partially immersed in a fluid experiences an upward buoyant force equal to the weight of the fluid it displaces. F_buoyant = ρ_fluid × V_displaced × g. The object floats if ρ_object ρ_fluid; is neutrally buoyant (stays at any depth) if ρ_object = ρ_fluid. The fraction submerged = ρ_object / ρ_fluid. The principle applies to gases as well — a helium balloon rises because helium is less dense than air. The buoyant force acts through the **centre of buoyancy** (the centroid of the displaced volume), which may not coincide with the centre of gravity — this offset creates the righting moment that keeps ships upright.

**In action.** Archimedes' principle governs everything that floats. A cargo ship weighing 200,000 tonnes displaces 200,000 tonnes of seawater — the hull is shaped to spread the displacement over a huge volume. Submarines adjust buoyancy by flooding or blowing ballast tanks, changing their average density. Hot-air balloons rise because heated air is less dense than cold air. Icebergs float with about 90% of their volume submerged — because ice (917 kg/m³) is only slightly less dense than seawater (1025 kg/m³). The human body, with lungs full of air, is slightly less dense than water (about 985 kg/m³) — most people float. Exhale completely, and you'll sink. The specific gravity test (comparing density to water) was allegedly how Archimedes proved a crown was not pure gold — the goldsmith had mixed in silver, which is less dense, so the crown displaced more water than pure gold of the same weight.

---

### Bernoulli's principle: why planes fly and chimneys draw
*BB 581 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Blow across the top of a piece of paper, and it lifts. Squeeze a garden hose nozzle, and the water shoots faster but with less pressure against the walls. Bernoulli's principle: where a fluid moves faster, its pressure drops. This is not a separate law — it's conservation of energy applied to fluid flow: kinetic energy + pressure energy + potential energy = constant along a streamline.

**Concrete.** Water flows through a horizontal pipe that narrows from diameter 10 cm (area A₁ = 78.5 cm²) to diameter 5 cm (A₂ = 19.6 cm²). Continuity: A₁v₁ = A₂v₂, so if v₁ = 2 m/s, then v₂ = 2 × (78.5/19.6) = 8 m/s — four times faster. Bernoulli: P₁ + ½ρv₁² = P₂ + ½ρv₂² (horizontal, same height). With ρ = 1000 kg/m³: P₁ + ½(1000)(4) = P₂ + ½(1000)(64). P₁ + 2000 = P₂ + 32,000. P₁ − P₂ = 30,000 Pa — the pressure in the wide section is 30 kPa higher. If P₁ is atmospheric (101 kPa), P₂ is 71 kPa — low enough to pull fluid from a side tube. This is how a Venturi meter measures flow rate: the pressure difference tells you the speed.

**Definition.** **Bernoulli's principle:** For steady, incompressible, inviscid flow along a streamline: P + ½ρv² + ρgh = constant. The three terms are pressure energy, kinetic energy, and potential energy — all per unit volume (J/m³). **Consequences:** as velocity increases, pressure decreases (and vice versa). The principle explains: aerofoil lift (faster air over the curved top → lower pressure → upward force), the curveball (spinning ball drags air faster on one side → pressure difference → sideways force), the atomiser (fast air across a tube draws liquid up), and chimney draft (wind across the top lowers pressure, drawing smoke up). **Limitations:** Bernoulli ignores viscosity and compressibility — real fluids have friction, and at high speeds, compressibility matters.

**In action.** Bernoulli explains why your shower curtain billows inward when you turn on the water — the fast-moving water-air mixture has lower pressure than the still air outside. It explains why a roof can lift off in a hurricane — wind racing over the roof creates low pressure above, and the higher pressure inside pushes the roof upward. Pitot tubes on aircraft measure airspeed by comparing stagnation pressure (½ρv²) to static pressure — a direct application of Bernoulli. In medicine, a Venturi mask delivers precise oxygen concentrations by using a fast oxygen jet to entrain room air. The principle is one of the most elegant in physics: three forms of energy — pressure, motion, height — trading off against each other while their sum never changes.

---

### Stress and strain: the language of materials
*BB 582 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Pull on a rubber band and it stretches. Pull on a steel rod and it stretches too — just so little you can't see it. Every material deforms under force. Stress is the force per unit area; strain is the resulting deformation as a fraction of the original dimension. Stress is the cause; strain is the effect. The ratio tells you how stiff the material is.

**Concrete.** A steel cable 5.0 m long, cross-sectional area 2.0 cm² (2.0 × 10⁻⁴ m²), supports a 2000 kg load. The tensile stress = F/A = (2000 × 9.8) / (2.0 × 10⁻⁴) = 19,600 / 2.0×10⁻⁴ = 9.8 × 10⁷ Pa = 98 MPa. The steel stretches by 2.45 mm (measured). Tensile strain = ΔL/L₀ = 0.00245 / 5.0 = 4.9 × 10⁻⁴ = 0.049%. Compare with a rubber band of the same dimensions under the same load: the rubber stretches by maybe 2 metres — strain ≈ 0.4 = 40%, about 800 times more. The stress is the same; the strain is dramatically different because steel is far stiffer.

**Definition.** **Stress** σ = F/A (units: pascal, Pa = N/m²). Three types: **tensile** (pulling apart), **compressive** (pushing together), **shear** (sliding layers past each other). **Strain** ε is dimensionless: tensile strain = ΔL/L₀ (increase in length / original length), compressive strain = ΔV/V₀ (decrease in volume / original volume), shear strain = x/y (displacement / perpendicular distance). Stress and strain are related by the material's stiffness — see BB 708 for the modulus. For most materials at small strains, stress ∝ strain (Hooke's law). Units note: 1 MPa = 10⁶ Pa ≈ 10 atmospheres of pressure. Atmospheric pressure is about 0.1 MPa; structural steel yields around 250 MPa; diamond can withstand over 100 GPa in compression.

**In action.** Every structure is a stress-and-strain problem. The cables of a suspension bridge are under tensile stress — they're being pulled apart. The columns of a building are under compressive stress — they're being crushed. The rivets and bolts are under shear stress — they're being sliced. Engineers calculate the maximum stress each component will experience and choose a material that can handle it with a safety margin. The human body has its own stress limits: bone (compressive strength ~170 MPa), tendon (tensile strength ~50–100 MPa), skin (~5–20 MPa). When you run, your tibia experiences compressive stresses of about 5–10 MPa — well within its limit. A hard landing from a jump can briefly reach 50 MPa, which is why stress fractures happen.

---

### Young's modulus: why steel is stiffer than rubber
*BB 583 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Steel is stiff. Rubber is floppy. The number that captures this is Young's modulus — the ratio of stress to strain in the elastic region. A high modulus means the material barely deforms under load. A low modulus means it stretches easily. Young's modulus is a material property, like density or melting point — it doesn't depend on the shape or size of the object.

**Concrete.** A steel rod (A = 1.0 cm² = 10⁻⁴ m², L = 2.0 m) is loaded with 500 kg. F = 500 × 9.8 = 4900 N. Stress = 4900 / 10⁻⁴ = 4.9 × 10⁷ Pa. Measured stretch = 0.49 mm. Strain = 0.00049 / 2.0 = 2.45 × 10⁻⁴. Young's modulus E = stress / strain = 4.9×10⁷ / 2.45×10⁻⁴ = 2.0 × 10¹¹ Pa = 200 GPa. That's steel.

Compare common materials (E in GPa): Diamond = 1200, Steel = 200, Aluminium = 70, Glass = 70, Wood (along grain) = 10, Bone = 15, Nylon = 3, Rubber = 0.01–0.1. Steel is 2000–20,000 times stiffer than rubber. A steel wire 1 mm² under 10 N stretches 0.05%; a rubber band of the same dimensions would stretch 10,000% — impossible, it would snap first.

**Definition.** **Young's modulus** E (also called the elastic modulus or tensile modulus) is the ratio of tensile stress to tensile strain in the linear elastic region: E = σ/ε = (F/A)/(ΔL/L₀). Units: Pa (typically GPa for structural materials). **Hooke's law in engineering form:** F/A = E × (ΔL/L₀), or equivalently F = (EA/L₀) × ΔL. The term EA/L₀ is the **spring constant** k of the specific specimen — unlike E, it depends on geometry. A thick, short rod is stiffer than a thin, long one of the same material. Young's modulus applies only in the elastic region, where removing the load returns the material to its original length. Beyond the yield point, the material deforms permanently (plastic deformation).

**In action.** Young's modulus determines material selection in every engineering design. Aircraft wings are aluminium (E = 70 GPa) — stiff enough to hold shape, light enough to fly. A bicycle frame is often steel or carbon fibre — the tube diameter and wall thickness are chosen to give the right stiffness without excess weight. Concrete has E ≈ 30 GPa in compression but is weak in tension — that's why reinforced concrete uses steel rebar (E = 200 GPa) to carry the tensile loads. The Eiffel Tower (wrought iron, E ≈ 190 GPa) was designed so that the wind load produces a maximum stress of about 10% of the material's yield strength — the safety factor is 10. A violin string (steel, E ≈ 200 GPa) is tuned by adjusting its tension: higher tension = higher pitch, but also higher stress — too close to yield and it snaps.

---

### Ultimate strength: when things break
*BB 584 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Every material has a breaking point. Pull hard enough on anything and it snaps. The ultimate strength is the maximum stress a material can withstand before it fails. Engineers design structures to operate at a fraction of this — the safety factor — because real loads are unpredictable, materials have flaws, and failure is usually unacceptable.

**Concrete.** A steel bolt of diameter 10 mm (area = π × 5² = 78.5 mm² = 7.85 × 10⁻⁵ m²) is loaded in tension. Steel's ultimate tensile strength is about 500 MPa. Maximum load before breaking: F_max = σ_ult × A = 500 × 10⁶ × 7.85 × 10⁻⁵ = 39,250 N — about 4 tonnes. With a safety factor of 5, the allowable working load is 39,250 / 5 = 7,850 N (~800 kg). The bolt will hold 800 kg reliably, every day, for decades.
