Common ultimate strengths (MPa): Steel (structural) = 400–550, Aluminium alloy = 300–500, Titanium = 900, Copper = 200, Wood (pine, along grain) = 40 in tension, 30 in compression, Concrete = 20–60 in compression (essentially 0 in tension), Glass = 30–100 (but brittle — fails without warning), Kevlar = 3600, Carbon fibre = 4000–7000, Spider silk = 1000–1500 (and also remarkably tough — absorbs energy without snapping).

**Definition.** **Ultimate tensile strength** (UTS) is the maximum stress a material can withstand while being stretched before necking and fracture. **Yield strength** is the stress at which permanent (plastic) deformation begins — typically 50–80% of UTS for metals. **Compressive strength** is the maximum stress under compression. Brittle materials (glass, ceramic, concrete) have high compressive strength but low tensile strength and fail suddenly. Ductile materials (steel, copper) stretch and neck before breaking — they give warning. **Safety factor** = ultimate strength / allowable working stress. Typical values: 1.5–2 for aircraft (weight-critical), 3–5 for buildings, 5–10 for elevators and bridges, 10+ for critical components.

**In action.** The safety factor is why bridges don't collapse. The Golden Gate Bridge's main cables are made of 27,572 individual steel wires, each 5 mm in diameter, with a combined ultimate strength of about 1.5 million tonnes — far above the bridge's own weight plus maximum traffic load. The Titanic's hull steel had a high sulphur content that made it brittle in cold water — the impact with the iceberg produced stresses that exceeded the steel's reduced ultimate strength at −2°C. Modern ship steel is specified for low-temperature toughness. In medicine, hip implants use titanium (UTS ~900 MPa, biocompatible) with a safety factor that accounts for millions of loading cycles over decades — fatigue failure, not just peak stress, becomes the limiting factor.

---

### Bulk modulus and shear modulus: the other stiffnesses
*BB 585 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Young's modulus measures resistance to stretching. But materials can be deformed in other ways — squeezed uniformly from all sides (bulk modulus) or twisted (shear modulus). A diamond is hard to scratch (high hardness) but also hard to compress (high bulk modulus). Jelly is easy to shear (low shear modulus) but resists compression (moderate bulk modulus — it's mostly water).

**Concrete.** **Bulk modulus B:** Apply uniform pressure ΔP to a material. Its volume shrinks by ΔV. B = −ΔP / (ΔV/V₀). The negative sign makes B positive (pressure increase → volume decrease). For water: B ≈ 2.2 GPa — it takes 2.2 GPa (22,000 atmospheres) to compress water by 1% of its volume. Water is nearly incompressible. For steel: B ≈ 160 GPa. For air at atmospheric pressure: B ≈ 0.00014 GPa — air is 10,000–100,000 times more compressible than liquids and solids.

**Shear modulus G:** Apply a shear stress τ = F/A parallel to a face. The material distorts by angle γ (in radians). G = τ/γ. For steel: G ≈ 80 GPa. For rubber: G ≈ 0.001 GPa. For jelly: G ≈ 0.0001 GPa. The three moduli are related: for isotropic materials, E = 2G(1+ν) = 3B(1−2ν), where ν is Poisson's ratio (≈ 0.3 for most metals).

**Definition.** **Bulk modulus** B = −V₀ (ΔP/ΔV) measures resistance to uniform compression. The reciprocal 1/B is **compressibility**. **Shear modulus** G (also called rigidity modulus) = τ/γ measures resistance to shape change at constant volume. Both are measured in Pa. A material can be stiff in compression but compliant in shear: cork (B ≈ 0.05 GPa, G ≈ 0.002 GPa) resists volume change (good for bottle stoppers) but shears easily. Liquids and gases have G = 0 — they cannot support a shear stress at rest; they flow. The speed of sound depends on these moduli: c_longitudinal = √((B + 4G/3)/ρ) in solids, c = √(B/ρ) in fluids.

**In action.** The bulk modulus explains why hydraulic systems use oil, not air — oil's high bulk modulus means the force is transmitted immediately; air would compress and absorb the motion. Submarine hulls must withstand enormous uniform pressure — at 1000 m depth, ΔP ≈ 10 MPa, and the hull's bulk modulus determines how much it shrinks. Seismic waves come in two types: P-waves (compression, governed by B + 4G/3, faster) and S-waves (shear, governed by G, slower). The fact that S-waves don't travel through Earth's outer core tells us it's liquid (G = 0). The shear modulus is also why a steel spring works — it's actually twisting the wire (shear), not stretching it.

---

### Boyle's law: squeezing gases
*BB 586 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Take a bicycle pump, block the outlet with your thumb, and push. The air resists, and the harder you push, the smaller the trapped air becomes. Boyle's law: for a fixed mass of gas at constant temperature, pressure and volume are inversely proportional. Squeeze the volume to half, and the pressure doubles. Release it, and it springs back. Gases are springs made of molecules.

**Concrete.** A cylinder contains 2.0 L of air at atmospheric pressure (101 kPa). The piston is pushed in until the volume is 0.5 L — one quarter of the original. Temperature stays constant (slow compression, heat can escape). Boyle's law: P₁V₁ = P₂V₂ → 101 × 2.0 = P₂ × 0.5 → P₂ = 404 kPa — about 4 atmospheres. The force on the piston (area 10 cm² = 0.001 m²): F = P × A = 404,000 × 0.001 = 404 N — about 41 kg. Your arm has to push that hard. Real compressors get hot — in rapid compression, the gas heats up (adiabatic), and pressure rises even more. Boyle's law assumes temperature stays constant — it's the isothermal case.

**Definition.** **Boyle's law:** For a fixed mass of gas at constant temperature, P ∝ 1/V, or PV = constant. Equivalently: P₁V₁ = P₂V₂. The law holds for **ideal gases** and is a good approximation for real gases at moderate pressures and temperatures. It is a consequence of the kinetic theory: pressure comes from molecules colliding with the walls. Halving the volume doubles the number of collisions per unit area per second — hence double the pressure. Boyle discovered the law in 1662 using a J-shaped glass tube and mercury. The law combines with Charles's law (V ∝ T) and Gay-Lussac's law (P ∝ T) to give the ideal gas law PV = nRT.

**In action.** Boyle's law is why your ears pop on an aeroplane. As the plane climbs, cabin pressure drops; the air trapped in your middle ear expands according to PV = constant, pushing against your eardrum until you swallow and equalise. Scuba divers live by Boyle's law: at 10 m depth, pressure is 2 atmospheres, so a lungful of air occupies half the volume it does at the surface. Ascending while holding your breath would cause the expanding air to rupture your lungs — the most important rule in diving is "never hold your breath." In medicine, a syringe draws fluid by increasing the volume inside (lowering pressure), creating suction. The humble bicycle pump is a Boyle's law machine: muscle force creates pressure; pressure reduces volume; the compressed air flows into the tyre.

---

### Pascal's hydrostatic paradox: pressure depends only on depth
*BB 587 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Fill a narrow tube and a wide barrel to the same height with water. The pressure at the bottom is exactly the same in both — even though the barrel contains far more water. Hydrostatic pressure depends only on depth and fluid density, not on the shape or total volume of the container. This is Pascal's paradox — counterintuitive but mathematically inevitable.

**Concrete.** A swimming pool 2.0 m deep. At the bottom, gauge pressure P = ρgh = 1000 × 9.8 × 2.0 = 19,600 Pa ≈ 0.19 atmospheres. The total force on the bottom (area 50 m²): F = P × A = 19,600 × 50 = 980,000 N — about 100 tonnes. The force on the side wall at the same 2.0 m depth: same pressure per unit area, but the pressure varies with depth — zero at the surface, maximum at the bottom. The average pressure on a vertical wall is ρgh/2, acting at a point 2h/3 from the surface.

Now the paradox: a narrow vertical pipe 10 m tall, 1 cm² in cross-section, filled with water. Pressure at the bottom: P = 1000 × 9.8 × 10 = 98,000 Pa. Force on the bottom plug: F = 98,000 × 0.0001 = 9.8 N — about 1 kg. But that same pressure, if connected to a wide chamber, could lift a car. The force multiplies with area, but the pressure is set by height alone.

**Definition.** **Hydrostatic pressure** at depth h in a fluid of density ρ: P = P₀ + ρgh, where P₀ is the pressure at the surface (usually atmospheric). The ρgh term is the **gauge pressure**. Key fact: pressure at a given depth is **independent of container shape** — it depends only on the vertical distance from the free surface. This follows from the fact that pressure in a static fluid must be the same at all points at the same horizontal level; otherwise, fluid would flow sideways until it equalised. The force on a submerged surface is the integral of pressure over area — for a vertical rectangle, F = ρg × (width) × (h²/2).

**In action.** Hydrostatic pressure is why dams are thicker at the bottom than at the top — the pressure increases linearly with depth, and the structural requirement follows. A dam 100 m tall experiences gauge pressure of about 1 MPa at its base — roughly 10 atmospheres. Water towers use height, not pumps, to maintain pressure: a tower 30 m tall provides about 300 kPa (3 atm, ~44 psi) at the tap. The city water supply is pressurised by gravity, not electricity — which is why water still flows during a power outage. Deep-sea fish are adapted to pressures of 100 MPa at 10,000 m depth — a thousand atmospheres. Bring them to the surface, and the dissolved gases in their tissues expand according to Boyle's law, killing them. The pressure at the bottom of the Mariana Trench (11 km) would crush a human instantly.

---

### Bending and twisting: why I-beams work
*BB 588 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A wooden plank laid flat bends easily under your weight. Turn it on edge, and it barely flexes at all. Same material, same cross-sectional area — completely different stiffness. Bending resistance depends on the shape of the cross-section, not just its area. An I-beam puts most of its material far from the neutral axis, where it does the most work resisting bending.

**Concrete.** A simply supported beam of length L = 3.0 m carries a central load F = 2000 N. Material: steel (E = 200 GPa). Two cross-sections with the same area (20 cm²):

Case A — solid square, 4.47 cm × 4.47 cm. Second moment of area I = bh³/12 = (0.0447)(0.0447)³/12 = 3.33 × 10⁻⁷ m⁴. Maximum deflection: δ = FL³/(48EI) = 2000 × 27 / (48 × 2×10¹¹ × 3.33×10⁻⁷) = 54,000 / 3,196,800 ≈ 0.0169 m = 16.9 mm.

Case B — I-beam, 10 cm deep, flanges 2 cm × 10 cm, web 6 cm × 0.5 cm. I ≈ 4.5 × 10⁻⁶ m⁴ — roughly 13.5 times larger. Deflection: 16.9/13.5 ≈ 1.25 mm. The I-beam is 13× stiffer with the same weight.

For twisting: a solid circular rod vs a hollow tube. The polar moment of area J: solid: J = πr⁴/2. Hollow (same area, larger radius): much larger J. Angle of twist θ = TL/(GJ). Hollow shafts are far stiffer in torsion per unit weight.

**Definition.** **Bending** of a beam: the curvature is proportional to the bending moment and inversely proportional to EI — the **flexural rigidity**. E is Young's modulus; I is the **second moment of area** (units: m⁴). I = ∫ y² dA — material far from the neutral axis contributes much more (y² weighting). Maximum deflection for common cases: cantilever with end load = FL³/(3EI); simply supported with centre load = FL³/(48EI). **Torsion** of a shaft: twist angle θ = TL/(GJ), where T is torque, G is shear modulus, J is the polar second moment of area. Hollow shafts maximise J for a given weight — almost all the torsional stiffness comes from the outer region.

**In action.** Every steel-framed building, bridge, and crane uses I-beams. The cross-section is optimised so precisely that modern beams use about 30% less steel than their 1900 equivalents for the same load. Aircraft wings are hollow — the skin (far from the neutral axis) carries the bending loads; the interior is largely empty. Bicycle frames use oversized thin-walled tubes for the same reason — stiffness scales with diameter cubed. A human long bone (femur) is essentially a hollow cylinder — cortical bone on the outside, marrow within. Evolution discovered the hollow shaft long before engineers did. The Eiffel Tower's iron lattice uses triangulated beams — the diagonal braces carry shear, the verticals carry compression, and the overall shape approximates the bending moment diagram of a vertical cantilever loaded by wind.

---

### The spring: Hooke's law in practice
*BB 589 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Stretch a spring, and it pulls back. Hooke's law says the restoring force is proportional to the extension: F = kx. Double the stretch, double the force. Every elastic object — a rubber band, a steel beam, a diving board — is a spring at small deformations. The spring constant k tells you how stiff it is. Stiffer spring = larger k.

**Concrete.** A spring stretches 5.0 cm when a 2.0 kg mass is hung from it. F = mg = 2.0 × 9.8 = 19.6 N. k = F/x = 19.6 / 0.050 = 392 N/m. If you stretch it 10 cm, it pulls back with 39.2 N. The energy stored: U = ½kx² = ½ × 392 × (0.05)² = 0.49 J. Stretch it twice as far, and the energy quadruples: ½ × 392 × (0.10)² = 1.96 J — exactly 4×.

**Springs in series:** Two identical springs (each k = 392 N/m) end to end. Equivalent k = (1/k₁ + 1/k₂)⁻¹ = k/2 = 196 N/m — half as stiff. The same force stretches twice as far. **Springs in parallel:** side by side. Equivalent k = k₁ + k₂ = 784 N/m — twice as stiff. Each spring carries half the load. Resistors in parallel add their conductances; springs in parallel add their stiffnesses.

**Definition.** **Hooke's law:** F = kx, where k is the spring constant (N/m) and x is the extension from the equilibrium position. The restoring force is **opposite** to the displacement: F⃗ = −kx⃗. **Elastic potential energy:** U = ½kx². **Springs in series:** 1/k_eq = 1/k₁ + 1/k₂ + ... (like capacitors in series, resistors in parallel). **Springs in parallel:** k_eq = k₁ + k₂ + ... (like capacitors in parallel, resistors in series). Any elastic structure under small deformation behaves as a spring — k = EA/L for a uniform rod in tension, where E is Young's modulus, A is area, L is length. Hooke's law is the small-deformation limit; beyond the elastic limit, it fails.

**In action.** Springs are everywhere. Your car's suspension uses coil springs (typically k ≈ 20,000–50,000 N/m), with shock absorbers (dampers) to dissipate energy and prevent bouncing. A ballpoint pen's click mechanism uses a tiny spring. A mechanical watch's balance spring (hairspring) oscillates at a precise frequency, keeping time. Truck weigh stations use massive springs — the compression under the truck's weight measures the load. In earthquake engineering, base isolation systems use enormous springs and dampers to decouple a building from ground motion. The simplicity of F = kx hides its universality: from the bonds between atoms (which behave as tiny springs) to the suspension cables of a bridge, the same linear relationship governs.

---

### The applied mechanics toolkit
*BB 590 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** After 15 BBs of applied mechanics, here is the unified picture. Every machine — from a crowbar to a hydraulic press to a bridge — trades force for distance, or pressure for volume, or stress for strain. The governing principles are few: mechanical advantage, Pascal's principle, Archimedes' principle, Bernoulli's principle, Hooke's law, and the material moduli. Master these, and you can analyse any structure or machine you encounter.

**Concrete.** Test yourself — identify the principles at work in each:

1. A car jack: inclined plane (screw thread) + lever (handle). MA = 2πr/pitch × lever ratio ≈ 100–200. Lifts 1 tonne with 5–10 kg of hand force.

2. An oil tanker floating: Archimedes' principle. Displaces 500,000 tonnes of seawater. Steel hull is hollow — average density < water.

3. A dam: hydrostatic pressure P = ρgh. Force on dam face = ½ρgwh² (w = width). Centre of pressure at 2h/3 from surface.

4. A bicycle frame: Young's modulus (E for aluminium ≈ 70 GPa), bending stiffness (EI), designed for minimum weight at required stiffness.

5. A syringe: Boyle's law (PV = constant) + Pascal's principle (pressure transmits undiminished). Pull plunger → volume increases → pressure drops → fluid drawn in.

**Definition.** **Applied mechanics master principles:**

• **Mechanical advantage:** MA = F_out / F_in = d_in / d_out (ideal). Real: η = MA_actual / MA_ideal.

• **Pascal:** P = F/A, transmitted equally. Hydraulic MA = A₂/A₁.

• **Archimedes:** F_b = ρ_fluid × V_displaced × g. Float if ρ_object < ρ_fluid.

• **Bernoulli:** P + ½ρv² + ρgh = constant. Fast flow → low pressure.

• **Hooke:** F = kx, U = ½kx². Stress = E × strain.

• **Materials:** E (stiffness), UTS (strength), safety factor = UTS/working_stress.

• **Beams:** δ ∝ FL³/(EI). I = ∫ y² dA — put mass far from neutral axis.

**In action.** This toolkit analyses every mechanical system you'll ever encounter. The Golden Gate Bridge: the cables are in tension (UTS of steel), the towers in compression, the deck is a beam (bending stiffness EI), the foundations resist hydrostatic pressure and buoyancy. A hydraulic excavator: the engine drives a pump (Pascal), the fluid powers rams (hydraulic MA), the boom is a lever (mechanical advantage), the bucket teeth concentrate force on a small area (high pressure = easy digging). The human body: bones are hollow beams (max I for weight), joints are levers (MA < 1 — speed over force), the heart is a pump (Bernoulli + Pascal), the lungs obey Boyle's law. Physics isn't just in textbooks — it's in the crane outside your window and the spring inside your pen.

---

## 7. Oscillations, waves & sound

### What is an oscillation?
*BB 739 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** An oscillation is something moving back and forth, repeating. A playground swing, a guitar string after being plucked, a pendulum in a clock, your chest as you breathe — all oscillate. An oscillation has a regular, repeating pattern. The time for one complete back-and-forth is the period (T). The number of complete swings per second is the frequency (f). f = 1/T. If a swing takes 2 seconds for one swing, its frequency is 0.5 Hz (hertz — cycles per second).

**Concrete.** A pendulum: a 1 m string with a weight on the end swings with a period of about 2.0 seconds (T = 2π√(L/g) = 2π√(1/9.8) ≈ 2.01 s). Frequency = 0.5 Hz. A shorter 0.25 m pendulum: T ≈ 1.0 s, f = 1 Hz. A spring with mass: T = 2π√(m/k). A car's suspension (m ≈ 300 kg per corner, k ≈ 20,000 N/m) has T ≈ 2π√(300/20000) = 0.77 s — about 1.3 Hz. This is the bounce frequency over bumps. Your heartbeat: about 1.2 Hz (72 bpm). A hummingbird's wings: 50–80 Hz. The fastest oscillation you'll encounter: the clock in your phone's processor at about 3,000,000,000 Hz (3 GHz).

**Definition.** **Oscillation:** repetitive variation around a central value. **Period T:** time for one complete cycle (seconds). **Frequency f = 1/T:** cycles per second (hertz, Hz). **Amplitude A:** maximum displacement from equilibrium. **Simple Harmonic Motion (SHM):** a special oscillation where the restoring force is proportional to displacement (F = −kx). In SHM: T = 2π√(m/k) for mass-spring, T = 2π√(L/g) for pendulum. The motion is sinusoidal: x(t) = A sin(2πft).

---

### What is a wave?
*BB 740 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** A wave is a disturbance that travels through a medium (or through space), carrying energy without carrying matter. Drop a pebble in a pond: the ripples spread outward, but the water molecules don't travel with them — they just bob up and down as the wave passes. A Mexican wave in a stadium: the people stand up and sit down in place, but the "wave" travels around the stadium. Energy moves; matter doesn't.

**Concrete.** Two kinds: **Transverse waves:** the vibration is perpendicular to the direction of travel. Water ripples (the surface moves up and down, the wave moves sideways), light, radio waves, and plucked guitar strings. **Longitudinal waves:** the vibration is parallel to the direction of travel. Sound waves in air — air molecules vibrate back and forth in the same direction the sound travels. A slinky spring demonstrates both: stretch it out, wiggle one end side-to-side (transverse), or push-and-pull (longitudinal). Earthquakes produce both types: P-waves (primary, longitudinal, faster) and S-waves (secondary, transverse, slower).

**Definition.** **Wave:** a propagating disturbance that transfers energy through a medium or vacuum. **Mechanical waves** require a medium (sound, water, seismic, string). **Electromagnetic waves** do not (light, radio, X-rays) — they can travel through a vacuum. **Key quantities:** wavelength λ (distance between crests), frequency f (cycles per second), amplitude A (height of disturbance), speed v (how fast the disturbance moves — v = fλ). The wave speed depends on the medium's properties, not on the frequency or amplitude. Particles of the medium oscillate around fixed positions — they don't travel with the wave.

---

### Frequency and wavelength: how to describe a wave
*BB 741 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** Every wave has a frequency (how often the peaks pass a point) and a wavelength (the distance from one peak to the next). They're linked by the wave speed: v = fλ. If the frequency doubles and the speed stays the same, the wavelength halves. Frequency is measured in hertz (Hz). Wavelength is in metres. Sound and light are both waves, but their frequencies and wavelengths are vastly different.

**Concrete.** Middle C on a piano: sound frequency = 261.6 Hz. Speed of sound in air ≈ 343 m/s. Wavelength λ = v/f = 343/261.6 = 1.31 m — about the length of a trumpet. A dog whistle: 30,000 Hz (ultrasonic). λ = 343/30000 = 0.011 m = 1.1 cm. Visible light: green light has f ≈ 5.5 × 10¹⁴ Hz and λ ≈ 550 nanometres (0.00000055 m). Speed of light c = 3 × 10⁸ m/s. Check: v = fλ = 5.5 × 10¹⁴ × 5.5 × 10⁻⁷ = 3.025 × 10⁸ ≈ c. Radio waves: BBC Radio 4 on 198 kHz. λ = 3 × 10⁸/198,000 = 1515 m — the wavelength is longer than a kilometre!

**Definition.** **Frequency f:** number of complete cycles per second. Units: hertz (Hz) = s⁻¹. **Wavelength λ:** distance between two consecutive identical points (crest to crest, trough to trough). Units: metres. **Wave speed v = fλ:** the fundamental wave equation. For a given medium, v is constant — f and λ are inversely proportional. **Human hearing:** 20 Hz to 20,000 Hz. **Visible light:** 4.3×10¹⁴ Hz (red, λ = 700 nm) to 7.5×10¹⁴ Hz (violet, λ = 400 nm).

---

### Sound waves: vibrating air
*BB 742 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** Sound is a longitudinal wave in air. When you speak, your vocal cords vibrate, pushing and pulling on the air molecules. The molecules bunch together (compression) and spread apart (rarefaction) in a pattern that travels outward at about 343 metres per second. Sound can't travel in a vacuum — there are no molecules to vibrate. That's why space is silent. Sound travels faster in water (~1500 m/s) and even faster in steel (~5000 m/s) because the molecules are closer together.

**Concrete.** Thunder and lightning: light reaches you almost instantly (300,000,000 m/s). Sound follows at 343 m/s. Count the seconds between flash and boom, multiply by 343 — that's how far away the lightning is. 3 seconds ≈ 1 km. A jet at Mach 2: twice the speed of sound — 686 m/s = 2470 km/h. The sonic boom is a shock wave from the aircraft overtaking its own sound waves. Echolocation: bats emit ultrasonic clicks (up to 100 kHz) and time the echo. In water, sound travels ~1500 m/s, so an echo taking 0.1 seconds means the object is 1500 × 0.1/2 = 75 m away. Sonar works the same way.

**Definition.** **Sound** is a longitudinal mechanical wave. Speed in air: v ≈ 331 + 0.6T (m/s), where T is temperature in °C. At 20°C: 343 m/s. **Pitch = frequency:** high frequency = high pitch. **Loudness = amplitude:** large amplitude = loud. Sound intensity is measured in decibels (dB) — a logarithmic scale. 0 dB = threshold of hearing. 60 dB = normal conversation. 120 dB = threshold of pain. **Infrasound:** 20 kHz (medical imaging, 1–15 MHz). **Speed depends on the medium:** faster in denser, stiffer materials.

---

### How waves reflect and refract
*BB 743 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** When a wave hits a boundary, two things can happen. It can bounce back — reflection (like a mirror, or an echo). Or it can pass through but change direction — refraction (like a straw appearing bent in a glass of water). Reflection happens at any boundary. Refraction happens when the wave speed changes — e.g., light going from air to water, or sound going from warm air to cold air.

**Concrete.** **Reflection:** sound echoes off a cliff. If you shout and the echo returns after 2 seconds, the cliff is 343 × 2/2 = 343 m away. Light reflects off water — you see the sky mirrored on a lake. Radar works by reflecting radio waves off aircraft. **Refraction:** a pool looks shallower than it is because light bends as it enters the water (and speeds up as it leaves). A prism splits white light into colours because different frequencies refract by different amounts. Sound refracts downward on a warm day (sound travels faster in warm air, so waves curve downward), which is why sound carries farther over water on a calm morning — the air above cool water bends sound waves back toward the surface.

**Definition.** **Law of reflection:** angle of incidence = angle of reflection (θ_i = θ_r). Measured from the normal (perpendicular to the surface). **Refraction:** when a wave passes into a medium where its speed is different, it bends toward or away from the normal. Snell's Law: n₁ sin θ₁ = n₂ sin θ₂, where n = c/v is the refractive index. Light slows in water (n = 1.33) and glass (n ≈ 1.5), so it bends toward the normal. **Total internal reflection:** when light in a dense medium hits a boundary at a steep enough angle, it reflects completely — this is how fibre optics work.

---

### Resonance: when you push at the right time
*BB 746 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** Every object has a natural frequency — the rate at which it "likes" to vibrate. Push a swing at its natural frequency, and the amplitude grows with each push. This is resonance. A glass shatters when an opera singer hits its resonant frequency. A bridge collapsed (Tacoma Narrows, 1940) because wind drove it at its resonant frequency. Resonance can be catastrophic — or useful, as in a radio receiver tuned to one station's frequency.

**Concrete.** A child on a swing: natural period ~2 seconds, f = 0.5 Hz. A small push at 0.5 Hz makes the amplitude grow from a few cm to several metres. A wine glass: natural frequency ~500–800 Hz (depending on size). Tap it with a fingernail — it rings at that frequency. An opera singer at the right pitch with enough volume can drive the glass's vibration until it shatters. A microwave oven: the microwaves are at 2.45 GHz — the resonant frequency of water molecules. The water in food absorbs the energy and heats up. A radio: you tune the circuit's resonant frequency to match the station's broadcast frequency — resonance amplifies that one signal while rejecting all others.

**Definition.** **Resonance:** the tendency of a system to oscillate with maximum amplitude at its natural frequency when driven by an external force. **Natural frequency:** determined by the system's physical properties (mass, stiffness, length). **Standing wave:** a pattern that forms when reflected waves interfere with incoming waves at the natural frequency — nodes (zero motion) and antinodes (maximum motion). Standing waves on a string: λ = 2L/n. **Q factor (quality factor):** measures how sharply a system resonates — high Q = very narrow, strong resonance.

---

### Music and harmonics: why instruments sound different
*BB 747 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** A guitar and a flute can play the same note, but they sound different. Why? Because a real vibrating string or air column doesn't just vibrate at one frequency — it vibrates at many frequencies simultaneously: the fundamental (the note you hear) plus a series of higher harmonics (overtones). The mix of harmonics — their relative strengths — is the timbre, the "colour" of the sound. It's what makes a violin sound like a violin.

**Concrete.** A guitar string (length L, tension T, mass per length μ): fundamental frequency f = (1/2L)√(T/μ). The string also vibrates at 2f, 3f, 4f, ... — the 2nd, 3rd, 4th harmonics. Pluck it near the bridge: more high harmonics, brighter sound (like a banjo). Pluck it near the middle: mostly fundamental, softer sound. A clarinet (closed at one end): only the odd harmonics (f, 3f, 5f, ...) are produced — giving it a hollow, woody tone. An organ pipe (open at both ends): all harmonics. Change the length (fingers on a guitar fret, valves on a trumpet, sliding a trombone) and you change the fundamental frequency — and all the harmonics scale with it.

**Definition.** **Fundamental frequency f₀:** the lowest frequency produced by a vibrating system — determines the perceived pitch. **Harmonics:** integer multiples of the fundamental (2f₀, 3f₀, 4f₀, ...). **Timbre:** the characteristic sound quality determined by the relative amplitudes of harmonics. **Standing wave patterns:** string fixed at both ends: λ_n = 2L/n, f_n = nv/2L. Pipe open at both ends: same formula. Pipe open at one end: λ_n = 4L/n (n odd), f_n = nv/4L. **Octave:** doubling of frequency (f and 2f). A440 (A above middle C) = 440 Hz. One octave up: 880 Hz. One down: 220 Hz.

---

### Measuring the speed of sound: a classic experiment
*BB 748 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** You can measure the speed of sound with a stopwatch and a cliff. Stand a known distance from a wall, clap loudly, and time how long the echo takes. Speed = 2 × distance / time. It's direct, it's simple, and it works. A more precise version uses two microphones a known distance apart, or a tuning fork over a tube of water to find the resonance positions. Galileo tried this with cannons and lanterns; modern students do it with a phone and a pair of headphones.

**Concrete.** Stand 170 m from a large wall. Clap. Echo returns after 1.0 second. Total distance travelled = 340 m. Speed = 340 m / 1.0 s = 340 m/s. Closer to 343 m/s at 20°C, but this is close. **Resonance tube method:** a tuning fork (known frequency, e.g., 512 Hz) is held over a tube of water. Lower the water level until the tube resonates (loud sound). The air column length L = λ/4. Measure L (e.g., 16.8 cm = 0.168 m), then λ = 4L = 0.672 m. v = fλ = 512 × 0.672 = 344 m/s. Next resonance at L = 3λ/4 = 0.504 m. The difference gives λ/2 = 0.336 m, λ = 0.672 m — independent of the end correction.

**Definition.** **Two main methods:** (1) Echo timing: v = 2d/t — needs a large distance for accuracy. (2) Resonance tube: v = fλ, λ found from standing wave spacings. For an open-closed tube, resonance at L = λ/4, 3λ/4, 5λ/4, ... The spacing between successive resonances = λ/2. **Source of error:** end correction (the antinode is slightly outside the tube — ~0.6× tube radius). Using the difference between successive resonances eliminates this error. **Temperature dependence:** v = 331 + 0.6T — so a 10°C change shifts the speed by 6 m/s, about 1.7%.

---

### Water waves: ripples, tsunamis, and surf
*BB 751 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** Water waves are the most visible waves, but they're also more complex than sound or light. In deep water, water particles move in circles — and the wave speed depends on wavelength (longer waves travel faster). In shallow water, the particles move in flattened ellipses, and the speed depends only on depth. A tsunami in the deep ocean travels at jet-plane speeds (~800 km/h) but with a wavelength of hundreds of kilometres — the amplitude is small, so ships don't notice it. When it reaches shallow water, the wave slows, piles up, and rises into a wall of water.

**Concrete.** Deep-water waves: ocean swell with λ = 100 m. Speed v = √(gλ/2π) = √(9.8 × 100 / 6.28) = 12.5 m/s (45 km/h). A surfer's wave travelling at 45 km/h — you need a jet ski to tow onto waves that fast. **Tsunami:** λ ≈ 200 km. In 4000 m deep water, v = √(g × 4000) = 198 m/s = 713 km/h. At the shore, depth drops to 10 m — v drops to √(9.8 × 10) = 9.9 m/s. The energy piles into a smaller volume — the wave height grows from <1 m in the open ocean to 10–30 m at the coast. A ripple in a puddle: λ ≈ 1 cm, v = √(gλ/2π) = 0.12 m/s — you can out-walk it.

**Definition.** **Deep-water waves:** depth > λ/2. Speed v = √(gλ/2π) — longer waves travel faster (dispersive). **Shallow-water waves:** depth < λ/20. Speed v = √(gh) — depends only on depth (non-dispersive). **Intermediate depth:** a more complex formula involving tanh(kh). **Tsunami:** a shallow-water wave even in the deep ocean (λ ≈ 200 km, depth ≈ 4 km, so depth ≪ λ). Generated by undersea earthquakes, landslides, or volcanic eruptions. **Capillary waves (ripples):** very short λ — surface tension dominates over gravity.

---

### The wave equation and wave speed
*BB 752 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** The speed of a wave on a string depends on two things: how tight the string is (tension T) and how heavy it is (linear density μ — mass per metre). The formula is v = √(T/μ). A tighter string → faster waves. A heavier string → slower waves. This is why guitar strings are tuned by adjusting tension (tuning pegs) and why bass strings are thicker/heavier than treble strings.

**Concrete.** A guitar low E string: T ≈ 80 N, μ ≈ 0.005 kg/m. v = √(80/0.005) = √16000 = 126.5 m/s. Length = 0.65 m. Fundamental frequency: f = v/2L = 126.5/1.3 = 97.3 Hz (close to E2 = 82.4 Hz — the real tension is a bit lower). The high E string: μ ≈ 0.0004 kg/m, T ≈ 70 N. v = √(70/0.0004) = √175000 = 418 m/s. f = 418/1.3 = 322 Hz (close to E4 = 329.6 Hz). The speed on the thin string is more than 3× the speed on the thick string — that's how you get the two-octave range across six strings. A skipping rope: if T ≈ 50 N, μ ≈ 0.02 kg/m, v = √(50/0.02) = 50 m/s — you can easily make standing waves.

**Definition.** **Wave speed on a string:** v = √(T/μ), where T = tension (N) and μ = mass per unit length (kg/m). Derived from the wave equation: ∂²y/∂t² = (T/μ) ∂²y/∂x². The general solution is y(x,t) = f(x − vt) + g(x + vt) — any shape travelling at speed v. For a given string, v is constant — frequency is set by length: f_n = nv/2L = (n/2L)√(T/μ). This is how all string instruments work: adjust T (tuning), L (fingering), or μ (thicker strings for lower notes).

---

### The waves toolkit
*BB 753 · physics · waves*

**Idea.** Waves are disturbances that carry energy without carrying matter. They can be transverse (vibration ⟂ to travel) or longitudinal (vibration ∥ to travel). The key numbers are frequency (f, in Hz), wavelength (λ, in m), and speed (v = fλ). Waves reflect, refract, diffract, and interfere. Resonance amplifies vibrations at the right frequency. Sound, light, water, and earthquakes are all wave phenomena — the same basic rules apply to all of them.

**Concrete.** Key numbers to carry:

• Speed of sound in air: 343 m/s (at 20°C). In water: 1500 m/s. In steel: 5000 m/s.

• Speed of light: 3 × 10⁸ m/s (all EM waves).

• Middle C: 261.6 Hz, λ ≈ 1.31 m (sound).

• Green light: f ≈ 5.5 × 10¹⁴ Hz, λ ≈ 550 nm.

• Human hearing: 20 Hz–20 kHz.

• Visible light: λ = 400–700 nm.

• Pendulum (1 m): T ≈ 2 s, f ≈ 0.5 Hz.

• Guitar string speed: ~100–400 m/s.

• Tsunami in deep water: ~200 m/s (700 km/h).

• Doppler shift: Δf/f ≈ v/c.

**Definition.** **Waves toolkit:**

• **Oscillation:** repetitive motion. T = 1/f. SHM: F = −kx.

• **Wave:** propagating disturbance. v = fλ.

• **Sound:** longitudinal mechanical wave. v ≈ 331 + 0.6T.

• **EM spectrum:** radio→microwave→IR→visible→UV→X-ray→gamma.

• **Reflection:** θ_i = θ_r. **Refraction:** Snell's Law.

• **Diffraction:** waves bend around obstacles. Significant when gap ≈ λ.

• **Interference:** constructive (in phase) / destructive (out of phase).

• **Resonance:** driving at natural frequency → large amplitude.

• **Standing waves:** nodes and antinodes. λ_n = 2L/n (both ends fixed).

• **String speed:** v = √(T/μ). **Water (shallow):** v = √(gh).

• **Doppler:** frequency shift due to relative motion.

---

### Back-and-forth: what simple harmonic motion is
*BB 591 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Push a child on a swing. She goes forward, slows, stops, comes back, slows, stops — over and over. That's oscillation. Simple harmonic motion is the purest kind: the restoring force pulling her back toward the centre is exactly proportional to how far she is from it. Twice as far from centre = twice the force pulling her back. The result is a motion that traces a perfect sine wave in time.

**Concrete.** A 0.50 kg mass hangs on a spring (k = 8.0 N/m). Pull it down 0.10 m and release. The restoring force at maximum stretch: F = kx = 8.0 × 0.10 = 0.80 N — about the weight of 80 grams. The period (time for one complete back-and-forth): T = 2π√(m/k) = 2π√(0.50/8.0) = 2π × 0.25 = 1.57 s. Frequency f = 1/T = 0.637 Hz. The motion is exactly the same whether the amplitude is 10 cm or 1 cm — period depends only on mass and spring stiffness, not on amplitude. This is why a guitar string plays the same note whether you pluck it softly or hard.

**Definition.** **Simple harmonic motion (SHM)** occurs when the restoring force is proportional to displacement and opposite in direction: F = −kx. The equation of motion: a = −(k/m)x = −ω²x, where ω = 2πf = √(k/m). The solution (with calculus): x(t) = A cos(ωt + φ). **Period:** T = 1/f = 2π/ω = 2π√(m/k) for a spring-mass system. **Key properties:** T is independent of amplitude (isochronism); the motion is sinusoidal; energy alternates between kinetic (maximum at centre) and potential (maximum at extremes); total energy E = ½kA² is constant. SHM is the projection of uniform circular motion onto a diameter — a point moving in a circle, viewed from the side, executes SHM.

**In action.** SHM is the fundamental rhythm of the physical world. Atoms in a crystal vibrate around their lattice positions with SHM — the frequency determines the speed of sound in the material. A quartz crystal in a watch oscillates at 32,768 Hz (exactly 2¹⁵) — divide by two fifteen times and you get the 1 Hz tick. The suspension of a car is a damped spring-mass system — the spring provides SHM, the shock absorber removes energy to prevent endless bouncing. Seismometers use SHM: a heavy mass on a weak spring stays nearly stationary while the ground shakes around it, recording the earthquake. Even the gravitational dance of binary stars is approximately SHM when the orbits are nearly circular — the same equation, cosmic scale.

---

### The simple pendulum: timing the swing
*BB 592 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Tie a weight to a string and let it swing. The time it takes to go back and forth — the period — depends only on the string's length and the strength of gravity. It doesn't depend on the weight of the bob, and (for small swings) it barely depends on the amplitude. A 1-metre pendulum takes about 2 seconds to complete a full swing, no matter what you hang from it.

**Concrete.** A pendulum of length L = 1.00 m. Period T = 2π√(L/g) = 2π√(1.00/9.8) = 2π × 0.319 = 2.01 s. Half-period (one swing from left to right) = 1.00 s — which is why grandfather clocks use roughly metre-long pendulums. On the Moon (g = 1.62 m/s²): T = 2π√(1.00/1.62) = 4.95 s — the clock runs much slower. Mass of the bob: irrelevant — a 10 g washer and a 10 kg cannonball on the same 1 m string both take 2.01 s. The restoring force (mg sin θ) increases with mass, but so does inertia (m), and they cancel.

**Definition.** A **simple pendulum** consists of a point mass m suspended by a massless, inextensible string of length L. For small angles (θ < ~15°), the motion is approximately SHM with period **T = 2π√(L/g)**. Derivation: restoring force = mg sin θ ≈ mgθ for small θ; torque = −mgLθ; angular acceleration α = −(g/L)θ → ω = √(g/L) → T = 2π/ω. **Isochronism:** T is independent of amplitude (Galileo's discovery, allegedly from watching a swinging chandelier in Pisa Cathedral). For large amplitudes, T increases: T = 2π√(L/g) × (1 + θ₀²/16 + ...) where θ₀ is the maximum angle in radians. At 30°, the correction is about 1.7%.

**In action.** The pendulum was the world's most accurate timekeeper for nearly 300 years. Christiaan Huygens built the first pendulum clock in 1656, achieving accuracy of about 15 seconds per day — a revolution in navigation (longitude requires precise time). Foucault's pendulum (1851) demonstrated Earth's rotation: a 67-metre pendulum in the Panthéon in Paris visibly rotated its plane of swing by 11° per hour. The pendulum also measures g: geophysicists use precise pendulum timing to map variations in Earth's gravity, revealing underground ore deposits and oil fields. A child on a swing is a pendulum — pumping her legs at the resonant frequency (matching the natural period) amplifies the motion through resonance.

---

### The compound pendulum: any swinging object
*BB 593 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** A real pendulum isn't a point mass on a massless string — it's a cricket bat, a door, a swinging leg. Any rigid object that swings about a pivot is a compound (physical) pendulum. Its period depends on its moment of inertia and the distance from the pivot to its centre of mass. Measure the period, and you can calculate the moment of inertia — a technique engineers use to characterise irregular objects.

**Concrete.** A uniform metre stick (mass M = 0.150 kg, length L = 1.00 m) is pivoted at one end and swings as a pendulum. For a rod pivoted at one end: moment of inertia I = ML²/3 = 0.150 × 1²/3 = 0.050 kg·m². Distance from pivot to centre of mass: d = L/2 = 0.50 m. Period: T = 2π√(I/(Mgd)) = 2π√(0.050/(0.150 × 9.8 × 0.50)) = 2π√(0.050/0.735) = 2π × 0.261 = 1.64 s. Compare with a simple pendulum of length L_eq = I/(Md) = 0.050/(0.150 × 0.50) = 0.667 m — this is the **equivalent length**. A simple pendulum of 0.667 m has the same period.

**Definition.** A **compound pendulum** (physical pendulum) is any rigid body swinging about a fixed horizontal axis. Period: **T = 2π√(I/(Mgd))**, where I is the moment of inertia about the pivot, M is the mass, g is gravity, and d is the distance from pivot to centre of mass. The **equivalent length** L_eq = I/(Md) is the length of a simple pendulum with the same period. Every compound pendulum has a **centre of oscillation** — the point at distance L_eq from the pivot along the line through the COM. If you pivot the pendulum at the centre of oscillation, the period is the same (the pivot and centre of oscillation are interchangeable). This principle was used by Captain Henry Kater in 1817 to measure g to high precision — the Kater reversible pendulum.

**In action.** Compound pendulums measure moments of inertia without calculation. Suspend an irregularly shaped object (a connecting rod, a propeller blade) from a pivot, time the swing, and T = 2π√(I/(Mgd)) gives I directly. This technique was used in early aircraft design to verify the calculated moments of inertia of wings and control surfaces. A cricket bat swung as a pendulum reveals its "sweet spot" — the centre of percussion, where impact produces no reaction at the pivot (your hands). A door is a compound pendulum — the hinges are the pivot, and the period determines how fast it swings shut. A metronome is an adjustable compound pendulum: sliding the weight changes d and thus the tempo.

---

### Transverse and longitudinal waves
*BB 594 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Flick a rope and a bump travels along it. The rope moves up and down; the wave moves sideways. That's a transverse wave — the medium moves perpendicular to the wave's direction. Clap your hands and a pulse of compressed air travels outward — the air molecules move back and forth along the wave's direction. That's longitudinal. Every wave is one of these two types, or a combination.

**Concrete.** On a stretched rope of length 5 m, tension 50 N, linear density 0.02 kg/m: wave speed v = √(T/μ) = √(50/0.02) = √2500 = 50 m/s. A pulse travels the 5 m in 0.1 s. The individual particles of rope move vertically (transverse) — they do not travel with the wave. If you tie a ribbon to the rope, it bobs up and down but stays in place.

In air at 20°C, the speed of sound is 343 m/s. This is a longitudinal wave: air molecules oscillate back and forth along the direction of travel, creating alternating regions of compression (high pressure) and rarefaction (low pressure). In 0.001 seconds, a sound pulse travels 34.3 cm. The molecules themselves move only about 0.0001 mm — the disturbance travels, not the air.

**Definition.** **Waves** transfer energy through a medium without transferring matter. In **transverse waves**, particle displacement is perpendicular to wave direction (e.g., waves on a string, water surface waves, electromagnetic waves). In **longitudinal waves**, particle displacement is parallel to wave direction (e.g., sound in fluids, P-waves in earthquakes). **Surface waves** (water waves) are a combination: particles move in circular orbits. Wave speed v = distance/time = λf = wavelength × frequency. The speed depends on the medium's elastic and inertial properties, not on the frequency (for most media — dispersion occurs when this fails). **Key insight:** the wave is a pattern moving through the medium; the medium itself oscillates locally.

**In action.** The distinction between transverse and longitudinal determines what waves can travel where. S-waves (shear, transverse) from earthquakes cannot travel through Earth's liquid outer core — only P-waves (compression, longitudinal) can. This is how we know the outer core is liquid. Ultrasound imaging uses longitudinal waves in tissue. Sonar uses longitudinal waves in water. Fibre optics use transverse electromagnetic waves in glass. In a tsunami, the wave is transverse at the surface but involves the entire water column — at 4000 m depth, the wave speed is √(gh) ≈ 200 m/s, about the speed of a jetliner. That's why tsunamis cross oceans in hours. The wave that destroys the coast started as a gentle swell in deep water, barely noticeable to a ship.

---

### Reflection, refraction, and diffraction of waves
*BB 595 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Waves don't just travel — they interact with obstacles and boundaries. When a wave hits a wall, it bounces back (reflection). When it crosses into a different medium, it bends (refraction). When it passes through a narrow opening, it spreads out (diffraction). All three behaviours are characteristics of waves — and all three can be explained by the same simple idea: every point on a wavefront acts as a source of new circular ripples.

**Concrete.** **Reflection:** A pulse on a rope hits a fixed end and returns inverted — the wall pulls the rope down as the pulse arrives. Hit a free end (a ring sliding on a pole) and the pulse returns upright. The angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection — true for water waves, sound, and light.

**Refraction:** Water waves travel slower in shallow water. When they cross from deep to shallow at an angle, the part that hits first slows down, bending the wavefront. Snell's law for waves: sin θ₁/v₁ = sin θ₂/v₂. A lens focuses light by delaying the centre more than the edges.

**Diffraction:** Water waves passing through a harbour entrance spread into a semicircular pattern. The narrower the gap, the more they spread. When the gap width ≈ wavelength, the spreading is dramatic. This is why you can hear someone talking in the next room even though you can't see them — sound waves (λ ≈ 0.1–1 m) diffract around doorways; light waves (λ ≈ 0.0005 mm) do not.

**Definition.** **Huygens' principle:** Every point on a wavefront acts as a source of secondary spherical wavelets. The envelope of these wavelets forms the new wavefront. This single principle explains all three behaviours. **Reflection:** secondary wavelets from a flat surface reconstruct a reflected wave at equal angle. **Refraction:** wavelets travel at different speeds in different media, bending the envelope. **Diffraction:** when a wavefront is partially blocked, the wavelets at the edges spread into the shadow region. The amount of spreading ≈ λ/w (wavelength/gap width). Significant diffraction requires the gap to be comparable to or smaller than the wavelength.

**In action.** These three behaviours are the foundation of optics, acoustics, and radio. Concert hall design uses reflection (sound reflects off walls and ceilings) and diffusion (irregular surfaces scatter sound) to create good acoustics. Ultrasound imaging uses reflection — the probe sends pulses and listens for echoes from tissue boundaries. AM radio (λ ≈ 300 m) diffracts around buildings and hills — you can hear it in valleys. FM radio (λ ≈ 3 m) does not — it's line-of-sight. The reason your WiFi signal drops when you walk into another room is diffraction-limited shadowing: the doorway is a gap, and the 2.4 GHz wavelength (12.5 cm) spreads somewhat, but walls absorb. Sonar uses refraction: sound bends downward in deep water because pressure increases speed, creating "shadow zones" where submarines can hide.

---

### Interference: when waves meet
*BB 596 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Two ripples meet on a pond. Where they overlap, they don't collide or bounce — they pass right through each other, and while they overlap, the water surface is the sum of both. This is superposition. If the crests align, they add (constructive interference — bigger wave). If a crest meets a trough, they cancel (destructive interference — flat water). Interference is the signature of wave behaviour.

**Concrete.** Two identical speakers emit a 440 Hz tone (wavelength λ = v/f = 343/440 ≈ 0.78 m). You stand 3.00 m from speaker A and 3.39 m from speaker B. Path difference = 0.39 m = λ/2. The waves arrive out of phase — crest from A meets trough from B. Destructive interference: you hear almost nothing. Move 0.39 m to the side, making the distances equal, and the sound is loud — constructive interference. Walk along a line and you hear alternating loud and quiet zones spaced λ/2 apart.

**Beats:** Two tuning forks at 440 Hz and 442 Hz struck simultaneously. The interference alternates between constructive (in phase) and destructive (out of phase) at 2 Hz — you hear a "wah-wah-wah" twice per second. Beat frequency = |f₁ − f₂|. Musicians use beats to tune instruments — when the beats disappear, the frequencies match.

**Definition.** **Superposition principle:** When two or more waves overlap, the resultant displacement at any point is the algebraic sum of the individual displacements. **Constructive interference** occurs when the path difference is an integer number of wavelengths (ΔL = nλ): crests align. **Destructive interference** occurs when ΔL = (n + ½)λ: crest meets trough. The pattern of alternating maxima and minima is an **interference pattern**. For two sources, the angular positions of maxima satisfy d sin θ = nλ, where d is the source separation. **Beats** occur when two waves of slightly different frequencies interfere: f_beat = |f₁ − f₂|. Beats are interference in time rather than space.

**In action.** Interference is everywhere. Noise-cancelling headphones use a microphone to pick up ambient sound, invert it, and play it through the speaker — the inverted wave destructively interferes with the original, producing silence. Concert hall designers avoid parallel walls to prevent standing-wave interference patterns (dead spots and hot spots). The colours of an oil slick on water are thin-film interference — light reflecting from the top and bottom of the oil film constructively interferes for some wavelengths, destructively for others. Radio telescopes use interferometry — combining signals from dishes hundreds of kilometres apart — to achieve the resolution of a single dish that size. The LIGO gravitational wave detector uses laser interference to measure changes in distance smaller than a proton's diameter, caused by black holes colliding billions of light-years away.

---

### Stationary waves: the reason strings play notes
*BB 597 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Pluck a guitar string. It doesn't just vibrate randomly — it settles into a pattern of stationary waves. The ends are fixed (can't move), so the wave bounces back and forth, and the forward and reflected waves interfere to create a pattern that appears to stand still. Points that never move (nodes) alternate with points of maximum motion (antinodes). The pitch depends on the string length, tension, and density.

**Concrete.** A guitar string: length L = 0.65 m, tension T = 70 N, linear density μ = 0.005 kg/m. Wave speed v = √(T/μ) = √(70/0.005) = √14,000 ≈ 118.3 m/s. Fundamental frequency (first harmonic): f₁ = v/(2L) = 118.3/1.30 = 91.0 Hz (roughly F#₂, the low E string's neighbour). The string has nodes at both ends and an antinode in the middle. Second harmonic: f₂ = 2f₁ = 182 Hz — a node in the middle, two antinodes. Third harmonic: f₃ = 3f₁ = 273 Hz. These integer multiples — the harmonic series — are present simultaneously when you pluck the string, and their relative strengths determine the timbre (why a guitar sounds different from a piano playing the same note).

**Definition.** **Stationary (standing) waves** form when a travelling wave and its reflection interfere. For a string fixed at both ends: the boundary conditions (displacement = 0 at x = 0 and x = L) allow only wavelengths λ_n = 2L/n, where n = 1, 2, 3, ... The corresponding frequencies are f_n = n v/(2L) = n f₁. **Nodes** (zero displacement) occur at x = 0, L/n, 2L/n, ..., L. **Antinodes** (maximum displacement) occur midway between nodes. The **fundamental** (n = 1) is the lowest frequency; **overtones** (n ≥ 2) are integer multiples. For a pipe open at both ends: same pattern (antinodes at ends, pressure nodes). For a pipe closed at one end: only odd harmonics (n = 1, 3, 5, ...), λ_n = 4L/n.

**In action.** Stationary waves are the physics behind every musical instrument. A violin string produces the fundamental plus a rich spectrum of overtones. A flute (open at both ends) produces all harmonics. A clarinet (approximately closed at one end) produces only odd harmonics, giving it its characteristic hollow timbre. Microwave ovens create standing electromagnetic waves — the turntable moves food through the antinodes where the electric field is strongest, preventing cold spots. In quantum mechanics, an electron in an atom is a standing wave around the nucleus — only certain wavelengths (and thus energies) fit, which is why atomic energy levels are quantised. The Schrödinger equation for a particle in a box has the same mathematical form as the standing wave on a string.

---

### The speed of sound: why thunder lags behind lightning
*BB 598 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Light travels almost instantly. Sound plods along at about 340 metres per second — a leisurely stroll by physics standards. Count the seconds between the flash and the thunder, divide by three, and you have the distance in kilometres. Sound needs a medium — it can't travel through vacuum — and its speed depends on what it's travelling through and how warm that medium is.

**Concrete.** Sound in air at 0°C: v = 331 m/s. At 20°C: v ≈ 331 + 0.6 × 20 = 343 m/s. At 40°C (desert): v ≈ 355 m/s. The temperature dependence is roughly v = 331 + 0.6T (T in °C). The formula v = √(γRT/M) gives the exact value: for air (γ = 1.4, R = 8.314, T = 293 K, M = 0.029 kg/mol), v = √(1.4 × 8.314 × 293 / 0.029) = √(3409/0.029) = √117,551 ≈ 343 m/s.

Sound in water at 20°C: v ≈ 1480 m/s (about 4.3× faster than air — water is denser but also far less compressible; the bulk modulus dominates). Sound in steel: v ≈ 5000–6000 m/s (about 15× faster than air). Sound in helium: v ≈ 1000 m/s (low molecular mass → high speed — which is why your voice sounds squeaky after inhaling helium; your vocal cords produce the same frequencies, but the resonant frequencies of your vocal tract shift up).

**Definition.** The **speed of sound** in a fluid (gas or liquid) is v = √(B/ρ), where B is the bulk modulus and ρ is density. For an ideal gas, this becomes v = √(γRT/M), where γ = C_p/C_v (≈ 1.4 for diatomic gases), R is the gas constant, T is absolute temperature, and M is molar mass. In solids, v_longitudinal = √((B + 4G/3)/ρ) and v_transverse = √(G/ρ). Key facts: sound speed increases with temperature (for gases), is independent of pressure at constant temperature, and is generally 3–15× faster in liquids and solids than in gases. The temperature dependence accounts for about 0.6 m/s per °C in air.

**In action.** The speed of sound limits how fast information can travel through matter. In earthquakes, P-waves (~6 km/s in crust) arrive before S-waves (~3.5 km/s), and both arrive before the destructive surface waves. The time gap tells you the distance to the epicentre. Sonar uses sound speed in water (~1500 m/s) to measure depth — a ping returning in 2 seconds means the seafloor is 1500 m down. Ultrasound imaging measures tissue density by timing echoes — the machine assumes v ≈ 1540 m/s in soft tissue. In music, the speed of sound causes a noticeable delay in large venues — sound from the stage takes about 0.1 s to reach the back of a 34 m hall, which is why orchestras need a conductor to stay synchronised.

---

### The Doppler effect: why sirens change pitch
*BB 599 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** An ambulance races toward you — its siren sounds high-pitched. The moment it passes, the pitch drops. The siren isn't changing; the distance between wavefronts is. As the source moves toward you, each successive wave crest is emitted closer to the previous one — the wavelength is compressed, the frequency is higher. Moving away, the waves are stretched, and the pitch drops.

**Concrete.** An ambulance siren emits f₀ = 800 Hz. Ambulance speed v_s = 30 m/s (108 km/h) toward you. Speed of sound c = 343 m/s. For a moving source approaching a stationary observer: f' = f₀ × c/(c − v_s) = 800 × 343/(343 − 30) = 800 × 343/313 ≈ 877 Hz — almost a semitone sharp. As it recedes: f' = 800 × 343/(343 + 30) = 800 × 343/373 ≈ 736 Hz — about a tone and a half flat. The total shift is roughly 140 Hz.

If you were moving at 30 m/s toward a stationary siren: f' = f₀ × (c + v_o)/c = 800 × 373/343 ≈ 870 Hz. Moving away: 800 × 313/343 ≈ 730 Hz. The formulas differ slightly — for sound, it matters whether the source or the observer is moving, because the medium (air) provides an absolute reference frame.

**Definition.** The **Doppler effect** is the change in observed frequency when the source and observer are in relative motion. For sound: **source moving toward observer:** f' = f₀ × c/(c − v_s). **Source moving away:** f' = f₀ × c/(c + v_s). **Observer moving toward source:** f' = f₀ × (c + v_o)/c. **Observer moving away:** f' = f₀ × (c − v_o)/c. For **light** (relativistic): f' = f₀ √((c±v)/(c∓v)) — only the relative velocity matters; there is no medium. Redshift (moving away, f ↓) and blueshift (approaching, f ↑) are used in astronomy. The effect applies to all waves: sound, light, water waves.

**In action.** Doppler radar measures the speed of a car by bouncing a radio wave off it and measuring the frequency shift — the speed gun a police officer uses. Weather radar uses Doppler shift to measure wind speed inside storms — the rotation signature of a tornado appears as adjacent regions of strong approach and recession. In medicine, Doppler ultrasound measures blood flow velocity — narrowing of arteries produces higher velocities. Astronomers measure the redshift of distant galaxies to calculate their recession speed — Edwin Hubble used this in 1929 to discover the expansion of the universe. Bats and dolphins use Doppler shifts in their echolocation clicks to determine not just distance but relative speed of prey.

---

### Pitch, loudness, and timbre: the three qualities of sound
*BB 600 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Three things distinguish one sound from another: how high or low it is (pitch = frequency), how loud it is (loudness = amplitude, measured in decibels), and what makes a piano sound different from a violin playing the same note (timbre = the mix of overtones). Every sound you hear is a combination of these three physical properties.

**Concrete.** A piano plays middle C (262 Hz). A violin plays the same note. Both are at 60 dB. They sound completely different because of their harmonic content. The piano's 262 Hz tone contains overtones at 524 Hz (2nd harmonic, strong), 786 Hz (3rd, weaker), 1048 Hz (4th, moderate), etc. The violin has a different recipe: strong 2nd, very weak 3rd, prominent 5th and 7th. The fundamental is the same; the spectrum of overtones differs. A tuning fork produces almost a pure sine wave — only the fundamental, almost no overtones — which is why it sounds dull and electronic.

Loudness: 0 dB is the threshold of hearing (10⁻¹² W/m²). Normal conversation ≈ 60 dB. A rock concert ≈ 110 dB (pain threshold). Jet engine at 30 m ≈ 140 dB. Every 10 dB increase sounds roughly twice as loud. The decibel scale is logarithmic: dB = 10 log₁₀(I/I₀). A 3 dB increase doubles the intensity (power per area) but is just barely noticeable.

**Definition.** **Pitch** is the subjective perception of frequency. The human ear can hear approximately 20 Hz to 20,000 Hz (20 kHz), with peak sensitivity around 3–4 kHz. **Loudness** is the subjective perception of intensity. The decibel scale: sound intensity level = 10 log₁₀(I/I₀) dB, where I₀ = 10⁻¹² W/m². Each 10 dB is a factor of 10 in intensity. **Timbre** (tone colour) is determined by the harmonic spectrum — the relative amplitudes of the fundamental and its integer-multiple overtones. A pure tone has no overtones; most musical instruments have rich harmonic spectra. The **attack** and **decay** (how quickly the sound starts and stops) also contribute to timbre.

**In action.** Timbre is why you can identify a friend's voice on the phone — the telephone transmits only 300–3400 Hz, but the relative harmonic pattern within that band is enough for recognition. MP3 compression works by discarding harmonics that the ear is less sensitive to (psychoacoustic masking). A sound engineer EQing a mix is adjusting the harmonic balance. Sonar operators are trained to distinguish whale species by the timbre of their calls. The Stradivarius violin's legendary sound is partly its harmonic spectrum — strong high overtones give it "brilliance." And in speech, vowels are distinguished by the frequencies of formants (resonant peaks in the vocal tract), which are essentially a timbre fingerprint.

---

### Kundt's tube: making sound visible
*BB 601 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Sound waves are invisible — you can hear them but not see them. In 1866, August Kundt devised a way to make standing sound waves visible. A glass tube, a pinch of fine powder, and a loud tone: the powder dances and settles into little piles at the nodes, where the air is still. The distance between the piles is half a wavelength. Measure it, multiply by the frequency, and you have the speed of sound — no electronics required.

**Concrete.** A Kundt's tube is a horizontal glass tube about 1 m long, closed at one end with a movable piston. A loudspeaker (or a rod stroked with a resin-coated cloth) generates a pure tone at the open end. Fine cork dust or lycopodium powder is sprinkled along the bottom. When the piston is adjusted to create a standing wave (resonance), the powder gathers in small heaps at the displacement nodes — the points where the air is not moving. The distance between adjacent heaps = λ/2. With f = 1000 Hz and λ/2 = 0.171 m, λ = 0.342 m, so c = fλ = 1000 × 0.342 = 342 m/s — the speed of sound at room temperature.

**Definition.** **Kundt's tube** is a device for measuring the speed of sound in a gas (or a solid, via a rod extending into the tube). A standing longitudinal wave is established in the tube. Fine powder collects at the displacement nodes (pressure antinodes), making the waveform visible. The distance between adjacent nodes = λ/2. With a known frequency f, v = fλ. The tube can also measure the speed of sound in different gases by filling it with that gas. For a solid rod: the rod is stroked longitudinally to produce a standing wave, and the powder pattern in the gas inside the tube reveals the wavelength in the gas, from which the speed in the solid can be calculated using the frequency match.

**In action.** Kundt's tube was the standard laboratory method for measuring the speed of sound for over a century, replaced only by electronic timing in the late 20th century. It's still used in teaching labs because it makes an invisible phenomenon visible. The principle — standing waves in a column — is the same as in an organ pipe. In industrial settings, ultrasonic standing waves are used to levitate small objects and to agglomerate particles in smokestacks (acoustic agglomeration). The pattern of nodes and antinodes also explains why some positions in a room have "dead spots" for certain bass frequencies — the room dimensions create standing waves, and at the nodes, the sound cancels.

---

### Organ pipes and wind instruments
*BB 602 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** An organ pipe is a standing-wave machine. Blow air across the mouth and it sets the air column vibrating at its resonant frequencies. An open pipe (both ends open) produces all harmonics — 1, 2, 3, 4... A closed pipe (one end stopped) produces only odd harmonics — 1, 3, 5... This is why a clarinet (closed pipe) sounds hollow and woody while a flute (open pipe) sounds bright and clear.

**Concrete.** An open organ pipe of length L = 0.78 m. Fundamental: f₁ = v/(2L) = 343/(2 × 0.78) = 343/1.56 ≈ 220 Hz (A₃, the A below middle C). Harmonics: f₂ = 440 Hz (A₄), f₃ = 660 Hz (E₅), f₄ = 880 Hz (A₅). All harmonics present.

A stopped (closed) pipe of the same length: f₁ = v/(4L) = 343/(4 × 0.78) = 343/3.12 ≈ 110 Hz (A₂, an octave lower). But only odd harmonics exist: f₃ = 330 Hz (E₄), f₅ = 550 Hz (C♯₅), no f₂, no f₄. A stopped pipe sounds an octave lower than an open pipe of the same length and has a different timbre.

The flute (open at both ends) produces all harmonics. The clarinet (roughly closed at the reed end) produces odd harmonics. The saxophone (conical bore) behaves like an open pipe despite being closed at the reed — the cone shape tricks the physics into producing all harmonics.

**Definition.** For a cylindrical pipe: **open at both ends:** displacement antinodes at both ends, f_n = n v/(2L), n = 1, 2, 3, ... All harmonics. **Closed at one end:** displacement node at closed end, antinode at open end, f_n = n v/(4L), n = 1, 3, 5, ... Only odd harmonics. The **fundamental** wavelength for an open pipe is λ = 2L; for a closed pipe, λ = 4L. **End correction:** the effective length is slightly longer than the physical length — the antinode sits about 0.6 × radius beyond the open end. For a flue pipe (organ), the air jet oscillates at the lip, driving the standing wave. For a reed pipe, the reed vibration drives the column.

**In action.** Every wind instrument is an application of standing waves. A flute changes pitch by opening and closing holes, effectively changing L. A trombone extends a slide to lengthen the pipe. A trumpet uses valves to route air through additional tubing. The human voice uses the vocal tract as a pipe — vowels are shaped by tongue position changing the resonant frequencies (formants). A didgeridoo is a naturally closed pipe (lips vibrate at one end, open at the other), producing a drone with rich odd harmonics. Cathedral organs with 32-foot pipes (about 10 m) produce frequencies around 16 Hz — near the lower limit of human hearing, felt as much as heard.

---

### The Doppler effect at the speed of a bullet: shock waves
*BB 603 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** When an object moves faster than sound, the wavefronts pile up and merge into a single intense shock wave — a sonic boom. The object outruns its own sound. The shock wave forms a cone behind the object; the angle of the cone depends on the speed. This is the extreme limit of the Doppler effect — the frequency shift goes to infinity, and the mathematics of smooth compression gives way to a discontinuity.

**Concrete.** A fighter jet flies at Mach 2 (twice the speed of sound, about 686 m/s at altitude where c ≈ 343 m/s). The sound waves it produces cannot propagate ahead of it — they pile up along a conical shock front. The half-angle of the Mach cone: sin θ = c/v = 1/M = 1/2 → θ = 30°. A person on the ground hears nothing until the shock wave passes — then a sudden sharp crack (the sonic boom) as the entire accumulated wavefront hits in an instant. The boom is not just one event; it's a continuous pressure pulse that travels along the ground.

For a bullet at 900 m/s: M ≈ 2.6, θ = arcsin(1/2.6) ≈ 22.6°. The crack you hear is a miniature sonic boom. The "crack" of a whip is also a sonic boom — the tip of the whip briefly exceeds the speed of sound.

**Definition.** When a source moves at speed v > c (the speed of sound in that medium), it creates a **shock wave**. The wavefronts cannot propagate upstream and merge into a conical envelope: the **Mach cone**. The half-angle is given by **sin θ = c/v = 1/M**, where M = v/c is the Mach number. The shock is a thin region (a few mean free paths thick) where pressure, density, and temperature change almost discontinuously. **Sonic boom** is the audible manifestation — a double bang (bow shock and tail shock) from an aircraft, or a single sharp crack from a bullet. For M < 1, the Doppler shift is finite and the waves remain smooth. At M = 1 (transonic), the waves pile up at the source — the "sound barrier."

**In action.** Shock waves are everywhere in high-speed physics. The crack of lightning (thunder) is a shock wave from the rapid thermal expansion of air heated to ~30,000 K by the lightning channel. The space shuttle re-entering the atmosphere at Mach 25 created a shock wave that ionised the air — the resulting plasma blackout blocked radio communication for several minutes. In medicine, lithotripsy uses focused shock waves to break up kidney stones without surgery. In astrophysics, supernova remnants expand as shock waves into the interstellar medium, heating gas to millions of degrees. The solar wind forms a bow shock where it meets Earth's magnetosphere — a standing shock wave in space, protecting us from the full force of the solar particle stream.

---

### Sound energy: how much power is in a whisper
*BB 604 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Sound carries energy. A whisper is about a billionth of a watt. A symphony orchestra at full blast puts out about 10 watts of acoustic power — the same as a nightlight. The ear is extraordinarily sensitive: it can detect vibrations smaller than the diameter of a hydrogen atom. The energy spreads over the surface of a sphere, so intensity falls as 1/r².

**Concrete.** A point source emits 1.0 W of acoustic power uniformly in all directions. At distance r = 10 m, the power is spread over a sphere of area 4πr² = 4π × 100 ≈ 1257 m². Intensity I = P/A = 1/1257 ≈ 8.0 × 10⁻⁴ W/m². Sound level = 10 log₁₀(8.0×10⁻⁴/10⁻¹²) = 10 log₁₀(8.0×10⁸) = 10 × 8.9 ≈ 89 dB. At r = 100 m: I falls by 100× to 8.0 × 10⁻⁶ W/m² → 69 dB. Doubling the distance reduces intensity to 1/4 and drops the level by 6 dB.

Comparison of acoustic powers: whisper ≈ 10⁻⁹ W, normal speech ≈ 10⁻⁵ W, shouting ≈ 10⁻³ W, piano ≈ 0.1 W, full orchestra ≈ 1–10 W, rock band ≈ 10–100 W, jet engine (acoustic output) ≈ 1000 W. Even a jet engine converts only about 1% of its mechanical power into sound — most goes into thrust.

**Definition.** **Sound intensity** I = P/A is the power per unit area (W/m²). For a point source in free space: I = P/(4πr²) — the inverse-square law. **Sound intensity level** L_I = 10 log₁₀(I/I₀) dB, where I₀ = 10⁻¹² W/m². The **total acoustic power** P = ∫ I dA over a closed surface. The ear's sensitivity spans 12 orders of magnitude in intensity — from 10⁻¹² W/m² (hearing threshold) to 1 W/m² (pain). The **energy density** in a sound wave u = I/c (J/m³) — for normal speech at 60 dB, u ≈ 3 × 10⁻¹¹ J/m³, astonishingly small.

**In action.** The inverse-square law explains why a concert sounds different in the front row versus the back. Outdoor venues use line arrays — vertical stacks of speakers that produce cylindrical rather than spherical wavefronts, so intensity falls as 1/r instead of 1/r², reaching farther. Sonar systems must account for spherical spreading plus absorption in seawater (which increases with frequency — limiting long-range sonar to low frequencies). In biology, the faintest sounds a bat can detect at 20 m from its prey have intensities around 10⁻¹² W/m² — exactly at the threshold of hearing. The bat compensates by emitting intense pulses (~100 W/m² at 10 cm from the mouth) and using the returning echo, which has travelled a round trip and fallen off as 1/r⁴.

---

### Reverberation: why cathedrals echo
*BB 605 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** Clap your hands in an empty room. You hear the direct sound, then a rapid series of reflections from the walls, floor, and ceiling, each slightly quieter than the last. This is reverberation. In a cathedral, it lasts for seconds; in a recording studio, it's suppressed to milliseconds. The reverberation time — how long it takes sound to decay by 60 dB — determines whether a room feels "live" or "dead."

**Concrete.** A concert hall has dimensions 30 m × 20 m × 15 m (volume V = 9000 m³). Total surface area S ≈ 3000 m². The average absorption coefficient ᾱ (fraction of sound energy absorbed per reflection) is about 0.20 for a well-designed hall at mid-frequencies. Sabine's formula: T₆₀ = 0.161 V/(Sᾱ) = 0.161 × 9000/(3000 × 0.20) = 1449/600 ≈ 2.4 seconds. This is ideal for symphonic music — rich, full sound. For speech, the ideal is about 1.0–1.5 seconds. A recording studio (heavy absorption, ᾱ ≈ 0.5) might have T₆₀ ≈ 0.3 s. A cathedral (stone walls, ᾱ ≈ 0.05, huge volume) can have T₆₀ > 10 seconds — you hear the last syllable of a word long after it was spoken.

**Definition.** **Reverberation time** RT₆₀ is the time required for sound to decay by 60 dB after the source stops. **Sabine's formula:** T₆₀ = 0.161 V/(Σ S_i α_i), where V is volume (m³), S_i is the area of each surface (m²), and α_i is its absorption coefficient (0 = perfect reflection, 1 = perfect absorption). The formula assumes a diffuse sound field (sound energy uniform and isotropic). **Absorption coefficients** vary with frequency: heavy curtains absorb treble well; bass traps (porous panels, Helmholtz resonators) are needed for low frequencies. An **echo** is a distinct reflection arriving more than ~50 ms after the direct sound; reverberation is a continuous decay of innumerable overlapping reflections.

**In action.** Reverberation design separates good halls from bad ones. The Sydney Opera House's original acoustics were criticised; subsequent renovations adjusted absorption to achieve a T₆₀ of about 2.0 s. Boston Symphony Hall (opened 1900) is considered one of the finest in the world — its "shoebox" shape and plaster surfaces give a T₆₀ of about 1.9 s. Recording studios use diffusers (irregular surfaces that scatter sound) to avoid flutter echoes while maintaining some liveness. The anechoic chamber at Orfield Labs in Minnesota has a T₆₀ of about 0.1 s — so quiet you can hear your own heartbeat and the blood moving in your veins. Standing in it for more than a few minutes is disorienting; the absence of reverberation feels unnatural.

---

### The oscillations and acoustics toolkit
*BB 606 · physics · mechanics*

**Idea.** After 15 BBs of oscillations and acoustics, here is the unified picture. Everything that vibrates — a spring, a pendulum, a guitar string, a column of air, the surface of the Sun — obeys the same few principles: restoring force proportional to displacement (SHM), wave speed determined by medium properties, and the universal behaviours of reflection, refraction, diffraction, and interference. Sound is just the particular case where the medium is air and the receiver is a human ear.

**Concrete.** Test yourself — identify the principle at work:

1. A pendulum clock: T = 2π√(L/g). Length sets the tempo. Isochronism makes it reliable.

2. A tuning fork: SHM. Pure tone — almost no overtones. Used for calibration.

3. A guitar string: standing waves. f_n = n v/(2L). Harmonics = integer multiples of fundamental.

4. An organ pipe: standing waves in air. Open = all harmonics. Closed = odd only.

5. Thunder lag: speed of sound. Distance (km) = seconds/3. Temperature-dependent.

6. A passing siren: Doppler effect. f' = f₀ c/(c ± v). Pitch shift reveals speed.

7. A concert hall: reverberation. T₆₀ = 0.161 V/(Sᾱ). Absorption controls decay time.

8. Noise-cancelling headphones: destructive interference. Inverted wave + original = silence.

**Definition.** **Oscillations and acoustics master principles:**

• **SHM:** F = −kx, T = 2π√(m/k) (spring), T = 2π√(L/g) (simple pendulum), T = 2π√(I/Mgd) (compound pendulum).

• **Waves:** v = fλ. Transverse (rope, EM) vs longitudinal (sound). v_string = √(T/μ), v_sound = √(γRT/M).

• **Wave behaviours:** reflection (θ_i = θ_r), refraction (Snell: sin θ/v = constant), diffraction (spreading ≈ λ/w), interference (constructive: ΔL = nλ; destructive: ΔL = (n+½)λ).

• **Standing waves:** string fixed at both ends: λ_n = 2L/n. Pipe open: f_n = n v/(2L). Pipe closed: f_n = n v/(4L), n odd.

• **Doppler:** f' = f₀ c/(c ± v_s) (source moving), f' = f₀ (c ± v_o)/c (observer moving).

• **Sound energy:** I = P/(4πr²). dB = 10 log₁₀(I/10⁻¹²). T₆₀ = 0.161 V/(Sᾱ).

**In action.** This toolkit explains every sound you've ever heard. A symphony orchestra: the strings are standing waves on stretched strings, the brass and woodwinds are standing waves in air columns, the timpani are vibrating membranes. The conductor's baton sets the tempo — a human pendulum. The hall's shape and materials determine whether the sound wraps around you warmly or dies in a muddy echo. A bat navigating in darkness emits ultrasonic pulses (f ≈ 50 kHz, λ ≈ 7 mm) and hears the echoes — using Doppler shift to track insect speed, time delay for distance, and binaural differences for direction. The principles are the same whether the wave is on a string, in air, or in the plasma of the Sun's corona. Oscillation is nature's default mode of energy storage and release.

---

## 8. Heat & thermodynamics

### Temperature: how hot is it?
*BB 754 · physics · heat*

**Idea.** Temperature tells you how hot or cold something is — specifically, it measures the average kinetic energy of the particles inside. The faster the atoms and molecules are jiggling, the higher the temperature. A thermometer works by letting its liquid (mercury or alcohol) expand or contract until it matches the temperature of whatever it's touching. This is thermal equilibrium: when two things are at the same temperature, no net heat flows between them.

**Concrete.** Water freezes at 0°C (32°F) and boils at 100°C (212°F) — at sea level. Human body temperature: about 37°C. Room temperature: about 20°C. A fridge: about 4°C. A freezer: about −18°C. Liquid nitrogen: −196°C. The surface of the Sun: about 5500°C. The coldest possible temperature is −273.15°C — absolute zero — where all particle motion stops.
